Reason: `scada_status` is allocated by the caller without a size field, so growing it overruns callers built against the old size (D-40). A process that wants totals adds up the counts of its queues.
Consequences: no change to code or to any structure. A later wish for process-wide counts needs a new function, not a larger `scada_status`.

## D-64: The two open measurements of contract 16: the ring gap at the reference load, and huge pages
Date: 2026-11-16
Phase / issue: 1, #21 (P1-13)
Status: accepted for the method, decided by the committee of the lead session on 2026-11-16 (plan of #21). The figures follow from the workflow `measurements` after this change is on main, and settle the two rows of contract section 16 in the pull request of contract 1.0.

Context: two rows of contract section 16 were still open after P1-12 (D-57): the gap half of the default change ring capacity (no gap for a consumer that keeps up with the reference load) and huge pages for the `tags` regions (used only if they lower the 99.9th percentile of a read). Both can be measured on the pipeline runners.
Decision:
- Ring gap: `scada-tag-ring-gap-perf-test` (label `perf`). scada-sim writes the reference load (50,000 tags, 10 owner threads, 50,000 updates per second, notify each tick, the default heartbeat timeout of 3,000 ms) with the default ring capacity, 65,536 entries per owner. This process subscribes to all 10 rings before the simulator writes, and one thread per ring takes every change. Any gap, whatever its reason, fails the test, and every change written must arrive. It runs for 10 s in the pipeline's perf gate and for 600 s in the workflow `measurements`.
- Huge pages: `scada-tag-huge-pages-root-test` (label `root`, since it mounts a file system). A huge page is 2 MB, and at the reference load an owner's `tags` region is 320 KB (5,000 records of 64 bytes), so huge pages cannot be used there at all. The test therefore uses one owner of 50,000 tags (3.2 MB), written at 50,000 updates per second, and measures 1,000,000 reads and 20,000 `read_many` of 500 while it is written. It does this with the database on a tmpfs mounted `huge=never` and on one mounted `huge=always`, in a mount namespace of its own (the method of conformance test 11). In the second, it checks that the region this process reads is mapped with huge pages (`ShmemPmdMapped` or `FilePmdMapped` above 0), so a run that did not use them cannot count. It reports the figures and gates on nothing: the decision is taken from the figures of both architectures. If huge pages give a lower 99.9th percentile on both, the lead is told before anything changes.
- Both run in the workflow `measurements` (steps "Ring gap at the reference load" and "Huge pages (as root)"); the job's time limit goes from 75 to 100 minutes.
Reason: a test of the reference load itself is the criterion the contract names; a gap at smaller loads (tests 13 and 14) says nothing about the capacity. Huge pages need a region of at least 2 MB; measuring them on a layout where they cannot apply would show nothing.
Consequences: the root preset runs the huge-page test in the dev build too (a few seconds, figures only). Locally, on a 4-processor container: the ring-gap test received 500,000 changes with 0 gaps in 10 s; the huge-page test mapped 2,048 kB of the 3.2 MB region with huge pages, and two runs gave read 99.9th percentiles of 497 and 459 ns on huge=never and 478 and 452 ns on huge=always.

## D-65: `scada_tag_write_string` checks ownership before the text
Date: 2026-11-16
Phase / issue: 1, #112
Status: accepted. The lead session ruled on 2026-11-16 that the code follows D-29.

Context: D-29 says "`SCADA_E_NOT_OWNER` before the value is looked at". `scada_tag_write` follows it. `scada_tag_write_string` returned `SCADA_E_INVALID` for text longer than `SCADA_STRING_MAX` (250 bytes) before it checked ownership, so a process that does not own the tag got `SCADA_E_INVALID` for a long text and `SCADA_E_NOT_OWNER` for a short one. This was found while writing contract 0.25 (#21).
Options: change the code to follow D-29; or write the order the code had into the contract.
Decision: the code follows D-29. `scada_tag_write_string` checks in this order:
1. `SCADA_E_INVALID` for a NULL context, or a NULL `text` with `len` above 0. These are argument errors: no tag is looked at.
2. `SCADA_E_INVALID` for a handle that is none of this generation.
3. `SCADA_E_NOT_OWNER`.
4. `SCADA_E_TYPE` for a tag that is not a STRING.
5. `SCADA_E_INVALID` for a length above 250 bytes, text that is not well-formed UTF-8, or a quality or flag that may not be stored.

No byte of the text is read before the length check.
Reason: one rule for every write function. A process that is not the owner learns nothing about the value checks, and the answer does not depend on the text it passed.
Consequences:
- `libs/scada-tag/src/c_api.cpp` drops its own length check. The check in `Attachment::write_string` is now the only one, and it already ran after ownership.
- Test: `CApi.WriteStringOfAnotherOwnersTagIsNotOwnerWhateverTheText`. It fails without the change.
- Contract section 13 states the order for `scada_tag_write_string` (version 0.26).

## D-66: Contract 1.0: the last open points of section 16
Date: 2026-11-16
Phase / issue: 1, #21 (P1-13)
Status: accepted. The rulings a to e are by the committee of the lead session on 2026-11-16; the deferral of commands after lost audit records is approved by the product owner. The rulings f to h are by the product owner, on the committee's recommendation, on 2026-11-22.

Context: contract 1.0 (P1-13) needs every point of contract section 16 settled. After D-57 and D-63 five were left: the gap half of the default change ring capacity, huge pages for the `tags` regions, a second look at the read retry limit, retiring the generation on a detected suspend (left open by D-46), and commands after lost audit records. The figures come from the workflow `measurements`, run 43905525505 (commit 5bbfa71, perf build, `seconds=600`), on shared GitHub-hosted virtual machines with 4 vCPUs (x86-64: AMD EPYC 9V74; ARM64: Neoverse-N2), with the tests of D-64. `docs/reports/phase-1.md` has every figure.
Options, suspend: report a detected suspend only (A, as D-46); retire the generation on a detected suspend, so that pending requests are answered ABORTED (B). Options, lost audit records: specify a latch for 1.0; defer it to the command service.
Decision:
- a. Default change ring capacity per owner: 65,536, both halves settled. Size (P1-12, D-57): 28.8 MB for the reference load, below 64 MB. Gap (P1-13): `scada-tag-ring-gap-perf-test` received 30,000,000 changes with 0 gaps at the reference load for 600 s on each runner (x86-64, ARM64 `isb`, ARM64 `yield`; run 43905525505). Its 10 s form runs in the pipeline's perf gate. The default is settled for the reference load of 10 owners; the capacity is per owner in the tag set, and phase 2 sizes each owner's ring (committee review of PR #115).
- b. Huge pages for the `tags` regions: not used, a judgement on single runs of about 0.6 s, one per mode and runner (committee review of PR #115). `scada-tag-huge-pages-root-test`, run 43905525505. No gain on x86-64: the read medians were equal at 80 ns, and the read 99.9th percentile was 261 ns without and 270 ns with huge pages. On ARM64 the read median was up to about 40 ns lower (`isb` 120 to 104 ns, `yield` 144 to 104 ns; the 99.9th percentile 312 to 272 ns and 416 to 384 ns), within the noise of single 0.6 s runs, and not needed for any target. D-64 asked for a lower 99.9th percentile on both architectures. An owner region at the reference load (320 KB) is smaller than one 2 MB page. To be measured again on the target board (#41) if a target there needs it.
- c. Read retry limit before `SCADA_E_BUSY`: stays at 64. Conformance test 26, 600 s, ARM64 `isb` (shipped): run 43875571560 (D-57), 0 in 1.20 billion reads; run 43905525505, 424 in 1,200,220,000, 0.35 per million. The rate varies between runs on shared virtual machines. Both runs are below the criterion of 1 per million; the margin in the second run was about 3 times. Run 43905525505 also gave x86-64 1 in 1,200,240,000 (0.0008 per million) and ARM64 `yield` 169 (0.14 per million; not shipped). The test bound was 1,309 in every job, and every job passed. The target board (#41) is to measure it again. The figure is of real-time readers on processors they share with non-real-time owners. The limit of 64 is a constant of the library, not of the layout: a worse figure on the board changes core placement (#116), not the layout (committee review of PR #115).
- d. Retiring the generation on a detected suspend: option A for 1.0. `scada-tagd` reports a detected suspend only, as before (contract 3.4 forbids suspend; D-46), and starts no generation change. Option B is phase 5, issue #109.
- e. Commands after lost audit records: deferred by the product owner; it does not block 1.0. Whether commands stay refused after `scada-tagd` reported lost audit records, until an operator acknowledges, is safety-relevant, and the library has no such latch. It is for the command service in phase 5 (issues #74, #75 and #83). The row leaves the table of contract section 16 for a list "Deferred by the owner, does not block 1.0" below it.
- Every other row of section 16 was already settled and stays as it is.
- Issue #41, the target board, does not block contract 1.0 (the product owner's decision). It stays open as the hardware acceptance before the first release on the board.
- 1.0 is cut on top of every phase 1 contract change: 0.24 (issue #96, PR #111, D-62) is merged; the 1.0 change-log row and migration note follow 0.26.
Reason: a and c meet their criteria on both architectures with the code that ships. For b, huge pages cannot apply to a region of the reference load, and the one architecture where they lowered the figures needs no gain for a target. For d, option A keeps one clock for every age and timeout (D-46), and option B is restart behaviour that belongs with the command service. For e, the latch is a behaviour of the command service, which is built in phase 5 (reference 18.2).
Consequences:
- Contract section 16: every point settled; heading and intro say so; the lost-audit item moves to the deferred list.
- Contract 1.0: layout version 1 frozen; a later change is of one of the three levels of ruling f. Section 17 says that reference 1.1 section 5 matches the contract and lists what remains: reference 1.1 also corrects the lines of 3.1, 4.2, 6.1, 14.4 and 17.4 that refer to section 5, and 10.4 stays a gap for phase 7 (#75).
- Reference document 1.1: section 5 updated to match the contract, and the lines of 3.1, 4.2, 6.1, 14.4 and 17.4 that refer to it corrected, approved by the product owner; no other section changed.
- No change to code or to any structure.

Rulings of 2026-11-22, by the product owner, on the committee's recommendation:
- f. Versioning after 1.0 (B1). A later change is of one of three levels, and every level gets a row in the contract change log.
  1. Layout version (now 1; `libscada-tag` refuses to attach on a mismatch). It goes up when a process built to the old contract and one built to the new could not safely share a generation: a structure's size, a field's offset or meaning; the first use of a reserved field or bit that readers refuse when it is not 0; a new enumeration value or flag stored in shared memory; a limit that readers validate; file names, owners and modes; the protocol steps and orderings of contract sections 6, 8 and 9; the liveness rules; the clock of `*_mono_ns`. Reason: the library is static (`cmake/ScadaTargets.cmake`, `scada_add_library`, `add_library(... STATIC ...)`), each service carries its own copy, and the layout version is the only guard against a partial upgrade.
  2. Contract minor version (1.1, 1.2, ...) with a migration note: additions to the library interface (a function, an appended return code, a counter); behaviour local to one process; the order of checks; defaults and tunable constants; a new conformance test; deployment requirements; the use of reserved bytes that every reader ignores.
  3. Editorial revision (1.0.1, ...): wording and formatting with no change of meaning; its change-log row says so, with "Migration: none".
  The retain file keeps its own format version (now 2). Contract section 14.1, new, "What 1.0 freezes": the reserved fields and unknown values that readers ignore or refuse, each with its place in the code, and the constants and defaults that layout 1 does not freeze. The 60 s request lifetime is judged at post and when taken, so it is a limit that readers validate and is layout. Contract section 16: a worse figure on the target board (#41) changes a default, a constant or core placement by a minor version, not the layout.
- g. Journalling of AUTO and REMOTE writes (B3). The contract's rule holds: every write request, whatever its source, needs its event record accepted first (contract 9.3, reference 5.3). Reference 17.4 said that REMOTE writes, like local AUTO ones, "are not journalled one by one"; it now says they need their event record, and keeps "every change into or out of `REMOTE` is audited". Open for phase 5, deferred by the owner and not blocking 1.0: whether "its event record" means one record per request or one per change of state, for AUTO and REMOTE. An output written every 10 ms is 100 records a second into an events queue of the default 1,024 records (`DEFAULT_QUEUE_CAPACITY`, `tag_set.h`). The library does not enforce the pairing: `scada_queue_post` of a request (`Attachment::post_record`, `queue_access.cpp`) checks the record and the consumer, not a prior event record. Neither answer changes the layout. The question goes to #83, the command service's issue for phase 5 (#74 is the audit service, #75 the system alarms).
- h. Freeze choices. Nothing changes. The product owner accepted, knowing that they close at the freeze and that a change to any of them is a layout version: the handle split of 10 and 22 bits, so at most 1,023 owners, where an owner is one writing thread (`HANDLE_OWNER_BITS`, `HANDLE_SLOT_BITS`, `MAX_OWNERS`, `handle.h`); bits 12 to 15 of `quality_flags`, which are unassigned and which readers refuse when set (`QUALITY_FLAGS_MASK` = 0x0FFF of 16 bits and `quality_flags_valid`, `types.h`), so that assigning one is a layout version; no spare bytes in `ChangeEntry` (32 bytes, `layout.h`), `scada_value` (48 bytes) and `scada_status` (32 bytes, `scada_tag.h`). Contract section 14.1 lists them.

## D-67: On a pull request, clang-tidy lints only the translation units the pull request could have affected
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 1, #120
Status: accepted

Context: the job `check` lints every translation unit with clang-tidy on every run. It took 28 to 36 minutes against a limit of 45, and a pull request of documents only waited as long as one that changes the library. Issue #120 asks for a lint of what a pull request could have affected, with two safeguards: dependents taken from the build, and a full lint when the rules change or the set cannot be worked out.
Options:
- Dependencies from Ninja's `.d` files: needs a full build in the job, minutes the job does not have.
- Dependencies from `clang-scan-deps`: a further tool, not on `PATH` under its plain name.
- Dependencies from the compiler of each compile command, run with `-M` (preprocessing only).
- For a deleted or renamed header: the former dependents from the dependencies of the base revision (a second checkout and configure), or a full lint.
Decision:
- `scada_check.py --tidy-base <revision>`, set by the CMake option `SCADA_CHECK_TIDY_BASE` (empty by default: everything). The pipeline sets it to the pull request's base commit on a pull request; a push to `main` and `workflow_dispatch` leave it empty.
- Changed files: `git diff --name-status --find-renames` from the merge base of the revision and `HEAD` to the work tree, plus untracked files.
- Dependencies: each unit's compile command from `compile_commands.json`, with its output options (`-o`, `-c`, `-MD`, `-MF` and the like) replaced by `-M`, run with the build's compiler. 165 units take about 5 s on 4 processors.
- Linted: every changed unit, and every unit whose dependencies contain a changed file.
- Everything is linted when a changed, deleted or renamed path matches `FULL_LINT_PATTERNS` in `tools/check/lint_selection.py` (`.clang-tidy`, `CMakeLists.txt`, `*.cmake`, the presets, `cmake/`, `third-party/`, `.github/workflows/`, `.github/actions/`, `tools/check/`, `*.in`); when a header is deleted or renamed; and when the changes or the dependencies cannot be worked out (shallow clone, no merge base, a unit that does not preprocess, an unknown git status, any exception of the selection).
- `banned` and `rt` run in one clang-query run per unit with both rule sets, instead of one run per set. The rules, the matches and the reports are the same; the parse of each unit is done once.
Reason: `-M` is the compiler's own view of what a unit includes, for the flags the unit is linted with, at a cost of seconds. A full lint for a deleted header is the simplest way to cover its former dependents: a dependent that stopped including it had to change a file it includes and is linted anyway, but `__has_include` and a header of the same name elsewhere on the include path are not visible in the dependencies after the change. The combined clang-query run keeps a pull request of documents only under the 5 minutes the issue asks for: format 2 s, banned 100 s and rt 72 s apart on 4 processors.
Consequences: a header that no unit includes is not linted, as before. If the selection misses a unit, its finding first shows in the full run on `main` after the merge; the self-test `scada-check-selection-test` and the planted-fault proof of #120 guard against that. A new file that sets the rules of the lint must be added to `FULL_LINT_PATTERNS`.

## D-68: A full clang-tidy lint is split across four parallel jobs
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 1, #123
Status: accepted

Context: since D-67 a pull request lints only what it could have affected, but a push to `main`, a manual run and a pull request that changes the rules still lint all translation units. That took 38 min 17 s for 165 units in run 45018629275, against the job's limit of 45 minutes; clang-tidy was about 35 minutes of it, the set-up and configure about 15 s. Issue #123 asks for a split across N jobs that finishes in under 20 minutes, with one required result.
Options:
- N: 2 (about 18 minutes of lint per share, no room for phase 2), 3 (about 12), 4 (about 9), 6 or more (more minutes of set-up for little wall time).
- The split: a round-robin over the sorted list; by file size; by the measured time of each unit (a table to keep up to date).
- The shares: a plan job that works out the selection once and hands each share its list (a dynamic matrix, the shares start after it); or a fixed matrix in which every share works out the same selection itself.
- The build: one configure shared through an artifact (absolute paths, generated headers), or one per share.
Decision:
- N = 4: a fixed matrix `check (tidy K of 4)`, K = 1 to 4, each `cmake --preset dev -DSCADA_CHECK_ONLY=tidy -DSCADA_CHECK_TIDY_SHARE=K/4` and the `check` target, which runs `scada_check.py --only tidy --tidy-share K/4`. Limit 25 minutes per share, more than three times the longest share measured.
- Measured in run 45023188297 (pull request #124, a full lint of 165 units): the linter step of the shares took 6 min 30 s (42 units), 5 min 43 s, 5 min 2 s and 4 min 57 s (41 each); the jobs 7 min 14 s, 6 min 17 s, 5 min 40 s and 5 min 35 s. `check (format, banned, rt)` took 2 min 25 s, `check` 5 s. The run's `check` finished 7 min 24 s after the run started, against 38 min 17 s in run 45018629275.
- Actions minutes of a full lint: before, one job of 38 min 17 s, billed 39 minutes. After, 27 min 16 s of job time in six jobs, billed 31 minutes (each job rounded up to the minute: 3 + 8 + 7 + 6 + 6 + 1). The set-up of a runner costs about 25 s per job.
- Each share configures itself (about 5 s) and works out the selection of D-67 itself. The selection depends only on the commit, the base revision and the compile commands, which are the same in every share.
- The split: the selected units sorted by their path relative to the source directory, and share K takes the units K, K + N, K + 2N and so on (`tools/check/lint_shares.py`). The shares differ by one unit at most. Sorted by path, the units of one library and its tests are next to each other, so the round-robin also spreads the expensive test units of each library over all shares. Measured on 4 processors with the time of each of the 165 units (3,748 s of clang-tidy in all, the longest `libs/scada-tag/tests/queue_test.cpp` at 247 s): the round-robin gives shares of 1,053, 941, 928 and 826 s; the longest-first by file size 1,054 s at most, no better; a split by the measured times themselves 938 s at most. One unit of 247 s bounds any split, so the round-robin stays.
- A selection of fewer than 40 units (`SPLIT_FROM`) is not split: share 1 lints all of it, shares 2 to 4 lint nothing and pass after their set-up. 40 units take about as long as one share of a full lint.
- The formatter, banned-function and `SCADA_RT` checks run once on every file, in the job `check (format, banned, rt)` (`SCADA_CHECK_ONLY="format;banned;rt"`), with the self-tests of the selection and of the split. Limit 20 minutes. `--tidy-share` with any other check is refused.
- Each share writes a record (`--tidy-record`): the selection, its number, the units it linted, and whether it found anything. The job `check`, which needs the two others and runs `if: always()`, fails unless both results are `success`, and runs `lint_shares.py verify` on the four records: a clean record for every share 1 to 4, of the same selection, each with exactly its part. It prints the totals. Limit 10 minutes. Only `check` is to be required on `main`.
- Locally, `cmake --build --preset dev --target check` is unchanged: the three options are empty and it is one run of every check over every unit.
Reason: the fixed matrix needs no plan job before the shares start and no list handed from job to job; the record makes the cost of that visible: if two shares ever worked out different selections, `check` fails. The round-robin is the cheapest balance that needs no data kept up to date. A configure costs seconds, less than handing a build directory from job to job.
Consequences: every run of the pipeline starts four share jobs and the job `check`, also for a pull request of documents only, and each pays the set-up of a runner. A new share is a new entry in the list `share` of the matrix; `strategy.job-total` gives N to the shares, and `check` takes N from the records.


## D-69: The configuration schemas are normative tables in the contract; the JSON Schema files are written in P2-03
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01)
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: the outline in `docs/contracts/README.md` says the contract is "the set of JSON Schema files ... plus this document", and reference 12.3 asks the builder to turn the examples into JSON Schema files. Issue #100 (P2-03) puts "the JSON Schema files of the configuration schemas contract, in the repository" in its own scope, next to the validator that uses them.
Options: write the JSON Schema files in #98 and keep the document for the rest; write the keys as tables in the document and generate or write the files in P2-03.
Decision: the contract states every key of every file type as a table (type, default, range, meaning) and is normative. P2-03 writes the JSON Schema files under `libs/scada-conf/schemas/` from the tables, with a test that the corpus of contract section 13 passes them. Where a table and a file disagree, the table wins and the file is fixed.
Reason: the schema files are only useful with the code that loads them, and a review of tables is a review of the format; writing JSON Schema twice, once without a validator to test it, would double the review. The keys a person reviews are the same either way.
Consequences: the contract is the one place to change a key; a change to a schema file without the table is a defect. The protocol schemas (contract Annex A for Modbus) follow the same rule.
Revision 0.2: every key that takes several shapes is listed in contract Annex C. The JSON Schema files use the 2020-12 dialect (`unevaluatedProperties` is needed to merge framework and protocol keys into one object). The validator is the project's own, over a parsed tree that keeps file, line and column, with a fixed subset of keywords each mapped to a CF-2xx code; a general validator library would report neither and would be a new dependency. This contract owns every configuration key, default and validation code; the driver interface contract cites them (lead's direction 1, D-102).

## D-70: The YAML subset of the configuration tree
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01)
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: the outline proposes "no anchors or custom tags" and leaves the subset to be decided.
Options: full YAML 1.2; YAML 1.2 without anchors and tags; a stricter subset that also refuses `null`, YAML 1.1 booleans, octal numbers and several documents.
Decision: the stricter subset of contract section 1.1: one UTF-8 document without markers or directives; block and flow collections; string keys only, no duplicates, no complex keys; no anchors, aliases, merge keys or tags; no `null` or empty values; `true` and `false` only; decimal and `0x` integers without leading zeros or separators; `.inf` and `.nan` not as numbers. Size limits: 8 MiB a file, depth 32, 64 KiB a scalar, 64 bytes a key, 20,000 files and 256 MiB a tree. JSON files use the same parser.
Reason: anchors and merge keys let one line change values far away and make "file and line" of an error ambiguous, and templates are the reuse mechanism (reference 12.2). Refusing `null`, `yes` and `010` removes the values people type by accident. The limits bound the parser's memory, and the parser is fuzzed (#100).
Consequences: P2-03 needs a parser that reports these constructs rather than silently accepting them, with a line and column for each (dependency choice and licence in #100).
Revision 0.2: scalar typing is defined by the key's schema type, not by a YAML schema (contract 1.2): regular expressions for integers and decimals, `true` and `false` only, the written form of a number kept (`10` is an integer, `10.0` a decimal), map keys typed by the map's schema. Limits against hostile input are completed (1.3): nodes per file and tree, entries per map, `.st` files, expressions, `repeat` ranges checked before iteration, expansion stopped at `limits.max_tags`, at most 1,000 errors reported. The tree holds regular files and directories only, with ASCII paths in the name grammar; links, FIFOs and device nodes are refused (section 1).

## D-71: Templates extend at most one other template
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01)
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: the outline asks whether templates may include other templates. The device survey of 2026-11-22 (not in the repository) shows device families with a shared core, and devices speaking several protocols for which a protocol-free signal base is useful.
Options: no inclusion; single inheritance (`extends`); several included templates.
Decision: `extends`, one parent, at most 4 parents deep, no cycles. A base template (no `protocol`) extends only a base template; a protocol template extends a base template or one of its own protocol. Optional parts of a device are parameters with `when`, not included templates.
Reason: both cases of the survey are single inheritance; several parents need an order between unrelated parents and give a point two origins, which makes errors harder to locate.
Consequences: #103 implements `extends` and the merge order of D-72; nested composition stays out.
Revision 0.2: templates have no subdirectories, so that a reference finds its file without a search (Linux review L-22). A protocol template removes signals of its base with its own `disable` (SCADA review S-10).

## D-72: The merge order of template expansion
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01)
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: the outline asks for the exact merge order of overrides; #103 needs it to test each rule.
Options: lists merged by position or by a key; lists replaced whole; deletion of keys by `null` or a marker.
Decision: the eight steps of contract section 4.5: chain from the root; per level, parameters, then `defaults`, `read` and `enums`, then the level's `repeat` expansion, then its points merged by name, then `identify` and `health` replaced whole; the instance's parameters bound before any level expands; `when` then `disable`; template and instance `defaults` under every point; instance `overrides` by point name; prefix; validation. Maps merge key by key; scalars and lists replace whole; nothing is deleted except points, by `when` and `disable`. An override or `disable` of an unknown point is an error.
Reason: replacing lists whole makes a result independent of positions in two files; errors on unknown names catch typing errors that would otherwise leave a point active or an override unused; refusing `null` (D-70) leaves no way to delete a key, which keeps the origin of every value one line.
Consequences: an override of one alarm of a point gives the whole `alarms` list. Every merged value keeps its origin, so that #103 can report an error with the instance and the template line.
Revision 0.2: a step 7, evaluation, evaluates every configuration expression of a point after the overrides, with the parameters and the `repeat` variables bound to that point; `when` is refused in `defaults` and `overrides`. Exclusive groups (`gain` or `divide_by`, `bit` or `field`, `register` or `parts`, the write encodings, the feedback forms): giving one key removes the inherited keys of its group. `replace: true` in a map replaces the inherited map whole. Template-level `disable`; instance `only` (keep these points, for large or site-chosen maps); an instance's `identify` entries are appended to the template's. Reason: the review showed overrides that could not be written (C++ S-25 to S-27, SCADA S-10, S-11).

## D-73: Process names, service users and uids in the tag set from configuration
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01); for #101
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: the tag set holds a numeric uid per process (D-26), and its canonical encoding hashes it; D-26 says the numeric uid "is replaced by a user name when the real configuration arrives in phase 2", and asks phase 2 to refuse uid 0 and two processes with one uid. The configuration tree must not hold uids, which belong to the unit's installation.
Options: put user names in the tag set and change the canonical encoding (a change of tag database contract 1.0 and its encoding version); keep uids in the tag set and resolve them in `scada-conf`.
Decision: the process name is the service name without `scada-`, its user is the service name (one system user per service, `docs/install.md`); `scada-conf` looks the uid up on the unit when it builds the tag set and refuses a missing user, uid 0 and a shared uid (CF-1005). The fixed process list and the REALTIME flags (`control`, `io`) are in contract section 12.2; drivers are not REALTIME in phase 2. The tag set and the encoding of contract 1.0 are unchanged.
Reason: it agrees with contract 1.0 without a change, and the uid is a fact of the unit the data plane must check anyway (tag database contract 3.1).
Consequences: the same tree on two units whose uids differ gives two `config_hash` values; contract section 12.8 says so for operators. `config_hash` is used only by `scada-tagd` on one unit to reuse a generation. Where configurations are compared across units (upstream sync, backup and restore, the system page), the contract defines `config_id`, a SHA-256 over the paths and bytes of the tree's files, independent of the unit; the node link manifest has its own hash, defined by the node link contract (phase 11), over the exported tags. The #101 test that pins a hash gives the lookup a fixed user table. (Lead's review of the plan, 2026-11-22.)
Revision 0.2: only `control` and `io` are REALTIME in phase 2 (lead's direction 2, owner's ruling O-4). The install tree creates every service user, also of later services (#102). `--offline` validation runs without the uid table and gives no `config_hash`. At its start `scada-conf` rebuilds the tag set if a uid changed. Section 12.8 of the contract says what a unit-dependent `config_hash` means for an operator and defines `config_id`, also its file set, its line-end rule and what it does not cover (owner's ruling O-6).

## D-74: `retain_max_age` is applied by the owner's library at attach, and is not in the tag set
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01); for #89
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: #89 (D-59, point 2) adds an optional per-tag `retain_max_age`: a restored value older than the bound, or of unknown age, is restored as BAD with RESTORED and the value kept; refused on mode, authority, counter and shelving tags. Tag database contract 12 restores a value as UNCERTAIN with RESTORED, at attach, before `ready`. The header has no field for the bound, and a first use of a field that readers refuse is a layout version (contract 14.1).
Options: a field in the header (`DirectoryEntry.reserved`, ignored by readers, so a minor version, plus an encoding version of `config_hash`); the owner rewrites the tag after attach from `scada_tag_retain_info`; the owner passes the bounds to its own library at attach, which applies them while it restores.
Decision: the third. The bound is in the configuration only, not in the tag set or `config_hash`. The configuration contract says it depends on tag database 1.1 (#89): #89 adds the attach option and the restore rule to the library and to contract 12 as a minor version with a migration note (D-66 f), reviewed by a person (restart behaviour). This pull request does not change `tag-database.md`; the proposal is a comment on #89. Until 1.1 the key is validated, has no effect, and gives warning CW-510.
Reason: a rewrite after attach lets readers see the old value as UNCERTAIN until the owner gets to it, which is what the bound is for. A header field puts a policy into the data plane and changes the hash encoding for a setting that matters only at a restore.
Consequences: a change of the bound alone needs no generation change. Found for #89, reported to the lead and written on #89: under contract 12 (D-45) a record read BAD without NOT_INIT is removed from the retain file at the next write, so a value restored BAD for an unknown age would be lost even if a later boot had a synchronised clock; #89 should decide whether an unchanged BAD + RESTORED restore counts as "no change", as an unchanged UNCERTAIN restore does (D-59, B1). The draft proposes that it does.

## D-75: Owners and queues, with generated short names for channels and tasks
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01); for #101
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: the tag set needs owners and queues with names of at most 31 bytes (tag database contract 1, 3). Owners are writing threads: one per control task, one per driver channel, one for a driver's main thread (reference 5.1, driver interface draft). The readable form `cmd.<channel>.<task>` fits only if channel and task names are cut to about 12 and 8 bytes. Contract 3 leaves the part of a queue name after its prefix as free text, so that the generator may shorten it.
Options: limit the names users write; numbered names (an index); a short stable hash of each name.
Decision: a short name of 10 lower-case hexadecimal digits, the start of SHA-256 of `channel:<name>` or `task:<name>`, written C() and T(). Owners `control.<T>`, `cmd`, `alarm`, `io`, `drv-<protocol>`, `drv-<protocol>.<C>`. Queues `cmd.<C>.cmd`, `cmd.<C>.<T>` and their `result.` queues, `cmd.io.<S>`, `events.cmd`, `events.control.<T>`, `events.alarm`, and from phase 5 `events.drv-<protocol>`. The longest is 28 bytes. Channel and task names keep the identifier limit of 31 bytes; protocol names are at most 12 bytes. Equal short names of two channels or two tasks are CF-1006. `scada-conf` writes the map of generated names to `/var/lib/scada/conf/tag-set-names.json` at each apply, and `scadactl` shows the configured name beside the generated one. The consumer of the event queues (`audit`) is provisional until phases 4 and 5 (#83).
Reason: a short name depends only on its own name, so adding, removing, reordering or renaming one channel or task renames only its own owners and queues; an index would rename the others. Users keep readable names of comfortable length (lead's review of the plan, 2026-11-22). 40 bits make a collision among 1,000 channels less likely than one in a million, and a collision is an error, never a silent merge.
Consequences: logs of `scada-tagd` show generated names; `scadactl` and the map translate them. A rename of a channel or task changes its owner and queue names and therefore `config_hash`. The temporary tag-set file of phase 1 used other names; it is test input only (#101).
Revision 0.2, replacing the queue scheme above (lead's direction 3; C++ B-3, SCADA S-18, Linux L-14): command queues are per driver process and source, as reference 5.1 names them: `cmd.<protocol>.<S>` and `result.<protocol>.<S>`, S being `cmd` or T(task). A driver's command thread routes requests to its channels' order queues, which answer `BUSY` when full; this keeps the queue count small and within one wait set of 127 sources. Short names are readable: S(kind, name, n) is the name itself when it has at most n bytes, else its first n − 9 bytes, `-`, and 8 hexadecimal digits of SHA-256(kind:name); C = S(channel, 15), T = S(task, 12); protocol names at most 10 bytes. Channel and task names keep the 31-byte limit of an identifier (lead's review of the plan). Every attached process has an owner named like the process for its main thread (`sys.proc` tags), so that no owner has two writing threads; `scada-io` has `io` (main) and `io.scan` (Linux L-4). The name map is kept with every committed version, with its `config_hash` (Linux L-25). Owner's ruling O-8.

## D-76: `scada-conf` states every owner's change ring capacity
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01); for #101
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: the tag-set builder does not raise the default ring capacity of 65,536 entries; an owner with more than 32,768 tags must state its capacity (contract 5). D-66 a: phase 2 sizes each owner's ring.
Options: the user states capacities; `scada-conf` computes them.
Decision: `scada-conf` gives every owner `max(data_plane.change_ring_capacity, the next power of two of at least twice its tags)`, at most 1,048,576 entries, and refuses an owner beyond that (CF-1003).
Reason: the rule of contract 8.3 can be computed exactly from the tree, and a user would only get it wrong.
Consequences: adding tags to a channel past a power of two changes the capacity and therefore the hash; it changes the tag set anyway.
Revision 0.2, replacing the rule above (lead's direction 4; C++ B-3, SCADA B-5): each owner's ring is the next power of two of at least four times its tags, at least 1,024 entries; a channel may override it (`change_ring_capacity`, at least twice its tags). The global key `data_plane.change_ring_capacity` is removed. 300 channel owners at the old default took 600 MiB against "under 64 MB" of reference 5.8; at 100 tags each they now take about 10 MiB.

## D-77: Validation codes and severities
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01)
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: reference 12.5 asks for validation rules; the outline asks for each with an error code and a message; reference 12.4 distinguishes errors and warnings.
Options: free-text messages; codes per rule.
Decision: codes `CF-<n>` for errors (a commit is refused) and `CW-<n>` for warnings, numbered by contract section (1xx files, 2xx schema, 3xx names, 4xx templates, 5xx tags and expressions, 6xx drivers, 7xx outputs, 8xx alarms and logging, 9xx control, 10xx tag set), with a fixed message and placeholders. A code never changes meaning from 1.0 on; a removed rule leaves its code unused. Every report has file, line, column, code and message, and for an expanded value the template's file and line.
Reason: stable codes let tests, the web editor and documentation refer to a rule without matching text.
Consequences: the severity of two rules (pacing, outputs without interlocks) waits for the owner's ruling X-13 of the device survey; the draft makes pacing a warning and a missing `interlocks` key an error.
Revision 0.2: validation runs in seven stages (files, parsing, schema, expansion, cross-file rules, the protocols' `configure` run by `scada-conf`, the tag set), errors sorted by file, line, column and code within a stage; warnings only when no stage failed. Refusals that need a protocol's knowledge carry codes CF-650 to CF-699, listed in the protocol's annex; the driver interface contract's own codes are for runtime diagnostics. New codes include CF-419, CF-516, CF-618 to CF-622, CW-608, CF-716, CF-717, CF-906, CF-1007, CF-1008.

## D-78: The expression grammar
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01)
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: reference 7.6 says virtual tags use the structured-text expression syntax with conditional selection, aggregates and time functions, and 7.4 gives tag access with `.quality` and `.age`. Tag names may contain `-` and segments that start with a digit, and a tag may be named `x.quality`.
Options: an operator `?:`; the IEC function `SEL`; a function `if`. Attributes only as `.quality`; also functions.
Decision: the EBNF of contract section 5.6, with `if(c, a, b)` evaluated lazily; `tag("...")` for names that are not identifiers; `.quality` and `.age`, refused when ambiguous, with `quality(x)` and `age(x)` as the unambiguous forms; TIME literals `T#5s`; INT, REAL, BOOL and TIME as reference 7.4; no mixing of BOOL and numbers; overflow, division by zero and non-finite results give BAD with OVERFLOW. Configuration expressions in templates use the same grammar without tags and time functions.
Reason: one grammar for virtual tags, alarms, interlocks and structured text means one parser and one set of tests (#100, phase 6); `if` reads better than `SEL` and needs no new operator.
Consequences: phase 6 implements the same grammar for structured text, with the statements of section 9.3.
Revision 0.2 (C++ B-6, S-16, S-17, S-36): a lexical section; `/` is true division (`ct_primary / 1000` with 250 is 0.25, not 0), `DIV` and `MOD` for integers; `AND`, `OR`, `XOR`, `NOT` take BOOL only, with bitwise functions for INT; keywords reserved in every position, `tag("...")` as the escape; a type table for every operator and function; aggregates `min_of`, `max_of` apart from the maths `min`, `max`; `in`, ranges and string literals for configuration expressions; `16#` hexadecimal; two deliberate departures from IEC 61131-3 stated (`**` right-associative, one unary operator in a row). Scaling is computed as two operations, never fused into a multiply-add, so that x86-64 and ARM64 give the same bits: the implementing pull requests (#100, #104) build the scaling and expression code with floating-point contraction off and record the flag; the transcendental functions may differ by one unit in the last place. A negative exponent divides by the exact power of ten.
Revision 0.3 (delta check, C++ N-B2, N-10): a range is no longer a primary (it was left-recursive and made `n IN 1..5` unparsable); it stands only after `IN` and as the value of `for`. Function names are not reserved: a one-segment name followed by `(` is a call, so `x.quality`, `flow.rate`, `power.avg` work. Only the operator words and literals are reserved, and only as a whole one-segment name (owner's ruling O-14). `**` binds tighter than unary minus, as in IEC 61131-3. `0x` hexadecimal accepted beside `16#`; integer literals bounded to 2^63 − 1 and real literals that underflow or overflow refused (CF-528); an unknown function is CF-529; `avg` is REAL; BOOL inputs refused in `sum`, `avg`, `min_of`, `max_of`. The syntax was transcribed into an LALR(1) parser generator, which built its tables with no conflict, and the corpus cases of contract test 11 were run through it. The contraction rule is the driver interface contract's D-98.
Revision 0.4 (C++ T-9): a number, time or string directly followed by a letter, digit, `_` or `#` is CF-520 (`3x`, `5mod 3`, `10ms`); a `-` ends a name; reserved words, function names and `T#` are case-insensitive, the units of a time are lower case; a `for` value that is not a range gives one INT; `quality(tag("…"))` for an escaped name. "As in IEC 61131-3" is dropped for the precedence of `**`, which is stated on its own. The LALR(1) check was run again with 80 cases, with no conflict.

## D-79: Generated tags and their names
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01); for #101
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: the tag set must contain every tag a service writes: the parts of an output (reference 8.1), area authority (8.3), alarm states (10.2), tunable parameters (7.3), driver system tags (6.3). The reference names none of them.
Options: names fixed by each service; names fixed by the configuration contract.
Decision: `<output>.mode`, `.status`, `.req_manual`, `.req_auto`; `sys.area.<area>.authority`; `<tag>.alarm.<id>` and `.shelved_until`; `ctl.<program>.<block>.<param>`; the interlock's own name; driver system tags as the driver interface contract names them, proposed `sys.drv.<channel>.<name>` and `sys.dev.<device>.<name>`. Owners, types, flags and value codes as in contract section 12.5. Mode and authority tags are RETAIN and RETAIN_NOW.
Reason: the tag set must be computable from the tree before any service runs, and the names must be checked against the 127-byte limit with the user's names (#119).
Consequences: services of phases 5 to 7 use these names and codes; a change is a minor version of the configuration contract and a tag-set change. Every tree with an output or area has RETAIN_NOW tags, so the 400 ms limit on `heartbeat_check_ms` always applies to it.
Revision 0.2: status value 6 ACCEPTED_UNVERIFIED; `<output>.refresh` (refresh state, alarmed when a refresh ends without a new request); `<output>.device_selected` only for a native select (none in phase 2); `sys.proc.<process>.config_id` and `config_state` for the apply report; process owners for `sys.proc` tags; no scan-group tag in phase 2.
Revision 0.3: `<output>.failsafe` (0 none, 1 safe state applied, 2 not confirmed and being repeated), owned by the writer of the target.

## D-80: Tree format and migration
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01)
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3; for review by a person. Review: committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux), 2026-11-22, on contract 0.1; revised in 0.2; delta check of 0.2 on 2026-11-22, answered in 0.3.

Context: outline item 11 asks how an older configuration tree is upgraded.
Options: a version per file; one version for the tree.
Decision: one integer `format` in `system.yaml`. Additions of optional keys or values are contract minor versions and keep the format; a change that invalidates or changes the meaning of a valid tree is a new format with a migration function in `libscada-conf` that rewrites only the lines it must change. After a software update `scada-conf` migrates the active tree and commits it as a new version; `scadactl config migrate` stages a migration for review. Protocol schemas are versioned with the format.
Reason: templates and instances depend on each other across files, so a per-file version would allow trees whose files have formats that do not fit together.
Consequences: older software refuses a tree that uses an addition as an unknown or reserved key rather than misreading it.
Revision 0.2: `format` may be left out while the tree is of format 1, as in the example of reference 12.3.

## D-81: A protocol is linked into its driver executable, not loaded as a plugin
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02)
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103)

Context: the outline of the driver interface contract leaves open whether a protocol is linked into its driver executable or loaded as a plugin. Reference 4.4 lists `/usr/lib/scada/` for "binaries and driver plugins"; reference 6.5 calls the hardware backends of `scada-io` plugins.
Options: (A) each protocol linked statically into its own `scada-drv-<protocol>` executable with `libscada-driver` and `libscada-tag`; (B) one generic driver executable that loads a protocol from a shared object at start.
Decision: (A). A protocol is a library (`lib<protocol>`) and a thin executable, as every service is (`CLAUDE.md`). The hardware backends of `scada-io` are compiled in and selected by configuration in phase 2; loading them at run time is not needed for the three backends of #107.
Reason: the process boundary already isolates a protocol (reference 6) and lets a driver be replaced, licensed and versioned on its own (reference 16.3); a loader adds an ABI to keep stable, a signing check for loaded code (reference 13.5) and a second way for a broken protocol to fail. `libscada-tag` is static and each service carries its own copy (D-66 f), so a loaded protocol would not share it anyway.
Consequences: a new protocol is a new executable and package. `/usr/lib/scada/` holds no protocol plugins in phase 2. If a later phase wants run-time loading (vendor I/O cards), it is a decision of that phase.

## D-82: Threads of a driver process: main, one command thread, one worker per channel; session protocols as an event loop (room)
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); survey item D-40; committee review of 0.1
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (sections 2.4, 2.7)

Context: the outline leaves open the threading model for report-driven protocols. The tag database allows one writing thread per owner and one consuming thread per queue (contract 1.0, sections 2 and 9); `scada_queue_next` is not real-time safe (its 9.5, #116); a wait set holds at most 127 sources (its 8.4).
Options: (A) a main thread, one non-real-time command thread that consumes every request queue of the process and posts every answer, one worker per channel that is the only writer of its channel's tags; protocols built on a stack with its own threads run as an event loop on the worker, helpers hand samples over bounded queues; (B) a protocol receive thread with a sample sink that writes tags; (C) workers take requests from the queues themselves.
Decision: (A). Request queues per process and source as reference 5.1 names them (`cmd.<protocol>.<S>`), so one wait set suffices (at most 126 sources); orders and outcomes between the command thread and a worker through pre-allocated lock-free queues of `max_pending_writes`, exactly one outcome per order; device state and a connection epoch per channel in atomics; every thread but the main thread blocks signals; workers log through a lock-free queue to the main thread. The event loop and helpers are room for phase 9 (`SessionProtocol`); a helper has one queue of its own, and write completions use reserved slots and a token passed by value.
Reason: (B) would make two writers of one owner; (C) would put system calls of `scada_queue_next` on workers and need one wait set per 127 queues. Per-channel queues (0.1) would use up to 2,400 of 4,096 queues at 300 channels for no gain: the in-process queue's BUSY already isolates a slow channel.
Consequences: an order reaches the device one in-process hop after `scada_queue_next`. A driver process has at most 126 request sources (configuration refuses more).
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check): the command thread admits an order to a channel only while admitted orders minus outcomes read are below `max_pending_writes`, so the outcome queue is never full; a logger thread, not the main thread, writes the journal, so a slow journal cannot stop the heartbeat.

## D-83: Real-time tier in phase 2: `scada-io` only; the hardware exchange sits between `SCADA_RT` parts
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); committee review of 0.1
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103); the ruling is O-4 of the configuration schemas contract (formerly R-3). `CLAUDE.md` and the coding standard are not changed.

Context: `CLAUDE.md` forbids system calls and I/O in code that runs in "the I/O scan"; an I/O scan must exchange with hardware. The committee found that 0.1 overstated what the `SCADA_RT` check does and that its system-call table was not what Linux gives.
Options: (A) only `scada-io` is in the real-time tier in phase 2; in a real-time thread the functions around the hardware exchange are `SCADA_RT`, the exchange itself is listed with its bound; enforcement by a per-thread allocation count and a system-call trace; (B) Modbus workers real-time as well (0.1); (C) a non-real-time I/O thread feeding the real-time one.
Decision: (A). The contract states the check as it is (direct calls to a deny-list; it does not follow calls and knows no system calls), lists the scan thread's system calls per backend, and exempts the two tests from the sanitizer builds; they run on native runners only. `scada_tag_notify` on a real-time thread is open, pending #116 point 3. Real-time protocol drivers and synchronous scan groups are a later minor version of this contract and the configuration contract together.
Reason: reference 7.5 gives Modbus 50 ms to several seconds, a 10 ms RTU scan is not reachable at common rates, and the honest statement of what is enforced is what the owner rules on.
Consequences: phase 2 Modbus workers run under the normal scheduler; `encode`, `frame_state` and `decode` are still `SCADA_RT` so that a later real-time worker uses them unchanged.

## D-84: A stalled channel worker ends its driver process
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); #116 comment, point 1; committee review of 0.1
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (O-3)

Context: liveness is per process while an owner is a thread (tag database contract 1.0, 10.1). A stalled worker in a process whose main thread beats leaves its tags with their last values, never OWNER_DOWN. Stopping only the tag heartbeat (0.1) is not seen by the service manager, and the other workers would go on executing orders in a process every reader sees as down.
Options: (A) the main thread detects the stall from a progress counter, stops the other threads at their next boundary, stops the tag heartbeat and the service manager's watchdog, and ends the process with `std::_Exit` and a distinct exit status; (B) per-owner liveness in the tag database (a layout change); (C) accept the gap.
Decision: (A). The counter moves at every transaction boundary and every idle wake; a stall is three times the longest single request deadline, at least 1 s. A channel key for separate processes per channel group is reserved.
Reason: it closes the gap without a layout change, and uses the existing response to a hung service (reference 14.4).
Consequences: one stalled channel restarts every channel of its protocol, and a stall that repeats can run the unit into the restart limit; the stall is logged with the channel's name. The owner rules on this (O-3).
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check): every wait of a worker is capped at a third of its stall limit, and the limit is three times the longest of the request deadline and `connect_timeout`, at least 1 s, so that an idle worker or a worker in a connect is never taken for a stalled one.

## D-85: Results of a driver: reason and raw protocol code in `WriteResult.protocol_error`
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); survey items D-30, D-31
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (R-4)

Context: the survey asks for about twenty outcomes of a write. The status of `WriteResult` has seven values and is frozen (tag database contract 1.0, 14.1); `protocol_error` is `int32_t`, "driver-specific, 0 if none".
Options: (A) `protocol_error = (reason << 16) | raw`, with a reason table common to every driver; (B) the raw code only, the reason in a diagnostic tag; (C) a layout version with a wider result.
Decision: (A), with one result per request at its final outcome, also for two-phase writes; a result the queue refuses is posted again until the request's expiry.
Reason: (A) gives `scada-cmd` one form to read and keeps the field driver-specific and 0 when there is nothing to say; a second result per request would be dropped by the requester as a duplicate id (contract 1.0, 9.2).
Consequences: the reason table is part of the driver interface contract; a new reason is a minor version of it.

## D-86: A driver-stamped event time is marked LOCAL_TIME
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); survey item D-12
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (section 5.8; R-1)

Context: the survey's reports differed on how to mark an event whose device gave no time, stamped by the driver.
Options: LOCAL_TIME; TIME_UNCERTAIN; both; none.
Decision: LOCAL_TIME. TIME_UNCERTAIN only for a clock that may be wrong: a device clock of unknown trust (the configuration default), a local time in the repeated or skipped hour, the unit's own clock unsynchronised.
Reason: LOCAL_TIME means "`source_time_ns` was set by the owner, not a device" (tag database contract, 6); TIME_UNCERTAIN for it too would give one flag two meanings.
Consequences: every polled Modbus sample without a time companion carries LOCAL_TIME.

## D-87: A change latch is an ordinary point in a side-effect read range
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); survey item D-21; committee review of 0.1
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (sections 5.6, 6.6; R-2)

Context: a device may report a state bit plus a latch bit that records "changed since last read", cleared on read.
Options: (A) expose the latch as its own BOOL tag; (B) synthesise an on-then-off event at the poll time.
Decision: (A). The latch is a bit point of a register range marked `side_effect` in the configuration's read rules, so the range is read once per poll, never retried and never read by keep-alive, probe, identity or `READ_NOW`. The reserved configuration key `change_latch` is dropped.
Reason: (B) invents samples and their times; with (A) a transient shows as the latch set for one poll.
Consequences: synthesis stays room for a later protocol option, which would carry LOCAL_TIME.

## D-88: Replayed events: deferred to the historian and alarm contracts
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); survey X-6; committee review of 0.1
Status: deferred by the product owner on 2026-11-23 to the historian contract (X-6; D-103)

Context: after a reconnect a device may replay buffered events, then an interrogation gives the current state. 0.1 proposed writing them with STALE. The committee found that phase 2 does not need it and that STALE has a cost: if the alarm engine raises no alarm from a STALE sample, a trip during the outage raises none.
Options: (a) write replayed events in device-time order with STALE, interrogation last; (a') the same without STALE; (b) mark them for the historian only, which needs a new path or a layout version.
Decision: defer the choice to the historian contract (phase 3) and the alarm contract (phase 7). The raw sample keeps room for a cause and a replay mark; the driver interface contract lists the options and their consequences.
Reason: no phase 2 protocol replays events, and the consequences fall on the historian and the alarm engine.
Consequences: none for phase 2 code.

## D-89: Native select with a short device window: per output `at_select` or `at_operate`
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02), agreed with #98 (P2-01); survey X-7
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (X-1, X-7). Review by a person: select before operate

Context: devices with a native select have windows from about 1 s to over 60 s; the gateway's select must suit an operator (reference 8.2 step 6, 30 s by default).
Options: (A) the gateway's select never longer than the device's window; (B) per output: `at_select`, the device selects at SELECT and `select_timeout` must not exceed the window, or `at_operate`, the gateway holds the operator's selection and the driver runs the device's select and operate back to back at OPERATE; (C) the driver chooses by comparing the two times.
Decision: (B), default `at_select`. The contract fixes the `at_operate` sequence, its timing and its results (section 8.6). An `at_operate` output needs a window of at least twice the device's request deadline (CF-716).
Reason: (A) is unusable for an operator with a 1 s window; (C) would switch behaviour silently when a timeout is edited.
Consequences: with `at_operate` a device's refusal arrives at OPERATE, after the operator's confirmation; the SELECT checks nothing on the device. Phase 2 has no native select.

## D-90: Refresh of setpoints belongs to the request whose value it repeats, and its end is visible
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); survey X-5; implemented in phase 5; committee review of 0.1
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (X-5, changed: a refused new request does not end the refresh). Review by a person: command handling, "no audit, no action"

Context: some devices drop a setpoint after a validity time, or fall back when setpoints stop. "No audit, no action" must hold. The committee found that a refresh can end silently (a restart of `scada-cmd`, any tag-set commit, a refused new request) and that 0.1 allowed refresh on any kind.
Options: (A) refreshes covered by the event record of the request whose value they repeat, ending for good when a condition fails; (B) a record per refresh; (C) no refresh service.
Decision: (A), for `kind: setpoint` only, never on a `dangerous`, `sbo` or `nvm` target. The end is shown in `<output>.refresh` (2: ended other than by a new request) and journalled best-effort; nobody re-establishes a standing MANUAL setpoint automatically.
Reason: (B) floods the events queue with no added information; (C) leaves devices that need it falling back; a silent end of a grid operator's curtailment limit is a compliance event, hence the tag.
Consequences: the journal record of a refreshed output says that it refreshes (phase 5). An AUTO output uses this or the refresh of `scada-control` (reference 7.2), not both.
Revised in contract 0.5 (ruling X-5 of 2026-11-23, D-103): a refused new request no longer ends the refresh. The refreshed value is that of the last request executed OK; a request refused before sending, or refused by the device, leaves it running with `<output>.refresh` 3 (active on an older value); a request sent with an unknown outcome (timeout, partial) ends it with 2, because writing either value again could undo or resend a command.

## D-91: One fail-safe table; a fail-safe action never waits for its audit record
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); survey X-9; committee review of 0.1
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (X-9; O-1, formerly R-5; O-12 changed to option (b)). Review by a person: fail-safe and shutdown behaviour, "no audit, no action"

Context: references 6.5 and 14.4 give safe states to `scada-io` and say drivers hold or go safe when `scada-control` stops. 0.1 made a remote safe write wait for the driver's own event record; the committee found that a fail-safe action that waits for audit fails exactly when control and audit are down together, against reference 6.5 and the phase 5 acceptance.
Options: (A) stop refreshing only; (B) the framework can hold, write the safe state or write the release value, journalled first, else only stop refreshing (0.1); (B') the same, the action taken first and journalled best-effort after.
Decision: (B'), with one table of cases and defaults (driver interface contract 8.13): local outputs `safe` on shutdown and on loss of `scada-control`, remote outputs `hold`. The main thread is the one producer of the process's events queue.
Reason: a safe state on loss of control is the configured answer to a failure; "no audit, no action" stays whole for commands.
Consequences: a refused event record is counted (`sys.proc.<process>.safe_unjournalled`) and logged. The keys and defaults are the configuration contract's (its 7.4, agreed with #98): `on_shutdown` `safe` for local and `hold` for remote outputs; `on_control_loss` for AUTO and `scada-control` only, `safe` locally and `hold` remotely; `on_generation_change` per output, default `hold`, safe when the limit of reference 5.5 is exceeded; in MANUAL a restart of `scada-cmd` drops nothing.
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check; ruling R-11): a fail-safe write that did not end OK is repeated every `failsafe_retry` (default 5 s) while its condition holds, and at once when the device returns, shown in `<output>.failsafe`; an exception to "no resend of a write" for fail-safe actions only. At a generation change each writer measures the limit itself (configuration contract 13.5, G6). Remote switching outputs left at `hold` get a warning (O-12).
Revised in contract 0.4 (third pass; rulings R-11, R-18): the condition is defined as a state machine per output (contract 8.13.2 to 8.13.4). Control is lost when the output is in AUTO (or OWNER_DOWN with a stored AUTO), `scada-control` reads down, a grace has passed (`scada-control` seen up once in this generation, or `control_attach_grace`, default 60 s, after the writer's attach) and `after` has passed; a mode change, an accepted request or control back ends it and cancels a pending write at once. Repeats double to 60 s after a timeout, go only to usable devices, count against write limits, and end FAILED after `failsafe_max_refusals` refusals or a spent budget; the first write and each state change are journalled, not each repeat.
Revised in contract 0.5 (rulings O-12 and the FAILED value, 2026-11-23, D-103): a remote `single` or `double` output with a `safe_state` of `off` or `on` defaults to `safe` on control loss, with `after: 30s`, and to `hold` at an orderly stop (a further ruling of the same day; a driver that is stopped and not started again leaves such outputs as they were) (one supervised restart of `scada-control` with back-off, its attach and first cycle, after its 500 ms heartbeat timeout, before a plant trips); other remote outputs stay `hold`. `<output>.failsafe` is 3 for FAILED, distinct from 2 (still trying).

## D-92: Checks a driver makes on every write request
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); survey items D-29, D-35, D-36; committee review of 0.1
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (sections 8.2, 8.6, 8.9; O-2). Review by a person: command handling, select before operate

Context: reference 8.1 has the driver accept AUTO requests only in AUTO mode and OPERATOR requests only in MANUAL; tag database contract 17 item 19 makes drivers time their selections; reference 14.4 forbids automatic retry of operator commands. The committee found that a dead `scada-cmd` stopped automatic control, that a selection survived a mode change, that a DIRECT could act on a selected output, and that `local_blocks_auto` had no enforcer for AUTO.
Options: for the mode tag read: fail closed always (0.1), or allow AUTO on the stored mode when OWNER_DOWN is the only defect.
Decision: the eleven checks of contract 8.2 in their order, for every source: value finite before limits; AUTO allowed on a stored mode of AUTO when the mode tag reads BAD with OWNER_DOWN only (O-2), every other defect fails closed; `local_blocks_auto` checked by the driver for AUTO; demoted and refusing devices refused; a selection ends on a mode change; a DIRECT on a selected output refused; no resend of any write once a byte was sent; rate limits counted at the first byte sent.
Reason: only `scada-cmd` can change the mode, so while it is down the stored mode cannot change; the driver is the only check AUTO requests pass besides the logic.
Consequences: a busy device answers REJECTED BUSY and the requester decides.
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check): `local_blocks_auto` is checked on the area's physical local-panel input, refused while it is active or not GOOD (R-16); the O-2 exception is written against tag database 6.2; a `READ_NOW` on a demoted device is an immediate probe, at most one per device in 10 s (R-12).

## D-93: The unit of the protocol interface is one request; the framework owns the exchange
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); committee review of 0.1
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (X-10)

Context: in 0.1, as in reference 6.1, a protocol's `poll` was one poll group and did its own I/O, so the framework could not act between requests, while it had to pace, retry, serve writes first, split refused blocks, send keep-alives, keep select and operate exclusive and count per request.
Options: (A) the protocol plans, encodes, finds the end of an answer and decodes, as pure functions; the framework owns the transport and every request; (B) one call per request, the protocol doing its own I/O; (C) one call per poll group (0.1).
Decision: (A) for phase 2 (`FrameProtocol`: `configure`, `device_caps`, `point_caps`, `plan`, `encode`, `frame_state`, `decode`), every function `noexcept` and the protocol object without mutable state. Stack-based protocols of phase 9 get a second interface (`SessionProtocol`, room).
Reason: scheduling, pacing, retries and the safety rules of the transport (abortive close on a write timeout, bounded answers) are written once and apply to every protocol; the protocol's functions can be fuzzed and checked as real-time code.
Consequences: #104 owns the transports (TCP, serial, RTU framing over TCP); #105 and #106 implement seven functions.
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check): `EncodeResult`, `FrameState` and `DecodeResult` are defined; the transaction id is per connection (`ChannelState`), so two devices sharing a unit id never share an id; overrides repeat `SCADA_RT`; IEC 104 and DNP3 are stated as room, with the channel-level form they would need.
Revised in contract 0.4 (third pass): `DecodeResult` has read-side kinds (`SPLIT`, `ABSENT`, `DEVICE_INVALID`, `BUSY`, `UNREACHABLE`), so that the framework acts on a refused read without reading protocol codes; the transaction id is recorded in a per-attempt record of the worker, so that planned requests stay immutable and a retry gets a new id.

## D-94: Engineering value to raw value on a write
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); committee review of 0.1
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (section 8.4). Review by a person: command handling

Context: neither draft of 0.1 said how a commanded engineering value becomes raw register contents.
Options: truncation or rounding; conversion of raw configured values or not.
Decision: raw = (value − offset) / gain, or (value − offset) × divide_by, in binary64 with contraction off, rounded half away from zero for an integer raw type and to nearest even for `float32`; a result that is not finite or outside the raw type is INVALID_VALUE; NaN and infinity are refused before any limit. `safe_state` and `release` declare their domain (`raw` or `value`). Points with `exponent_from`, `mod10k`, `datetime`, `epoch`, strings, and bit or field points without a `write.mask` are not writable. Golden vectors fix it.
Reason: truncation writes 124 for 12.5 kW at a gain of 0.1; a raw release pattern passed through the inverse scale overflows.
Consequences: the vectors are shared by #104, #105 and the simulators.
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check): INT64 and UINT64 tags use checked integer arithmetic, with an exact division by the gain, so no value above 2^53 goes through binary64; `divide_by` is the exact decimal form.

## D-95: TCP channels: abortive close on a write timeout, consecutive timeouts as a lost connection, idle close noticed
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); committee review of 0.1 (Linux L-1, L-12)
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (section 7.3; R-15, R-17). Review by a person: command safety

Context: on TCP a request passed to `send` stays in the kernel's send queue and is retransmitted for minutes; a write answered TIMEOUT could be executed after its expiry when the network returns.
Options: close the connection on a write timeout or not; detect a dead connection from timeouts or only from the kernel.
Decision: a write whose deadline passes without a complete answer closes the connection with `SO_LINGER` 0 before its result is posted; `lost_after_timeouts` consecutive timeouts (default 3) are a lost connection; `TCP_USER_TIMEOUT` set to the channel's largest request deadline; the worker watches an idle connection and checks it before a write, so that a write that cannot leave is COMM_FAIL (nothing sent); reconnect back-off resets after the first valid answer; literal addresses only.
Reason: "never executed late" must hold up to our last byte; a closed idle connection must not turn a command that was never received into "outcome unknown".
Consequences: what a gateway behind `rtu_over_tcp` buffers itself cannot be recalled; the contract states it. Conformance test 24.
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check; rulings R-15, R-17): consecutive read timeouts close a TCP connection only when the run spans every non-demoted unit of the channel (a request counts once after its retries; any valid answer, an exception included, resets the run; a probe neither counts nor resets); a read timeout on `rtu_over_tcp` never closes; a side-effect read timeout and a partial send close; after a close the driver makes itself, a reconnect within one request deadline keeps the other devices' tags, states and identity.
Revised in contract 0.4 (third pass; R-15, R-17): the run of consecutive read timeouts is one rule, quoted in the configuration contract: it covers every eligible unit id (a device in `INITIALISING` or `RUNNING` with a poll group), holds at least `lost_after_timeouts` requests, is evaluated before the timed-out device's demotion, and is emptied by any valid answer and by a reconnect; a second close before any valid answer uses the reconnect back-off. A broken frame on `tcp` closes; a wrong unit id on `rtu_over_tcp` is dropped. A reconnect keeps the other devices' state when it succeeds within `connect_timeout`. `TCP_USER_TIMEOUT` is `lost_after_timeouts` times the largest request deadline, at least 10 s.

## D-96: The meaning of `timeout`, the frame time, and serial line handling
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02), agreed with #98; committee review of 0.1 (Linux L-2, L-10)
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (sections 7.1, 7.2)

Context: neither draft said when a timeout starts; at 1,200 bit/s a 125-register answer takes 2.34 s, so a 1 s timeout demoted every such device. User space cannot meet RS-485 turnaround by draining and toggling lines.
Options: timeout from the send, from the end of the request, or including the frame.
Decision: `timeout` runs from the end of the request on the wire to the first byte of the answer; the framework adds the frame time of request and longest answer at the bit rate plus 2 characters (0 on TCP). The worker never drains or toggles lines in an exchange; direction control is the adapter's or the kernel's RS-485 mode; the end of an answer comes from its length and CRC; after a timeout input is discarded and a quiet time kept; the port is opened exclusively and reopened by its stable path when it disappears.
Reason: a value users write in every file must have one meaning from the first format.
Consequences: serial timing is tested on the target board (#41); a pseudo-terminal cannot.
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check): two stages, the first byte by end of request plus `timeout`, the rest within the frame time of the longest answer plus a margin of the larger of 3.5 characters and 20 ms; bits per character from the format; on `rtu_over_tcp` the user's `timeout` covers the line behind the gateway; the port is opened non-blocking without hardware flow control; no RS-485 direction control in phase 2 (R-13).

## D-97: Local I/O: bounded channels only on the scan thread; outputs at start, stop and crash
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02), #107; committee review of 0.1 (Linux L-3, L-9, L-13)
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (section 13). Review by a person: fail-safe behaviour

Context: a GPIO line on an expander or an IIO conversion sleeps for milliseconds to a second; the kernel promises no state for a line whose process died; a line requested as an output must be given a level.
Options: read every channel on the scan thread; a helper for slow channels. At a crash: nothing, or a stop step of the service manager.
