Decision: a backend declares per channel whether its calls are bounded; unbounded channels are read by a helper thread; `at_start: hold | safe` per output; a stop step run by the service manager after every stop of `scada-io`, crash included, drives the safe states; owners `io` (main) and `io.scan`.
Reason: a 10 ms scan cannot contain a 1 s bus timeout; a safe state after a crash can only be applied by another process.
Consequences: #107 adds the stop step to the install tree.
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check): `scan: auto` chooses the scan thread only for memory-mapped GPIO and a helper for other GPIO and every IIO channel, with an alarm and a move to the helper for a call above 1 ms; IIO analog outputs reserved; `hold` at start keeps direction and level; the stop step covers outputs whose `on_shutdown` is `safe` or `release`; after the step nothing is promised unless the controller keeps the level of a released line, a deployment requirement.

## D-98: Floating-point contraction off for scaling and value conversion
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); committee review of 0.1 (C++ S-17)
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (R-7)

Context: `raw x gain + offset` is contracted to a fused multiply-add on ARM64 and not on baseline x86-64, so scaled values can differ in the last bit between the architectures, which the golden vectors forbid.
Options: compile the scaling and conversion code with contraction off (`-ffp-contract=off`, an added flag, not one removed from coding standard 2); allow a tolerance in the tests.
Decision: contraction off for the framework's scaling and conversion code.
Reason: bit-identical values on both architectures make the vectors and the historian comparable across units.
Consequences: a flag on one library, recorded here as coding standard 2 asks for a flag change.

## D-99: Command kinds and what each sends
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02), agreed with #98; committee review of 0.1 (SCADA B-1)
Status: accepted: ruled by the product owner on 2026-11-23 (D-103) (section 8.3). Review by a person: command handling

Context: the configuration draft defined seven kinds and the driver draft none; a framework-timed pulse whose off write times out leaves a coil energised.
Options: framework-timed pulses or device-timed only; refresh on any kind or setpoints only.
Decision: per kind the request value and what is sent (driver interface contract 8.3); phase 2 pulses are device-timed (one write); a framework-timed pulse is room, and if added its trailing edge is repeated until it succeeds or is reported failed with an alarm; pulses and triggers are never refreshed and never a safe action.
Reason: no write is retried (D-92), so a two-write pulse cannot be made safe without that exception, which needs its own review.
Consequences: the configuration contract cites the table.
Revised in contract 0.3 (delta check): the kinds table gives target type and safe action per kind; a double point is INT64 1 or 2 also on two coils, its safe state `off` or `on`; `latch` reserved; step, pulse and trigger have fail-safe keys `hold` only (CF-719); a master-timed pulse is refused (CF-720, O-16).

## D-100: How a committed tree reaches the services
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01); for #102 and #104
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3, section 13; agreed with the #99 session; for review by a person (restart behaviour). Where the active tree lives is the owner's ruling O-5, the version store O-11, the trigger O-13, the check mode O-17.

Context: the committee review of 0.1 (Linux L-6, C++ fit section, lead's direction 9) found that neither draft said where the active tree lives (the root file system is read-only, reference 4.4, 14.5), how a commit is activated atomically, which files sit beside the tree, how a service learns of a change (`scada-conf` cannot signal processes of other users), how it reports back, which tree a reattaching driver loads, and who starts a driver that had no channel. The delta check of 0.2 (lead's blocking 1, Linux LD-1, LD-4, LD-11, C++ N-6, N-7) found three readings of what a driver on the old generation does when the link already names a version with another tag set, one of which stops all polling; no trigger for `scada-tagd`; a `notify` unit that exits 0 before ready counted as failed; a privileged helper needed in phase 2 that no issue builds; protocol libraries linked into `scada-conf`; a changed uid against "a version is never changed"; and an expanded file without a format.
Options, store: a Git repository as reference 12.4 suggests; plain version directories. Options, trigger: a signal through the privileged helper; the service manager; a socket; a watch on the active link by every service, `scada-tagd` included. Options, protocol validation: link each protocol library into `scada-conf`; run each driver executable in a check mode. Options, a driver with no channel: exit 0 and be started by a helper; idle.
Decision: versions are complete directories `/var/lib/scada/conf/versions/<n>/` (the tree in `tree/`, beside it `meta.json`, the tag set, the name map, one expanded file per process, later the signature); `active` is a symbolic link renamed atomically after every file and directory is synced; `/etc/scada` is a link to the active tree; `apply.json` records the apply in progress, and one apply runs at a time. Everything is `scada-conf:scada`, directories 2750, files 0640. Every service and `scada-tagd` watch the link. With the same `config_hash` services reload live and report within 2 s. With another one every service keeps running unchanged on the old generation, `scada-tagd` builds the new generation and retires the old one (tag database contract 11.3), the services move at RETIRING, each loading the expanded file whose `config_hash` matches its generation (the newest such version, so a driver restarted before the build runs the old file), and report within the 5 s limit; each output writer measures the limit itself. A tag set that does not build sends the link back and reports failed; a hung `scada-tagd` makes the apply `incomplete` with nothing made safe, and it completes when `scada-tagd` restarts. A power cut at any step boots into the active version. A driver with no channel never exits: it reports ready, idles and attaches when its protocol gains a channel. Stage 6 runs `scada-drv-<protocol> --check` (and `scada-io --check`) as a child process under the user of `scada-conf`, with limits, no device access, and its output parsed as outside input. The expanded file is written and read by one library type, keeps every scalar's written form and location, has limits, a round-trip test and a fuzz target. A changed uid makes a new version of the same tree.
Reason: the read-only root and reference 14.5 need writes under `/var/lib/scada` with a single atomic switch; Git is GPL-2.0 as a program (`CLAUDE.md` forbids it until the licensing decision). A watched link needs no privilege and no helper. Keeping the old configuration until RETIRING means a commit never stops acquisition. Running the driver's own loader and `configure` in a child process keeps a protocol's licence and faults in its own process (D-81) and checks the bytes the driver will read.
Consequences: #102 builds the store, activation, history, rollback and the report; #104 and #107 the watch, the idle state and the check mode; #105 gives `scada-tagd` the tag set path under `active` and installs the unit limits file read by CW-1015. Reference 12.4's "should be Git" and 5.5's reload signal are listed as differences (contract 16).
Revision 0.3: replaces the 0.2 text, in which drivers with no channel exited 0 and were started by `scada-sys`, `scada-conf` linked the protocol libraries, and the step order left the mismatch case open.
Revision 0.4 (lead's third pass, items 3, 6, 7; C++ T-4, T-6, T-10, T-11; SCADA T-5): stages 6 and 7 are swapped, so the check reads the expanded files with their `config_hash`, written to a private directory and then copied byte for byte into the version. A power cut inside commit step 3 before the rename is told from `apply.json` against the link and recorded `not applied`. The kind of apply is decided against the running generation's hash. A `config_state` counts only with its `config_id`; state 3 means "running the old configuration, waiting for the generation change"; `incomplete` is after 30 s. Each service remembers the version it last handled. Outputs that a commit removes get their old `on_shutdown` at G3, and G6 applies only to outputs present in both versions (O-18); the `scada-tagd` hang is O-19. The check mode child has standard input closed, no new privileges, no file size, its own process group and build-set limits; it is re-run after a driver update; an exit status that disagrees with its lines is CF-1012. The expanded file has its own limits (CF-1016), a stated JSON subset, a canonical text for computed values, and a format version that the loader reads one version back; a changed format makes a new version, as a changed uid does. Idle drivers keep the watchdog; a service whose uid changed waits instead of exiting. After the Linux third pass: `apply.json` is written by rename; after a refused G2 the switch back is a no-op for every service (state 1 again); a power cut before the switch back is repaired at the next start; the package installs a minimal tree that `scada-conf` commits as version 1; old versions are removed beyond `conf.keep_versions` (default 50), keeping the active one and the one of the `current` generation; the check runs on descriptor 3 with no new privileges, a death signal, file-size 0, 16 descriptors, a data limit, CPU 10 s and wall clock 30 s; after a driver update the re-check is part of the update's health check, so a refusal rolls the update back.

## D-101: The `io` protocol schema and local outputs at start and crash
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01); for #107
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3, Annex B; agreed with the #99 session; for review by a person (fail-safe output behaviour).

Context: contract 0.1 named `io` as a phase 2 protocol and had no schema for it (Linux L-3, lead's direction 8); #107 could not start. The kernel gives a GPIO line a level when it is requested and promises nothing after the process that holds it dies (Linux L-13); lines on bus expanders and slow converters sleep (L-9).
Options: chip and line numbers, or names; one scan for every line, or a helper for slow ones; safe states after a crash by the process, by the service manager, or not at all.
Decision: backends `simulator`, `gpio`, `iio`; a GPIO chip by its label and a line by name or offset, an IIO device and channel by name, never by `gpiochipN` or `iio:deviceN`; no path in the tree. Per channel `scan: every_cycle | helper`, the slow case read by a helper thread. Per output `start: hold | safe`, required. A crash leaves outputs as the hardware leaves them; a step run by the service manager after every stop of `scada-io` drives the safe states. A local output's default `on_control_loss` is `safe` at once (reference 6.5).
Reason: numbers change between boots; a sleeping call in a 10 ms scan breaks it; the process that died cannot drive a safe state.
Consequences: #107 builds the backends, the helper and the stop step; codes CF-660 to CF-662.
Revision 0.3 (Linux LD-5, LD-6, LD-7; SCADA N-4): `scan` defaults to `auto`, chosen at run time from the chip's bus, because the kernel does not report whether a chip sleeps; an explicit `every_cycle` is CW-660, and a call above 1 ms moves the channel to the helper at run time. A missing line or chip is a run-time channel failure and CW-661 at validation, never a refusal of the tree, so validation does not depend on the hardware present. IIO is read through the helper; IIO analog outputs are reserved. The stop step acts on outputs whose `on_shutdown` is `safe` or `release`, the same set as the driver interface contract; after it releases a line nothing is promised, and a safe level that must hold without a process is a deployment requirement. `at_start: hold` first requests the line as it is, so a driven output is not turned into an input.
Revision 0.4 (Linux, lead's item 8): CW-661 is removed: validation opens no hardware, so a missing line or chip is found when `scada-io` requests it and fails that channel only. The move of a slow channel to the helper is the driver interface contract's rule (not on one preemption; not stored). "Nothing is promised after the stop step" is put to the owner as O-20. The stop step reads the expanded file of the `current` generation (the one `scada-io` ran), runs after every stop with a 5 s bound, and exits 0 with no channel; `auto` gives `every_cycle` only when the chip and every ancestor are on the platform bus.

## D-102: One owner of every configuration key; fail-safe keys; command kinds and written values
Date: 2026-11-22
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01), with the driver interface contract (#99)
Status: proposed, in the draft contract `config-schemas.md` 0.3, sections 4.10, 7.2 and 7.4; agreed with the #99 session; for review by a person (command handling, fail-safe behaviour). The fail-safe audit rule is the owner's ruling O-1, the repeat of fail-safe writes R-11, the hold default of remote switching outputs O-12.

Context: the committee found the two drafts disagreeing on 20 to 29 keys and defaults (`on_mismatch`, back-off, reconnect, `connect_timeout`, `keepalive`, `on_invalid`, `bit_order`, the function list, the fail-safe keys and more), command kinds without a meaning per value, no inverse scaling for written values, a release value in the wrong domain, and fail-safe writes waiting for an audit record (lead's directions 1 and 6, C++ B-4, B-8, SCADA B-1 to B-3).
Options: each contract defines what it uses; one contract owns every key.
Decision: this contract owns every key name, type, default and validation code (section 4.10 lists the shared ones; the driver interface contract cites them and gives no defaults). Notable choices: `on_mismatch` `refuse`; `backoff` 10 s to `backoff_max` 300 s; `reconnect` 1 s to 30 s, reset after the first valid answer; `connect_timeout` 3 s; `keepalive` per device and template; `on_invalid` without `uncertain`; `bit_order` required with bits, no default; `functions`; `storage` required on writable points; `write_limits` on any writable point. Fail-safe keys, agreed with the #99 session after its objections: `on_shutdown` (`hold | safe | release`; default `safe` for a `scada-io` output, reference 6.5, and `hold` for a remote one, reference 14.4); `on_control_loss` (`{ action, after }`, `scada-control` down while the output is in AUTO; default `safe` at once for a `scada-io` output, `hold` for a remote one); `on_generation_change` (`hold | safe`, default `hold`, safe anyway when the limit is exceeded, as reference 19.2 makes it a setting). In MANUAL a restart of `scada-cmd` drops nothing: outputs hold, and a refreshed setpoint stops refreshing so that the device's own fallback acts. A fail-safe write is made first and journalled best-effort. `busy_retries` applies to reads only; `lost_after_timeouts` (channel, default 3) closes a TCP connection abortively after consecutive time-outs. Command kinds have a value each (section 7.2); `pulse` only device-timed in phase 2; `refresh` only for `setpoint`, never `dangerous`, `sbo` or `nvm`; the raw value of a setpoint is the inverse of `scale`, rounded half away from zero, with NaN and out-of-range values refused; `release` and `safe_state` state their domain (`value` or `raw`).
Reason: two documents defining one key drift, and a site writes the key once. The defaults chosen are the safe side where the drafts differed (refuse a mismatched device; require `storage` and `bit_order` rather than guess). Reference 6.5 requires local safe states when control stops, and the audit service may be down in the same failure.
Consequences: the driver interface contract removes its own key definitions and cites this contract; #104 and #107 implement the fail-safe table; phase 5 builds the rest of command handling on these keys.
Revision 0.3 (delta check: lead's blocking 2 and items 4 to 7, SCADA N-1 to N-3, N-5, N-10, B-1, F-1 to F-5): `lost_after_timeouts` applies to read time-outs only when they come from every device of the channel that is not demoted, so one dead unit behind a gateway is demoted alone; a write time-out still closes at once, and the other devices reconnect at once without COMM_FAIL; on `rtu_over_tcp` a read time-out never closes. A fail-safe write that was not confirmed is repeated every `failsafe_retry` (default 5 s) while its condition holds, shown in `<output>.failsafe`. `keep_last` gives UNCERTAIN with STALE, then BAD after `max_age`, never SUBSTITUTED. A switching output on a protocol driver that holds is CW-721. A `double` target is INT64 1 or 2, also on two coils; `safe_state` is defined per kind, and `step`, `pulse`, `trigger` have none (CF-719); a unit-timed pulse is CF-720; `latch` is reserved. A `false` to an on-only output is refused. `local_blocks_auto` acts on the panel's input tag, fail closed. A non-finite value without a marker is BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE. `timeout` cites the driver interface's two-stage deadline. Several outputs may share a command register when the bits they write are disjoint.
Revision 0.4 (lead's third pass, items 1, 2, 10; SCADA T-1 to T-4, T-8, T-9; C++ T-3): the condition, grace, end, repeat and stop of a fail-safe action are one state machine in the driver interface contract 8.13, which section 7.4 cites; this contract adds `control_attach_grace` (per process, default 60 s), `failsafe_max_refusals` (default 3) and makes `failsafe_retry` the first interval of a back-off to 60 s. `<output>.failsafe` is owned by the thread that writes the target (`io.scan` for a local output). CW-721 fires only when both keys are absent and never on a dangerous output; `safe` with `safe_state: hold` is CF-722; `release` outside a setpoint is CF-723. The integer path of the inverse scaling includes a target with no `scale` (gain 1, offset 0), gives the exact integer and checks every step.

## D-103: The product owner's rulings on the driver interface contract
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #99 (P2-02); PR #126
Status: decided by the product owner on 2026-11-23, on the committee's recommendation. Review by a person: command handling, fail-safe and shutdown behaviour, "no audit, no action"

Context: the driver interface contract 0.4 carried every conflict with reference 1.1 and every choice the reference left open as a ruling for the product owner (its section 18), with the committee's recommendation for each.
Options: per ruling, as listed in the contract's section 18 (the option drafted and the options not taken).
Decision: every ruling of the contract (X-1 to X-17, R-1, R-2, R-4, R-6, R-7, R-11 to R-18, O-2, O-3), and the rulings it shares with the configuration schemas contract (O-1, O-4, O-7, O-9, O-10, O-12, O-13, O-15 to O-20, recorded there), decided as drafted, with three changes: X-5, a refused new request does not end a running refresh (contract 8.12); O-12, option (b), remote switching outputs with a `safe_state` of `off` or `on` default to `safe` after 30 s on control loss, and remote outputs to `hold` at an orderly stop, so that a driver stop or update does not stop pumps (contract 8.13.1); `<output>.failsafe` 3 for FAILED (contract 8.13.3). X-6 stays deferred to the historian contract; X-13 stays a warning.
Reason: the committee's recommendations; the three changes as the product owner gave them.
Consequences: the contract is version 0.5, accepted for phase 2 implementation, and becomes 1.0 when phase 2 passes its acceptance tests. Reference 1.2 carries the changes to the reference, approved by the merge of PR #126. The decisions D-81 to D-99 are accepted with it. R-6 (SUBSTITUTED for a forced value and a configured substitute) takes effect with tag database contract 1.1 (#89), which changes the comment of the flag; until then a driver does not set it in those cases.

## D-104: Rulings of the product owner on the configuration schemas contract
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #98 (P2-01)
Status: decided by the product owner, on the committee's recommendation, 2026-11-23. Recorded in `config-schemas.md` 0.5, section 15. It settles the status of D-69 to D-80 and D-100 to D-102, which are accepted with the contract.

Context: contract 0.4 left 35 places open for the owner (X-1 to X-5, X-7 to X-9, X-12 to X-17, O-1 to O-21), each drafted with a proposed option and the recommendation of the committee of the lead session (C++, SCADA, Linux reviewers, three passes). Some rulings are shared with the driver interface contract, which records its own (R-1 to R-18) in its decision.
Options: per ruling, as listed in section 15 of the contract.
Decision: every ruling as drafted, on the committee's recommendation, with three changes:
- X-5: a newer request that is refused, before the device or by it, does not end the refresh of the old, accepted setpoint; a newer request sent with an unknown outcome ends the refresh, because refreshing either value would retry a command of unknown outcome or undo one; the refreshed value is that of the last request executed OK in the generation; `<output>.refresh` gains 3 (active on an older value after a refused request).
- O-12, option (b), with the owner's further ruling of the same day: a `single` or `double` output on a protocol driver whose `safe_state` is `off` or `on` defaults to `{ action: safe, after: 30s }` on control loss and to `hold` at an orderly stop, so that a driver stop or update does not stop pumps (a driver that is stopped and not started again leaves such outputs as they were); `hold` stays the default for both with `safe_state: hold`, for dangerous outputs and for the other kinds; local outputs keep `safe`. CW-721 now warns when such an output is set explicitly to hold on control loss.
- `<output>.failsafe` gets 3 for an action the writer gave up, apart from 2 for still trying (with R-11 of the driver interface contract).
X-13 stays a warning; X-6 stays deferred to the historian contract.
Reason: the owner followed the lead's recommendations. For O-12 the 30 s delay rides through a crash of `scada-control` and its supervised restart, attach and first cycle, so a short loss of the control process does not trip the plant, while a remote switching output still goes safe within about half a minute of control really being lost. The driver interface contract records its rulings in D-103.
Consequences: the contract is 0.5, accepted for phase 2 implementation, and becomes 1.0 when phase 2 passes its acceptance tests. The reference architecture becomes 1.2: the lines that section 16 of the contract names are changed in the driver interface pull request (#99), in one commit for both contracts. One ruling rests on a sentence of a document this pull request does not change: R-6 widens SUBSTITUTED beyond tag database contract 6 ("value entered by a person"); the lead takes the wording to the owner. O-2 needs no change there: tag database contract 6.2 returns the stored value under the OWNER_DOWN overlay, and when to use it is the driver interface contract's rule.


## D-105: Where stage 1 diagnostics point, and what counts toward the tree's entry limit
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03)
Status: accepted

Context: configuration schemas contract 1.4 says that every error names a file, a line and a column. Stage 1 errors are about a path, a directory or the whole tree, and have no line. Section 1.3 limits a tree to 20,000 files. A hostile tree can instead hold a directory with millions of entries that are not files of the tree, each one CF-101.
Options: line 0 for "no line"; line 1, column 1; a diagnostic without a location. For the limit: count regular files only; count every entry, directories included; count every entry but the directories the tree may hold.
Decision: a diagnostic about a whole file, a directory or the tree points at line 1, column 1 of that path. The 20,000 limit counts every entry of the tree, refused ones (links, FIFOs, unknown names) too, except the directories the tree may hold (at most 8 besides the root); a tree limit points at the entry that passed it. A directory is listed only as far as the limit plus those 8 directories, and a limit passed while listing points at that directory, `.` for the root, so the work of stage 1 is bounded whatever the tree holds. The scan reads directories depth first in byte order of their paths and stops when a tree limit is passed, so the same tree always stops at the same place.
Reason: 1-based locations keep one format for every diagnostic and every tool that reads it. Counting only regular files would leave the time spent on other entries unbounded; counting the tree's own directories would refuse a tree of exactly 20,000 files, which the contract allows.
Consequences: an invalid entry costs one of the 20,000, so a tree with many refused entries stops early with CF-113; it is refused either way.

## D-106: The YAML subset parser is the project's own; how it reports
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03)
Status: accepted (parser choice: the lead, 2026-11-23)

Context: D-70 leaves the parser of the YAML subset, a new dependency or not, to #100. Configuration schemas contract 1.1 and 1.4 ask for a refusal with a code, line and column for every construct outside the subset, and 1.3 for limits checked before the work they bound. The contract leaves open some details of how a refusal is reported.
Options: libyaml 0.2.5 (MIT), event API with marks, plus a layer that maps its events onto the subset's errors; rapidyaml (MIT), which builds the whole tree before anything can be checked; a parser of the subset written for the project.
Decision: the project's own parser (`libs/scada-conf/src/yaml_parser.cpp`), no dependency. It is recursive descent, and every collection it enters is checked against the depth limit first, so recursion is bounded. The reporting rules are these:
- The first refusal ends the parse of its file; a file gives at most one stage 2 error.
- Columns count characters (code points), not bytes. The offset in CF-114's message counts bytes from 0.
- A byte order mark is CF-110 ("a byte order mark"). Invalid UTF-8, a NUL, a CR without LF, C0 and C1 control characters and DEL are CF-114 anywhere. A tab is CF-114 outside a comment, a quoted scalar or a block scalar's content: indentation, separation and plain scalars use spaces only.
- An escape giving U+0000 (`\0`, `\x00`) is CF-110. Other escapes of YAML 1.2 are accepted; the schema decides whether a control character may stand in a value (contract 2.1).
- `null`, `Null`, `NULL` and `~` as plain values, an empty value and an empty sequence item are CF-112. As keys, and quoted, they are text.
- A single-pair mapping inside a flow sequence (`[a: b]`) is CF-110: the subset's flow mappings are written in braces.
- Two keys of one map with equal text are CF-111 in stage 2. Keys that are equal only after typing (`16` and `0x10`, contract 1.2) are CF-111 in stage 3, which types them.
- The nesting depth counts collections: the root collection is at depth 1, and 33 nested collections are CF-113.
- An empty file, or one of comments only, is an empty map; the schema then reports what it needs.
- A `.st` file is not parsed: structured text is not available in this version, and every `control/*.st` file is CF-920 at 1:1, "structured text is not available in this version" (contract 9.3, 0.6; the lead, 2026-11-23). A program that is not run must not look accepted. The structured-text parser is phase 6.
- U+FFFE and U+FFFF are CF-114, as YAML 1.2 does not allow them.
- Indentation is counted in spaces, also on a continuation line of a quoted scalar; a tab may follow the indentation there. A tab where the indentation is still due is a tab in indentation: CF-114 at the tab (contract 1.1; the lead, 2026-11-24).
- Every line inside a flow collection is indented more than the key or the sequence item that holds it. A closing bracket that is not is CF-110 with its own message: indent the bracket, or write the collection as a block (the lead, 2026-11-23). A block collection would also do, so the rule costs nothing.
- A key is on one line, in flow collections too, although YAML 1.2 allows a plain key over several lines in a flow mapping.
- A document marker at the start of a line is CF-110 inside a root block scalar too.
- The scalar limit holds for the value as it will be: lines are counted before they are stored, trailing white space of a line is not counted, and empty lines are kept as a count, so that no input makes the parser hold more than the limit for one scalar.
- Duplicate keys are found with an ordered set in the document builder (D-111), not a hash table with a fixed seed. A message names a key in double quotes, so that an empty key stays visible.
Reason: the subset refuses constructs that a general parser accepts quietly, and each refusal needs its own code and column. Every scalar must keep its source text and quoting style (contract 1.2), and every limit must be checked before its work. A library would need a mapping layer for all of that, and would bring code that the project cannot bound or fuzz on its own terms. Without a dependency there is no licence to record.
Consequences: the parser is a fuzz target (`yaml_fuzz`) with a seed corpus of every refused construct. The README of libscada-conf says plainly that it is a subset parser and lists what it refuses. A test compared its trees with PyYAML (BaseLoader) on 15,000 random documents written in every style PyYAML emits; they were equal. That comparison is not part of the suite, because PyYAML is not a dependency.

## D-107: The schema validator of stage 3, and the choices the key tables left open
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03)
Status: accepted (the `x-scada-type` keyword and its annotations: the lead, 2026-11-23)

Context: D-69 has the JSON Schema files written from the contract's tables, read by a validator of the project's own with a fixed subset of keywords. The subset cannot state the contract's scalar types (1.2, 2.2), name grammars (2.1), reserved keys and values, secrets, exclusive groups or the key types of maps. Some keys in the tables give a range or a requirement only in words, or not at all.
Options: the scalar types and ranges in C++ tables beside structure-only schema files; project keywords in the schema files.
Decision: project keywords, listed with their codes in contract Annex C (0.6): `x-scada-type` (the types of the Annex C table), `x-scada-min` and `x-scada-max`, `x-scada-key-type`, `x-scada-exclusive`, `x-scada-reserved`, `x-scada-reserved-values` and `x-scada-secret`, plus the standard `dependentRequired` for CF-208. Other decisions:
- The schema files are read with the YAML subset parser and built into the library at configure time.
- A message names a key by its dotted path from the file's root, with list indexes from 0 (`time.sync.servers[0]`). A missing key is reported at the map, which starts at its first entry.
- A value that fits no shape of a `oneOf` gets the errors of the shape that takes its kind of node and has the fewest errors; the first of equals wins.
- Where the tables are silent:
  - `time.timezone` is checked for the form of an IANA name only. Whether the zone exists on the unit depends on the installed tz database, a distribution path that the tree's validation does not read.
  - `storage.free_space_alarm` is 0 % to 100 %.
  - An entry of `network.interfaces` needs `name`, 1 to 15 bytes (the kernel's limit).
  - `email` needs `server` and `from`; `upstream` needs `url`; `upstream.window` needs `from` and `to`; `authority.local_panel` needs `input` and `active`.
  - `realtime.cpus` takes 0 to 4,095.
  - The names of an enumeration are free text.
  - A duration whose key states no range is at least 1 ms: section 2.2 allows `0s` "only where the key allows it".
- Rules that stage 3 checks in code are CF-102, CF-211 (`format` other than 1) and "`time.sync.servers` unless the mode is `none`" (CF-202, reported at `time` when `sync` is absent, because the mode defaults to `ntp`).
Reason: one place per key, the schema file, with types, ranges and codes beside the structure; the files stay valid JSON Schema for an editor or another tool.
- Integers (the lead's ruling on the review of #131, B3, 2026-11-24): a text reads as an integer in one of three ways: not of the integer form; of the form and within 64 bits; of the form but larger (`read_integer`, `IntegerForm`; `parse_integer` gives the value of the second). A scalar of the integer form that does not fit its key's range is CF-204 everywhere, keys included, never CF-203. Under `number` and its kin, an integer of the form must fit int64 or uint64, else CF-204; it is never read as a decimal ("the written form is kept", contract 1.2). A typed read gives OUT_OF_RANGE for it.
- Integer forms under `decimal` (the lead's ruling N1, 2026-11-24): a `decimal` key takes any scalar of the integer form (of any size, hex too) or of the decimal form whose nearest binary64 is finite and not rounded to zero, and `to_decimal` gives that nearest value; only another value is refused (CF-203). The value is correctly rounded (to nearest, ties to even): a decimal text through `from_chars`, a hexadecimal integer beyond 64 bits from its top 64 bits with a sticky bit for the rest, rounded once and scaled by a power of two. The "must fit int64 or uint64" rule is for `number` and its two variants, which keep the written form.
- Durations and sizes of their form beyond uint64 (milliseconds or bytes) are CF-204, as integers are, and `to_duration_ns` gives OUT_OF_RANGE (`read_duration_ms`, `read_size_bytes`). A unit that section 2.2 does not list (`EiB`) is not of the form: CF-206.
- Types (B1): a scalar typed through `$ref`, `allOf` or `oneOf` keeps the type the referenced schema gave it. A schema without an `x-scada-type` types a scalar as STRING only if nothing typed it in the same check, so the order of `allOf` parts does not matter. A scalar that got an error has no type.
- The compiler checks keyword values, not only names (S2): an `x-scada-type` or `x-scada-key-type` that the validator does not implement; `minimum` and `maximum` that are not numbers, or on a type that is not a number type; `x-scada-min` and `x-scada-max` not in the form of a duration, a size or (for `text`) a count of bytes; `x-scada-reserved` and `x-scada-secret` other than `true` or `false`; an `x-scada-key-type` without a schema in `unevaluatedProperties`; a `type` other than the one an `x-scada-type` is written as (`integer` for the integer types, `number` or `integer` for `decimal` and `number`, `boolean`, `number` and `string` for a number or a percentage or an expression, `integer` and `string` for a raw pattern and a `repeat` range, any kind for `string`, `expression` and `config_expression` as long as `string` is among them (C2 of the review of #132: `{ "type": "integer", "x-scada-type": "string" }` would accept `abc`), `string` for the rest); a keyword that does not apply to the kind of node the schema's `type` allows, or, without `type`, keywords of more than one kind of node. Each makes the schema file not compile, so a type not implemented yet cannot check nothing.
- A cycle through `$ref`, `allOf` or `oneOf` does not compile (S3); recursion through `properties` or `items` goes down the document and is allowed. A nested `oneOf` checks each branch in full; its cost is bounded when the set compiles (below).
- Work of validation (the lead, 2026-11-24): `check` checks a node once for each schema of its closure through `$ref`, `allOf` and `oneOf`, and each of those checks a child with its own property, item or unevaluated schema. The compiler finds, as a fixed point, the most checks of one document node, B(root), where B(s) = max(g(s), D(s)), g(s) = 1 + the g of s's closure targets, and D(s) = the largest B below s itself + the D of s's closure targets. A set whose B passes 64 does not compile; so does a recursion down the document through a `oneOf` or `allOf`, whose B grows without end. The bound is coarse for recursion (C1 of the review of #132, the lead's ruling: keep it): a schema that reaches itself through `properties`, `items` or `unevaluatedProperties` and also refers, through `$ref`, `allOf` or `oneOf`, to a schema with keys or items of its own does not compile, although its real work may be a few checks per node (`block = allOf[common] + { children: [block] }`). No built-in schema recurses; a schema that needs such recursion must bring a bound per child key with it. The compile message says so. Finding the fixed point takes up to one pass per schema over all schemas, so it is quadratic in their number: 17 ms for the built-in set, 83 s for 30,000 chained definitions. Validation work is at most B times the document's nodes, and a branch's held types at most B entries per scalar. The built-in schemas give 21 (`SchemaSet::checks_per_node`). Measured worst case: a template of 990,000 nodes, every point with an inline enumeration of 11,000 entries under the `oneOf` of `enum`, validates in 0.67 s to 0.81 s with a peak of 43 MB (release build, x86-64).
- A literal number under `number_or_expression` (the lead's review of #132, B3): a plain scalar of the integer or the decimal form is a literal and is checked at stage 3 against the key's `minimum` and `maximum`; where the key's `type` names `integer` and not `number`, it must be an integer (CF-203, "must be an integer or an expression"). Only another text is an expression and waits for expansion (CF-410 there). `config_expression` (`when`) and `for_value` (a `repeat` range) are the types of Annex C for configuration expressions; until stage 3 checks expression syntax (slice 5), both are text, and a `for_value` of the integer form must fit int64 (CF-204).
- One diagnostic per cause (the lead's review of #132, S2): a node of the wrong kind gets one CF-203, however many schemas state its kind (a framework key that the protocol's schema states again). Validation keeps one bit per node for it, and a `oneOf` branch the nodes it reported.
- A duration is at most 9,223,372,036,854 ms, the longest whose nanoseconds fit int64, else CF-204 (S4): a value that validation accepts is readable.
- Text without `%` where a percentage is expected is CF-203 ("must be a number or a percentage"); text with `%` that does not read is CF-206 (S6). A decimal of the decimal form that is not finite or rounds to zero is CF-203.
- `resolution` (C3 of the review of #132): `1s`, `1ms`, or `<n>ns` with n a whole number of the decimal form of at least 1 (`0x10ns`, `01ns` and `0ns` are CF-203); n beyond int64 nanoseconds is CF-204, as a duration is (`read_resolution_ns`). `raw_pattern_or_nan` takes `nan` plain only (Annex C: "Plain only").
- Messages name a key or a value with `quote_text`, cut at 64 bytes, with `...` after the closing quote so that a value ending in `...` is not taken for a cut one (S5, D-110).
- Memory (S1): outside a `oneOf` branch every error goes to the stage's diagnostics at once, which keep the first 1,000 and a count; a branch holds at most 1,000, the first in the order of the report (section 1.4), and counts the rest (`Diagnostics::count_unlisted_errors`). A branch also holds a type per scalar it typed; the bound on nested `oneOf` of the next slice is to cover that too.
Consequences: a new contract type is a new `x-scada-type` name, added to the validator, its known-type table and Annex C together. Templates, channels, tags, outputs, alarms and programs get their schema files in the next slice of #100; until then their files are parsed but not schema-checked.

## D-108: The schema files of templates, channels, protocols, tags, outputs, alarms and programs
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03)
Status: accepted; revised by the lead's review of #132 (2026-11-24)

Context: the remaining key tables of configuration schemas contract 0.6 (sections 4 to 9, Annexes A and B) become schema files. Stage 3 checks files before expansion (section 1.4), so a point in a template may still be partial, and some rules of the tables can only be checked once a tree is expanded or merged.
Options: check at stage 3 only what holds before expansion and leave the rest to stage 4 and 5; check more at stage 3 and refuse partial points that expansion would complete.
Decision: stage 3 checks only what holds before expansion and merging (the lead's review of #132, 2026-11-24):
- A protocol's schema composes with the framework's: `<protocol>.schema.json` defines `template` and `channel_file` as an `allOf` of the framework keys (`template_keys`, `channel`, `device`, which do not close their objects) and the protocol's keys, closed by `unevaluatedProperties: false`. A key in neither is CF-201, as Annex C and section 4.1 ask.
- Maps that section 4.5 merges, and that stage 3 therefore never refuses for a key they lack: a point in `defaults` or `overrides`; every map of a point (`address` and its `on`, `off`, `fault` and `write` with `values`, `on`, `off`; `scale`, `exponent_from`, `write_limits`, `counter`, `states`, `bits` and a bit's map, an inline `enum`, `on_invalid`, `logging` and its `tiers`, `device_time`, an `io` `profile`, the raw-type map of `invalid_values`); a template's `read` and `device_time`; a parameter of a template (a child gives only its `default`, `min` or `max`). Each of these takes `replace` (4.5: "a reserved key name in every map of a point"). Their completeness is stage 5, on the merged object (CF-417 for an address). A merged map that lacks a key its shape needs is CF-202 at stage 5, reported at the line of the map's last contributor (the lead's ruling on #132; #136, since 1.4 gives CF-202 to stage 3 only). For example: `exponent_from` without `point` or `direction`, a bit without `name`, a root template's parameter without `type`, an `enum` or `set` parameter without `values`, a `write` with `on` and no `off`, a double point with `on` only. A map in a list (an alarm, a quality rule, an identity check, an invalid-value entry, a range) is replaced with its list, never merged: it takes no `replace` and has its required keys at stage 3.
- Objects that no merge completes, whose required keys stage 3 checks, also those required by the value of another key (CF-202, `rules.h`): the channel (Modbus `device` for `rtu`, `host` for `tcp` and `rtu_over_tcp`; `io` `chip` for `gpio`, `device` for `iio`), a device instance (`name`; Modbus `unit_id`), a tag (`value_type` for `internal` and `manual`, `area` for `manual`, `expr` for `virtual`), an alarm (`limit` for the limit types, `deviation` and `rate_of_change`; `setpoint` for `deviation`; `alarm_on` for `state`; `expr` and `name` for `expression`; `tag` in `alarms/*.yaml` except for `expression`), an output and an interlock, a program and its blocks, an identity check (a target and a comparison), a `repeat` block (`for` with one or two variables, CF-204), and a template's `template`.
- Keys of one kind of an object are refused on another kind (CF-201), as their tables say: the serial keys of a Modbus channel on `tcp` and `rtu_over_tcp`, its TCP keys on `rtu`, `lost_after_timeouts` on any type but `tcp` and on every `io` channel (Annex A.1, section 4.6); a tag's `task`, `expr`, `quality`, `limits` and `initial` on the types section 5.1 does not list for them; `duration` on an output other than `pulse` (on a `pulse` it is CF-720, at stage 5); `params`, `disable` and `only` on an instance without `template` (4.5). A reserved key is CF-210 whatever the kind. These rules apply only to a kind the contract lists: a misspelt `type` or `kind` gets the schema's CF-205 and nothing else.
- A point's `address` has the protocol's keys and types, none of them required. A double point's `on`, `off` and `fault` take `table`, `register` and `bit` (A.2: "two bits, three bits, two coils"). A literal number where a key also takes an expression is checked at stage 3, each bound from its sentence:
  - `register`, `first`, `last`: an integer, 0 to 65,536. A.2 gives "0 to 65,535 after the base (CF-603)"; 65,536 is register 65,535 with `address_base: 1`. The bound that depends on the base, which merging may bring, stays CF-603 at stage 5.
  - `bit` of an address: an integer, 0 to 15 (A.2: "`bit` | 0 to 15").
  - The ends of a `field`, and a quality rule's `bit`: integers, 0 to 63 (5.4: "Bit numbers are 0 to 63 within the raw value"), lo at most hi (4.7: "`field: [lo, hi]`"). The keys of `bits`: 0 to 63, the same sentence.
  - `divide_by`: an integer of at least 1, for every value type (4.7: "`divide_by` is a positive integer or an expression giving one").
  - `data_bits`: 7 or 8. A.1 types it "8" and gives 7 to CF-622 ("7 belongs to ASCII, which is reserved (CF-622)"), so 7 passes stage 3 and is refused at stage 5; the stage 3 message for another value names 8 only.
- `invalid_values` in `defaults` may be a map from raw type to a list (section 4.7); its keys are the raw types of the protocol's schema (Modbus: those of A.2). A base template and `io` have no raw types, so every key of that map is CF-201 there. In `overrides` and in a point it is a list only.
- A quality map value that is a list holds exactly one of `good`, `uncertain` and `bad`, and flags from OUT_OF_RANGE, OVERFLOW, BLOCKED and TEST (4.7, "a list of one of these and flags"; D-131's device flags).
- A device path (`dev_path`) is free text: a space is allowed, a control character is not (`is_device_path`).
- Point names may hold `{<variable>}` of a `repeat` block (`pv{n}.voltage`); the full name is checked after expansion (CF-212 on a name made by expansion, section 1.4). An instance's `overrides` name points without variables.
- A template whose `protocol` this version does not know is checked against the framework keys alone, with the object open, so its protocol keys give no error; the unknown protocol is one error at stage 5, at the key.
- An interlock's `name` and an output's `interlocks` entries are tag names (7.3, and the example of 7.2), not identifiers as 2.6 lists them (#136, C-1).
- Values whose type depends on another key (`alarm_on`, `equals`, `initial`, a parameter's `default`, a profile's parameters) are typed as text here and checked against that key later.
- Ranges and required keys not stated in a key's own row come from these sentences: durations of no stated range are at least 1 ms (2.2, D-107); `unit` is at most 32 bytes (2.1); `priorities` holds 1 to 8 (8); a program's `program` and a template's `template` "equal the file name" (9.2, 4.2), so are required; a poll group's `period` has no default for a group the table does not name (4.6). A key whose table gives no bound has none: `max_per_day` is a `uint64`; `deadband`, `functions`, `classes`, `parts`, `fields`, `in`, `good_when` and `limits.ranges` take any count or value of their type.
- CF-517 at stage 3: where one point object gives an explicit integer `value_type` with `exponent_from`, a decimal `gain` or `offset`, or a `divide_by` above 1. A value type that is derived (from `enum`, `kind: double_point` or an integer raw type) with such a scaling, and keys that merging brings together, are CF-517 at stage 5, on the merged point; so is a `divide_by` written as an expression, after expansion.
- CF-504 (D-134) is a stage 5 check, on the merged point (the lead's ruling, replacing "within one point object"): an `enum` or a `kind: double_point` of a base template, a `defaults` or an override can make a point valid that one object alone would refuse. It is reported at the line of `value_type` or `counter` in the file that wrote it. The corpus case `s3-integer-on-floating-raw` moves to the merging slice as its expected result.
- Checked later, not by the schemas, each by its own code: `data_bits: 7` (CF-622), `unit_id` 1 to 247 on RTU, and `max_gap` with `gap_policy: illegal` (CF-208 on the merged read rules); the keys of `write.values` against the target's type; `duration` on a `pulse` output (CF-720); `retain_now` on a virtual tag (CF-509); `logging` settings without `policy` that lack `mode` or `raw_retention` (CF-802); the parameters and pins of a block, from the block library.
Reason: a stage 3 error must be one that no expansion or merge can remove, and every refusal at stage 3 that the contract allows must happen there, because section 1.4 gives the CF-2xx codes to stage 3 only.
Consequences: points of the contract that this decision reads one way until the next revision are in #136 (C-1 to C-8, C-11 to C-14). The corpus has a valid tree and a refusal case for templates, channels, tags, outputs, alarms and programs, and six device trees (`s3-valid-*`). The expanded-tree rules of slices 6 and 7, and #103, give the remaining codes.

## D-109: The expression parser, and what stage 3 checks of an expression
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03)
Status: accepted

Context: configuration schemas contract 5.6 gives the grammar (D-78) and 1.3 a limit of 4,096 bytes and a depth of 32 for an expression. Stage 3 checks expression strings "as text" (1.4). The names and types of an expression need the expanded tree.
Options: check expressions only in stage 5; check the syntax in stage 3 and the names and types in stage 5.
Decision:
- Stage 3 parses every expression and gives its syntax codes: CF-520, CF-526, CF-528, and CF-529, because the function table is fixed.
- The key decides the variant of the grammar:
  - run-time for `expr`, `suppress_when` and an interlock's `condition`;
  - configuration (strings, `IN`, ranges) for `when` and for the expression form of a number (addresses, scaling);
  - the `for_value` form for a `repeat` range.
  This takes two new `x-scada-type` names, `config_expression` and `for_value`.
- Names and types (CF-521 to CF-525, CF-527) are checked in stage 5, against the tags of the expanded tree.
- Where an error is (the lead's review of #133, S-1). The parser marks a scalar `verbatim` when its text is its source characters on one line: a plain or quoted scalar with no escape, folded break or doubled quote (`Document::verbatim`, one bit per node). For such a scalar the error is at the offending character in the file, its column counted in characters (contract 1.1). For any other scalar (a block scalar, an escaped or folded one) the file cannot give the character's place, so the error is at the scalar and the message names the line and column within the expression's text ("at line 2, column 5 of the expression's text"). CF-520's message gives the column within the expression, in characters.
- A parameter or a `repeat` variable named like a reserved word (any case) is CF-212 at its name (5.6: "a parameter or variable with such a name is refused"); `bad` would otherwise read as the literal BAD, 2.
- A tab is not white space (5.6: "Spaces and line ends separate tokens"): CF-520, with a message that names it.
- Messages give the right spelling of a common mistake: `!=` (`<>`), `&&`, `||`, `%`, `!`, `==`; a `.` that follows a name in a run-time expression (`tag("...")`; not after a number such as `1.` or `1.5.2`, and not in a configuration expression, which has no tags); a reserved word used as a name; an operator word used as a function (`mod(a, 2)`: MOD is an operator, written `a MOD b`); a pattern string where a value is meant; a range in parentheses. A character beyond ASCII is shown whole, and one that shows as a space or as nothing (a no-break space, a zero-width space, a byte-order mark) as its code point, `U+00A0` (the lead's final pass on #133).
- A parameter or variable name that is not an identifier (`Bad`) has the schema's CF-212 only, not a second one for the reserved word.
- The depth that CF-526 limits is the nesting of the parser: the expression counts as level 1, and a parenthesis, a call's arguments, a unary operator's operand and the right side of `**` one more each. So 31 nested parentheses, 31 nested calls (a ladder of `if(c, a, if(...))` stops at 31 levels) 15 of `-( ... )` or a chain of 31 `**` (`a ** a ** ...`, right-associative) are the most; 4,096 bytes are accepted and 4,097 refused (CF-526). A flat chain such as `a + b + c` does not deepen it, so 4,096 bytes of a flat chain are accepted, and every recursion of the parser is bounded by 32.
- Stage 3 keeps no tree: it parses each expression for its syntax and drops the result; stage 5 parses it again to check names and types. A parse is linear in the text, at most 4,096 bytes, so parsing twice costs less than keeping a tree per expression of the whole tree in memory.
- Each node of the tree keeps its extent in the expression's text (`begin`, `end`, byte offsets), so stage 5 can point at a whole operand, not only at its operator.
- A string or a pattern may hold any character, control characters included (a string writes its quote doubled); stage 3 does not refuse them. What a pattern may hold is a stage 5 check (CF-311).
- Not decided by the contract, and on #136: whether `identify`'s `equals` and `in` hold a text or a configuration expression (4.8: "The values may be configuration expressions"); they stay typed as text here, as D-108 has them, and are not parsed. A call with the wrong number of arguments, a pattern string where a value is expected (`abs("x")`), `tag(x)`, a pattern with a wildcard in `tag`, and an empty pattern pass stage 3: their codes (CF-523, CF-311) belong to stage 5, which knows each function's arguments. An expression's text that YAML cuts short (a plain scalar followed by ` #`) cannot be seen once parsed; quoting advice is on #136.
Reason: a syntax error is known from the text alone and belongs to the file's line. Types need the tags, which only the expanded tree has.
- Messages quote the expression's text with `quote_text` and reach the report through `Diagnostics::add` (D-110), like every other message of stage 3. The size limit is checked before the text is lexed, and the depth before each descent, so neither costs more than the work it bounds.
Consequences: the parser is a fuzz target with the cases of conformance test 11 as its seeds, and an oracle: the grammar written a second time in the fuzz target, by precedence climbing over a table of the binary levels, with its own lexer, which must agree with the parser on whether a text is accepted, on a refusal's code and column, and for an accepted text on the tree: its shape, operators, literal values and names (the lead's review of #133). The pipeline's `fuzz` job runs it, like every fuzz target, for `SCADA_FUZZ_SECONDS` (60 s). Stage 5 (slice 6 of #100) adds the type checker over the same tree.

## D-110: Text of the tree in diagnostics is escaped and capped
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03)
Status: accepted (the lead's review of #130, 2026-11-23)

Context: messages quote keys, values and paths of the tree (contract section 10). The tree is outside input (an imported archive, reference 12.7). Text with a line break can forge a second diagnostic line; a terminal escape or a character that reorders text (U+202E) can change what an operator sees. A 64 KiB scalar would make a 64 KiB message.
Options: escape when a report is printed; escape when a diagnostic is collected.
Decision: `Diagnostics::add`, which every diagnostic passes through from stage 1 on, makes its message, paths, note kinds and details printable with one function, `printable`:
- line feed, carriage return and tab as `\n`, `\r`, `\t`; other C0 control characters, DEL and bytes that are not valid UTF-8 as `\xNN`;
- as `\uNNNN`, or `\UNNNNNNNN` above U+FFFF: the C1 control characters; the invisible and bidirectional characters U+00AD, U+061C, U+180E, U+200B to U+200F, U+2028 to U+202E, U+2060 to U+2064, U+2066 to U+2069, U+FEFF and U+FFF9 to U+FFFB; the non-characters U+FDD0 to U+FDEF and U+nFFFE and U+nFFFF of every plane; the tag characters U+E0000 to U+E007F;
- a backslash is kept, so that `printable` leaves text it has made printable as it is. A key or value that a message quotes goes through `quote_text`, which writes it in double quotes with `\` and `"` escaped, so that a quoted piece has an unambiguous end;
- each text is cut at 1,024 bytes, before a whole character or escape, and ends in `...`.
Reason: one place, before any consumer (the command line, the API, the journal), so no consumer can forget it. Keeping the backslash in `printable` is what lets a message hold escaped pieces from `quote_text` without escaping them twice; a backslash cannot drive a terminal.
Consequences: a report holds only printable text; a consumer prints it as it is.

## D-111: The node type: an immutable Document built by a DocumentBuilder
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03)
Status: accepted (the lead's review of #130, ruling 7, 2026-11-23; agreed with #104, whose driver reads the same type)

Context: the parsed tree is read by the stages of validation and, through the loader of the expanded file (contract 13.3), by the drivers (#104). The first node type had a public mutable `Document`, accessors that throw (`.at()`), a linear `find` and 72 bytes per node.
Options: nodes as objects with pointers to their children; nodes in arrays of one Document, read by index.
Decision:
- A `Document` is immutable. Only a `DocumentBuilder` makes one: `add_file`, `scalar`, `key`, `begin_map` / `end_map`, `begin_sequence` / `end_sequence`, `finish`. Misuse and a duplicate key are returned as a status, not thrown.
- Nodes are read by `NodeId`: `kind`, `style`, `text`, `position` (file index, line, column) and `location` (with the file's name); a map's `entries` and a sequence's `items` are spans. No accessor throws; an id of another document ends the process (a programmer error), and a moved-from document must not be read.
- The type a schema gives a scalar is not in the node: stage 3 keeps it in a table beside the document, indexed by `NodeId`, 1 byte per node (the lead, 2026-11-24), so the document stays immutable.
- `find` is a binary search over each map's keys, sorted once by the builder: O(log n), for consumers that look keys up per point.
- A node is 20 bytes, a map entry 12 more, a sequence item 4 more, plus the text. At the tree's node limit (4,000,000) the structure is at most 104 MB.
- Reading a const `Document` from several threads is safe: there are no lazy caches.
Reason: index access keeps every node in a few arrays, which is small, needs no allocation per node, and makes the document cheap to move. A builder is the one place that enforces the invariants (a key is a scalar, keys are unique, collections are closed), for the parser and the loader alike.
Consequences: one bit per node records whether a scalar's text is its source on one line (`Document::verbatim`, D-109), so that an expression error can point at its character; the builder's `scalar` takes it as an optional last argument, so the loader of #104 is unchanged. The table of types (`ScalarTypes`, `types.h`) and the typed reads of a node (`read.h`) come with stage 3 (slice 3 of #100). A typed read gives INVALID_ARGUMENT for a node of the wrong kind or form (a quoted scalar is never a number or a boolean, contract 1.2) and OUT_OF_RANGE for a value that does not fit; `to_raw_pattern` takes the register's width, 1 to 64 bits, and gives a negative value as its two's complement in that width (section 2.3). Each file's bytes are freed once it has parsed; the README states the measured peak of a tree at every limit at once.

## D-112: The pipeline runs when a pull request's base changes or it leaves draft
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #138
Status: accepted (the lead, 2026-11-24)

Context: `pipeline.yml` listened to the default `pull_request` types (`opened`, `synchronize`, `reopened`). PR 133 was force-pushed while its base was a branch it no longer merged into; GitHub made no merge ref, so that push ran nothing, and the change of base to `main` that followed is an `edited` event, which ran nothing either. Marking a draft ready (`ready_for_review`) also ran nothing. The lead merges only on a `pull_request` run of the final head, which tests the merge with the base and gives the linter of D-67 its base.
Options: add `edited` and `ready_for_review` and run every job on them; add them and run only when the base changed; leave the types and rely on a later push.
Decision: add `ready_for_review` and `edited` to the types. On `edited`, the jobs run only when the base changed (`github.event.changes.base` is set). The test is one expression, `github.event.action == 'edited' && github.event.changes.base == null`, written the same in `decide` and in the concurrency group. `on:` cannot filter on that, so an edit of only the title or the description still makes a run, whose jobs are skipped. The condition is decided in one place, a first job `events`, whose outputs `run` and `prefix` every other job reads (`needs: events`); the workflow's concurrency group, which cannot read a job, states the same test once more. Two rules keep a run that tests nothing harmless (the lead's review on #138):
- it has a concurrency group of its own (`...-edit`), so it never cancels a real run of the same pull request;
- its jobs' names come from an expression (`${{ needs.events.outputs.prefix }}build and test (...)`), so its skipped checks never take the place of the real checks of the same commit: GitHub reads the latest check run of a name, and a skipped required check counts as passed. GitHub does not evaluate the name of a job its `if` skips, so such a check shows the template, not a real name. This is observed on the probe (#139), not documented. `prefix` (`(not run) `) is the fallback should GitHub start to evaluate a skipped job's name with the `needs` context: the names would then read `(not run) check` and so on. One case stays unsafe: should GitHub evaluate the names with an empty context, the skipped checks would take the real names, and a title edit could turn a red pull request green.
`check` also fails if `events` did not succeed, so a failure of the decision cannot pass as a skipped run.
Limits (the lead's review of #140):
- Because the name behaviour is observed, probe test 5 (a title edit after a red run leaves the real checks red) must be repeated when the checks become required on `main`.
- `events` must not be a required check: it succeeds in a run that tests nothing. The seven checks meant to be required are those of the README's table.
- New commits on the base branch send no event to the pull request, so they start no run; the next push does.
- A title and a base changed together are two `edited` events, one per field: the base event runs every job, and the title event runs none and cancels nothing (probe test 6 on #139: one request changed both; run 45443341858 was the full run, run 45443343088 skipped every job, and the earlier run 45443328296 was cancelled by the base run as any real run of the same group is).
- When a base branch is deleted, GitHub retargets its pull requests itself. Whether that event carries `changes.base` is not verified; if it does not, the result is no run, not a false success.
- The robust shape, a reusable workflow called by two small ones, is a separate issue: it renames every check, so it comes before required checks are set.
Reason: a change of base changes what the pull request merges, and a draft made ready is the point where reviewers rely on the run; both must be tested as a push is. A title edit must not re-run the pipeline, cancel a run, or turn a red pull request green.
Consequences: a title edit shows a few skipped checks named by their templates (`${{ needs.events.outputs.prefix }}...`) on the pull request; a red pull request stays red, because its real checks keep their names (probe test 5). The probe pull request that tested this is named in its pull request for the owner to delete.

## D-113: What stage 5 checks before the expander exists
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03), slice 6
Status: accepted, with the rulings of the lead's review of #144 (B1 to B6, S1 to S5)

Context: stage 5 checks names, references and cross-file rules "of the expanded tree" (contract 1.4). The expander is #103 and comes after this slice (the lead, 2026-11-24, on #100 and #103). Point tags (`<device>.<point>`), names that `repeat` makes, and the bindings of `repeat` variables exist only once instances are expanded.
Options: wait for the expander; check now only what expansion cannot change, and leave every tag reference unchecked.
Decision: stage 5 runs on the files as stage 3 read them, and checks only what no expansion can change:
- **Names that must exist** (CF-310, `{kind} {name} does not exist`):
  - `template` of a device and `extends` of a template;
  - a template's `protocol`, at its key (D-108);
  - areas, logging policies (`logging`, `policy`, `default_policy`) and aggregate tiers (`tiers` keys; `1m` and `1h` when `logging.yaml` gives no `aggregate_tiers`);
  - enumerations of a tag (in `enums.yaml`) and of an instance's points (in its template's chain, then `enums.yaml`). A template's own points are not checked: a child of the chain may define the table (4.5, step 3.1; B5);
  - tasks (`fast`, `normal` and `slow` when there is no `control/tasks.yaml`, 9.1; a file replaces them all; B1), programs (`tasks.yaml`), interlocks (`interlocks` of an output, also `on` and `off`), outputs (`output` of a block);
  - alarm priorities and classes, with the defaults of section 8 when no file gives the list, and the keys of `max_shelve`.
- **Names given twice:**
  - a channel (also in two protocols' directories), a device (across channels) and an interlock: CF-303, "first at file:line";
  - the tags of tag files and interlocks: CF-301 for a name whose first segment is `sys` (`sys` itself too, not `system.a`; 2.1), CF-302 when given twice;
  - a template, program, area, policy, task or enumeration cannot be given twice: file names and map keys are unique already.
- **The Modbus channel rules of Annex A.1 (CF-622):**
  - `data_bits` of 7 on `rtu`, by its value (`7`, `0x7`; B3);
  - a `host` that is a loopback (127.0.0.0/8, `::1`), multicast (224.0.0.0/4, `ff00::/8`) or the broadcast address (255.255.255.255). The host is classified by its bytes, however it is written (B4); an IPv4-mapped IPv6 address (`::ffff:a.b.c.d`) is classified by its IPv4 address. A subnet's broadcast address cannot be known from the address alone.
- **The configuration expressions of a template**, by the tables of 5.6:
  - `when` must give BOOL;
  - an expression under an address or a scaling must give a number;
  - a `for` value must give a range or an INT, over parameters only;
  - the expressions of an identity check (`address`, `range`; 2.4, 4.8), of a template or of a device without template, must give a number. `equals` and `in` stay text (D-108).

  Parameters are typed by the nearest declaration of the chain that gives a `type`: `int` INT, `number` REAL, `bool` BOOL, `string` and `enum` STRING, `set` SET. A `repeat` variable is INT.
- **Names in a template's expressions (B5).** A template is checked only through its own chain upward. A name that no parameter or `repeat` variable of that chain has may come from a child of the chain (contract 4.5 resolves names over the instance's chain; 4.1 describes a base with the signals and a child per protocol). Such a name gives no error here, and its expression is not typed. The names are checked at each instance, over the instance's chain, by the expander (#103). A chain cut by a missing template, a cycle or its length (more than 5 files) is not known: its names and types are not checked here (S3), and the missing template or CF-401 is the error.
- **Codes (B6).** Contract 2.4: "An expression that refers to an unknown name, has the wrong type, divides by zero or overflows is CF-410". So a failure of a configuration expression is CF-410, in the form of 10.4: `expression {expr}: {reason}`, at the offending character (D-109). This covers:
  - a type that does not fit;
  - a wrong number of arguments;
  - a function that reads tags (aggregates, `quality`, `age`, `is_good`, the windowed functions, `tag`);
  - a `when` that gives no BOOL;
  - an address or scaling expression that gives no number;
  - a name in a device without template, where nothing can define one.

  CF-523 stays where 5.6 names it for a configuration expression: a `for` value whose result is not a range or an INT (`expression: the value must be a range or an INT, not ...`). An operand of the range that is not an INT (`1..options`) is a type error of the range operator, CF-410. CF-310 is not used for names in expressions. The disagreement between 2.4 and 5.6 is on #136.
- **Run-time expressions in a point (B2).** An alarm's `expr`, `condition` and `suppress_when` read tags, wherever the alarm stands: on a point, a bit of a point or a tag. One function (`holds_run_time_expressions`) skips every `alarms` key, so these are never typed as configuration expressions.
- **Equality (S1).** Two SETs are two values of one type (5.6), so `=` and `<>` accept them. A range is not a value, only a part of `IN` and of a `for` value, so `=` refuses it (on #136).
- `round`, `trunc`, `floor` and `ceil` take REAL, as the function table says; `round(3)` is CF-410.
- **Instances.** The expressions of an instance of a template (its `defaults`, `overrides` and points) are typed by the expander, which knows the variables bound to each point.
- **Cost (S4).** The scope of each template (its chain's enumerations, parameters and variables) is built once, and its names are kept in sets. Measured in the release build: a template of 32,000 `repeat` blocks, 0.27 s; 65,536 devices of a template with 3,000 parameters, 4.6 s for the whole validation.
What waits, and where:
- **#103 (the expander):**
  - the names of a template's expressions and enumerations over the instance's chain, at the instance with a "from template" note;
  - CF-401 (a cycle, more than 4 parents);
  - CF-415 (a parameter's type changed);
  - the checks of a merged point (CF-504, CF-516, CF-517 at stage 5, CF-202 of an incomplete merged map).
- **Slice 7 of #100:**
  - every reference to a tag: outputs, feedback, `local_panel.input`, alarms (`tag`, `limit`, `setpoint`), block pins, run-time expressions;
  - CF-301 for point tags, CF-302 against point tags;
  - point references within a device: companions (`exponent_from`, `quality_from`, `source_time_from`), health and identity points;
  - poll group references (CF-604);
  - output names given twice;
  - alarm ids given twice on one tag (CF-812);
  - `priorities`, `max_shelve` or `classes` in two files (CF-811);
  - CF-311, CF-312, and the names and types of run-time expressions (CF-521 to CF-525, CF-527).
- The default task of an interlock and of a virtual tag (`normal`) is a name of the defaults above; whether it exists needs nothing more.
Reason: an error now must stay an error once the expander exists, and a name that only expansion, or a child of the chain, makes must not be refused before it can exist. Every check above reads names that neither does.
Consequences:
- A tree whose tag references are wrong passes stage 5 until slice 7. A unit test (`AReferenceToATagGivesNoErrorYet`) pins that, so slice 7 changes it on purpose.
- A template that no instance uses is never checked for names: open point O1 on #136.
- Corpus cases:
  - `s3-unknown-protocol` is now `s5-unknown-protocol`, with its CF-310;
  - `s3-program-of-section-9-2` is now `s5-program-of-section-9-2`, whose output is not in its tree (its task `normal` is a default task).

## D-114: The expander of templates and instances (stage 4)
Date: 2026-11-24; revised 2026-11-25 for the review of #146
Phase / issue: 2, #103 (P2-06)
Status: proposed

Context: contract 4.4 and 4.5 give the merge order and the merge rules of an instance; 1.4 puts the expansion in stage 4 and says where its errors are; 13.3 says how a computed value is written. Several details are left open. The review of #146 found eleven faults and ruled on nine open points (O-1 to O-9, all on #136).
Options: per detail below; the simplest that fits the contract was chosen, or the lead's ruling where one was given.
Decision:
- **One tree type.** The result is one `Document` (D-111) of the expanded channels, with its `ScalarTypes`, built by `expand_tree` (`expand.h`):
  - `channels`, each with `protocol`, `file`, the `channel` object as written, and its `devices`;
  - each device holds its own keys and the template's device keys (`read`, `enums`, `bit_order`, `functions`, `device_time`, `keepalive`, `idle_timeout`, `on_mismatch`, `identify`, `health`, `restart_indication`) under them, and its `points`.

  The keys that steer expansion (`template`, `params`, `defaults`, `overrides`, `disable`, `only`), `when` and every `replace` are gone. A scalar keeps its text, style, position and type. A computed value has the canonical text of 13.3 (an INT in decimal, a REAL as the shortest decimal with a point, a BOOL as `true` or `false`, a TIME as milliseconds and `ms`) and the position of its expression. For each node, `instance_of` gives the device it belongs to, for diagnostics of later stages. `validate_and_expand` gives the report and this tree; #102 writes the expanded files from it.
- **Where an error is (1.4).**
  - An error whose site is in the instance's own file is there.
  - One whose site is in a template is at the instance's `name` key, with a note `from template <file>:<line>:<column> (<detail>)`. The detail names the point, the parameter or `identify`; for CF-410 it adds each name the expression used with its value, as `(point s3; n = 3)` (S2 of the review of #146).
  - The same diagnostic is reported once. One cause in a template met by several instances (one code, one template position, one message) is one error, at the first instance, with ` (and N more instances: <names>)` after its message, naming the first 10 others (the ruling O-6; S7 of the delta review). The note's detail gives the values of the instance the error is at. Points of one instance that meet the cause count that instance once.
- **A template's own faults (O-6)** are found once, at the template, before any instance and whether or not an instance uses it:
  - its chain: a cycle (CF-401 at the `extends` that closes it, for each template of the cycle), more than 4 parents (CF-401 at its own `extends`), a base and a protocol template mixed (CF-421);
  - its parameters along the chain: a changed type (CF-415), a declaration that no level types (CF-202, `parameter "<name>" needs type`);
  - two written points of one name in it (CF-416);
  - a base template's `address` (of a point, a `repeat` block's point or `defaults`), `read` or `identify` (CF-421, `template "<name>": a base template has no <key>`; B11);
  - `when` in its `defaults` (CF-419).

  An instance of a template with such a fault, or with one in its chain, is not expanded: its errors would repeat the template's. What depends on parameters stays per instance.
- **What stage 4 leaves to stage 5.** A device whose template, or a template of its chain, the tree does not have, or whose template's protocol this version does not know, is left out without an error: the missing name is CF-310 of stage 5 (D-113). So stage 5 runs, and gives it.
- **Parameters.**
  - An instance's parameter that the chain does not declare is CF-412 (`the template has no parameter of this name`); the contract has no other code for it.
  - A `number` parameter keeps its written form: written as an integer it is an INT, with a point or an exponent a REAL (the ruling O-9). Stage 5 does not type it at the template, whatever its default, as each instance may write either form (B12 of the delta review of #146); the expander checks each instance's value, an integer key taking an INT.
  - A child's declaration may give `default`, `min`, `max` and `values` without `type`; giving another `type` is CF-415 at it.
  - `min` and `max` are compared exactly: an INT against an integer bound as integers (T3), and against a decimal bound, read as its nearest binary64, without converting the INT (D7 of the delta review); a REAL as binary64.
  - A `repeat` variable with the name of a parameter of the chain is refused at the instance: CF-412, `parameter "<name>": a repeat variable cannot have its name`, at the variable (the ruling O-3). Through step 3.3 a child's variable would otherwise change a parent's expression that meant the parameter. CF-412 is the code whose message names a parameter.
- **Step 4 (O-1, O-5; B2 to B4).** A point exists when its own `when` and the `when` of each `repeat` block that made it hold. Each block's `when` is carried on the points it makes and decided at step 4 with the point's variables, so a child that refines a point of an absent option does not bring it back, and `disable` matches it whatever the option. Then the instance's `disable` or `only`. Without a template, steps 1 to 3 are empty and the instance's points enter step 4, so their `when`, the instance's `defaults` and `overrides` apply. With a template, the instance's own points are added after step 6 (no default reaches them) and their `when` is decided with the parameters, before CF-416, so an absent one collides with nothing (S10). Without a template the instance's points are a level, as a template's points are, so two of one name are CF-416 at the merge, even when one has `when: false`, as in a template (E5 of the final pass). The points of a block whose `when` is false do not count toward CF-414, which counts the points that exist after step 4; the work of making them counts toward the node limit and the `repeat` budget (C2).
- **`when` in `defaults` or `overrides`** is CF-419 (`when cannot be given in defaults`, `... in overrides`; 4.4, B10), at the template (O-6) or the instance.
- **Evaluation.** An expression is evaluated with the parameters and the variables bound to the point, over the instance's whole chain (B5 of the review of #144).
  - Its types are checked first, with the type checker of stage 5 (D-113).
  - Every failure is CF-410, in the contract's form `expression {expr}: {reason}` (2.4, 10.4; B6), at the offending character (D-109). This covers a type error, a `when` that gives no BOOL, and a name that is neither a parameter nor a variable bound to this point (`... is not a parameter and no variable bound to this point`).
  - CF-523 stays only for a `for` value that gives no range or INT (5.6).
  - Every expression of a point's keys and of `identify` is a number (2.4), as stage 5 types a template's (D-113): `the value must be a number, not {type}`. A key the schema types as an integer (`register`, `bit`, `divide_by`) takes an INT: `the value must be an INT, not REAL` (B9). Stage 3 marks such an expression `INTEGER_EXPRESSION`, and stage 5 types it the same way at the template, a `number` parameter apart. The range of a computed value (`register: "70000"`, `divide_by` 0) is the merged-point checks' (the second pull request of #103).
  - `identify` of the device, the template's with the instance's appended, is evaluated with the parameters and no variable (B8).
  - `AND` and `OR` evaluate both sides (the ruling O-4): `false AND 1 DIV k = 0` with k = 0 is CF-410.
  - The evaluator (the lead's rulings on the C++ review of #146, all on #136):
    - two SETs compare as sets: `=` holds when they hold the same strings, whatever their order or repeats (C3);
    - `/` of two INTs, an INT with a REAL, and a TIME times or divided by a number are binary64 operations on the operands converted to binary64, so an INT above 2^53 may round;
