    - `shl` drops the bits it shifts out, with no error; a shift or a bit number outside 0 to 63 is CF-410;
    - `limit(lo, x, hi)` with `lo` above `hi` is CF-410 (`limit needs lo at most hi`), not a silent `hi`;
    - `round` and the other rounding functions take REAL only, as the function table says.
  - Alarms hold run-time expressions and are not evaluated here, wherever they stand: on a point or on a bit (B2 of the review of #144).
  - In a block scalar or an escaped string, which has no place in the file for a character, the message adds the place within the expression's text, as for CF-520 (D-109).
- **`repeat`.** A `for` value is evaluated with the parameters only; a second variable's range does not see the first (the ruling O-7). Its values are counted from the bounds before any is made, each run's span refused before it is added so that the count cannot wrap (B1): CF-113, `the range of n exceeds its limit of 65536`, and for two variables `the values of a repeat block exceed ...`.
- **CF-414** counts the points of the instance that exist after step 4 (C2 of the review of #146), with the contract's message `template "<name>" expands to <n> points, the limit is 65536`.
- **The limits of the expanded tree (C2, the lead's ruling).** The limits of a tree (1.3) hold for the expanded tree in stage 4: 4,000,000 nodes and 256 MiB of text (CF-113, `the expanded tree exceeds its limit of 4000000 nodes`, `... of 256 MiB of text`), and `limits.max_tags` (CF-1001, `<n> tags, the limit is <limit>`; a point's tag unless `tag: false`, and each bit's). The nodes and the tags are checked on a running total after every device, so before the next is expanded, and the text after every point the writer adds (D4 of the delta review); passing one stops the expansion, and the device's later steps are not done. What an instance makes before step 4, the defaults merged under its points before step 5 (their nodes times the points, D1) and its overrides count toward the node limit with what is written, so no instance holds more than the tree may. Each device is written into the document as soon as it is finished and then dropped; after an error nothing more is written, as no tree is given. Past 2,000 kept diagnostics an error is counted, not kept. A cause already met is looked up before any text is kept, so the texts that suppress repeated errors and the causes are at most 2,000 too (D2). `ExpandLimits` (expand.h) holds these limits; a test may give smaller ones.
- **The work of expansion: two budgets (the lead's ruling S1; D3 of the delta review; E1, E3 and G1 of the final pass).** Past either, CF-113 and the expansion stops; past it nothing more is counted, so one stop is one error. The contract states neither (on #136).
  - **Points and values.** Every point made, written or generated, kept or not, and every value of a `repeat` that is evaluated count one, against a budget of 4,000,000, the node limit (`the points and repeat values made for the tree exceed their limit of 4000000`). So instances times template points is limited to 4,000,000, whatever `only`, `disable` or `when` keep: each point is made before step 4 removes it, and the work is real (E3, the lead's ruling). 300 instances of a 15,000-point template with `only` of 10 points are refused, although they give 3,000 tags.
  - **Expressions (G1).** The nodes of every evaluation, and of every parse and typing, count against a budget of their own, 128,000,000, 32 times the node limit (`the expression nodes evaluated for the tree exceed their limit of 128000000`). A reference to a parameter adds the weight of the value it copies (a SET's items, and a unit per 64 bytes of its text). Charged against the points budget instead (E1 as first written), an expression node cost a point's share, and a valid tree was refused: 300 devices of a 1,000-point `repeat` with the contract's computed `register` (17 nodes) were refused at the 200th device with 200,000 points made; it is accepted in 1.1 s.
  - **Why 32.** A comparison node takes about 28 ns, but an addition, which builds a value, and an uncached parse take about 96 ns a node, so 32 times the node limit bounds the evaluation of the worst accepted tree at about 13 s; 64 gave 25 s. Measured, release build, x86-64, the whole of `validate_and_expand`: the 729 s tree (a `when` of 400 terms over 65,536 values, 30 instances) is refused in 5.2 s; one 4,096-byte `when` of additions over 65,536 values in 30 instances in 12.8 s; 120 distinct 4,096-byte expressions past the parse cache, 400 instances, in 11.0 s; the G1 tree is accepted in 1.1 s. Headroom: a site of 1,000,000 points with three expressions of 17 nodes each uses about 51,000,000 of the budget.
- **Each expression parsed once (E1 of the final pass).** An expression of the source is parsed once for all its evaluations and keeps its tree; it is typed again only when a name it reads changes type (a `number` parameter is INT at one instance and REAL at another, O-9). Evaluation walks the tree only. The parsed trees kept are at most 250,000 nodes (about 33 MB); past that the cache is emptied, and a later evaluation parses again, at its cost in the budget. `ExpandCounts::parses` and `typings` count them.
- **Names made by expansion (step 8, B7)** follow 2.1: a point or bit name that does not is CF-212 `"<name>" is not a valid point name`, and where a `{...}` is left, `: {x} names no variable bound to this point`. Its length is CF-402 or CF-403.
- **The merge (4.5).**
  - Maps are merged key by key. Two keys that stage 3 typed as integers by the schema's key type (`bits`, enumerations, a quality rule's `map`, `states`, a BOOL point's `values`; 1.2) are one key by their value (`15` and `0xF`); the child's spelling and value win (B6). Keys typed as strings (`write.values`) are compared as text.
  - `replace: true` on a point's map replaces it whole, the defaults under it too, and the map stays so through later levels until the end (the ruling O-2).
  - A template's `disable` matches any point so far, also of its own level (the ruling O-8).
  - A child's `enums` table replaces the inherited table of the same name whole (4.5, step 3.1).
- **Exclusive groups (4.5)** are keyed by the map they are in:
  - `scale`: `gain` and `divide_by`;
  - `address`: `bit`, `field` and the double-point shapes, and `register` and `parts`;
  - `write`: `value`, `values`, `mask`, and `on` with `off` as one member.

  `on_invalid` and `action` change form by replacing a scalar with a map, which the merge does anyway.
- **`invalid_values` of `defaults`** (a map by raw type) gives a point that has no list of its own the list of its raw type, or none. It is picked after step 6, which may change the raw type (B5). The raw type is `address.type`, or the protocol's default: for Modbus, `bool` for one bit, coils and discrete inputs, `uint16` otherwise.
- **`identify` of an instance** is appended to the template's; a level's `identify` replaces the one above; with no template it is the instance's own.
- **Name patterns** of `disable` and `only` follow 2.5: `*` and `?` stay within one segment.
Reason: each choice is the reading of the contract that needs no new key or code, or the lead's ruling. Errors stay where the user can act on them: a template's at the template, an instance's at the instance with the template's line, and one cause once.
Consequences: tag references and the checks of a merged point (CF-504, CF-516, CF-517 at stage 5, CF-202 of an incomplete merged map, the ranges of computed values) come in the next pull request of #103 and in slice 7 of #100, and read `ExpandedTree`. CF-1001 is given here, on the tags of the expanded points; the tag set (#101) counts the tags of the other files too. The README states the bound measured on the worst accepted trees (release build, x86-64, after E1 and E2 of the final pass): a peak of 858 MB (838 MiB), for one instance of 62,000 points each with an alarm `expr` of 4,056 bytes and 36 `invalid_values` (252 MiB of text, 3,844,027 nodes), and a run of 12.8 s, for the worst tree of expressions the expression budget accepts (G1), 10.7 s for 61 instances of a template of 65,536 written points that each keep one. `scada-conf` sizes its memory limit from this (#102). A template that no instance uses is now checked for its own faults, not for its names (D-113).

## D-115: The checks of a merged point (stage 5)
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #103 (P2-06), second part; review of PR 149
Status: accepted

Context: D-108 leaves to stage 5 what only a merged point can show: the keys a merged map still lacks (CF-202), a value type that cannot hold its raw type (CF-504, D-134), the range of an integer scaling (CF-516) and an integer value type with a scaling that is not integer when merging brought the keys together (CF-517). The review of #132 (C5) adds two points on CF-504; the review of PR 149 (M1 to M8, S1 to S6) the rest of this entry.
Options: per check below; the simplest reading of contract 4.7 and Annexes A and B.
Decision: `check_merged_points` reads each point of the expanded tree (D-114), whatever level, default or override gave each key.
- **Where.** An error is placed by `expanded_diagnostic`:
  - a key that a template wrote is at the instance, with a note `from template <file>:<line>:<column> (point <name>)` (1.4), or `(<key>)` for a key of the device itself (`identify`, `read`, `health`, `device_time`);
  - a key the instance's own file wrote (an override, an instance point) is at its own line.

  This replaces D-108's "at the line of `value_type` or `counter` in the file that wrote it", which is now the note. The message names the point by its whole tag, `"<device>.<point>"`.
- **One cause, one error** (D-114's rule, M6): errors of one code at one template position whose messages are the same once the instance's part is taken out (`"<device>.` and `"<device>"`) are one error, at the first instance, ending `(and N more instances: <names>)`. The grouping is the expander's (`GroupedErrors`), shared, not copied.
  - An error is grouped only when nothing at the node it is placed at, or below it, was written in the instance's own file: an override or an instance point makes the error that instance's own (item 1 of the delta review). CF-516 is placed at the factor that makes the range, `gain` or `offset`, the one the instance wrote first; so an instance that overflows through its own override is shown at its override's line.
  - Past the kept limit (2,000 diagnostics), a cause not yet kept is counted once per instance where it is met, not once in all: 6,000 causes at 120 instances report 482,000 errors, of which the 1,000 listed are right. Stage 4 counts the same way. The count is an upper bound of the errors, never below it.
- **Order** (M5): a point's computed values are checked first; a point with a computed value out of its key's rules gets no further check, so a wrong value gives one error, not a second one that follows from it.
- **CF-202, the keys merging must have completed** (M4):
  - a Modbus address: `table` and `register`, unless the point is a double point given by its shapes: then `on` and `off` (and `fault` where given), each with `table` and `register`; `register` also unless `parts`. A write-only point whose `write` has `on` and `off` needs `table` and `register` in each of them, and its address then needs neither (A.2, A.5). A.2 excepts only `register` there; that the address needs no `table` either is on #136;
  - by the raw type: `length` for `string` and `bytes`, `count` for `mod10k`, `width` and `resolution` for `epoch`, `fields` for `datetime`;
  - an `io` address, by its channel's backend (B.2): `line` and `direction` for `gpio`, `at_start` as well for an output (`direction: output`, or `access` `rw` or `wo`), `channel` for `iio`, `profile` with its `type` for `simulator`;
  - `exponent_from`: `point` and `direction`;
  - a bit given as a map: `name`;
  - `on_invalid` given as a map: `substitute`;
  - a `device_time` (device, point address) with `zone: local`: `tz`;
  - a parameter that no level of its chain gives a `type` (stage 4, as the declarations are merged).

  The message names the map: `address`, `address.on`, `write.on`, `bit 4`. A device's own key is named in the note: `(device_time)`.
- **The value type** is the given one, or the derived one of 4.7: `bool` for a bit or a `bool` raw type; `int64` for an enumeration or a double point; `float64` for a floating raw type, `exponent_from`, a `divide_by` other than 1, or a `gain` or `offset` that is not an integer; `string` for `string` and `bytes`; otherwise `int64`, and `uint64` for a `uint64` raw type. A Modbus raw type without `type` is `bool` for coils and discrete inputs and `uint16` otherwise. A derived type is named so in a message: `value_type int64 (derived from the enumeration)`.
- **Numbers in any form** (M1): whether a `gain`, `offset` or `divide_by` is an integer, and whether a `divide_by` is 1, is decided by parsing the scalar as stage 3 does (`is_integer_one` in `rules.h`, one helper for both stages), so `0x1` is 1.
- **Which protocol** (M2): CF-517 and the value type apply to the merged point of every protocol (CS 4.7 and 1.4 name none). The checks that need the raw type (CF-504, CF-516) read the Modbus address, where the raw type is written; the raw type of an `io` point comes from its backend, not its address, and is not checked here.
- **CF-504** (S1), exactly the pairs that CS 4.7 decides:
  - a string raw type (`string`, `bytes`) is held by `string` only, and `string` holds nothing else;
  - `bool` holds a `bool` raw type only (a bit, a coil, a discrete input);
  - `int64` and `uint64` do not hold `float32` or `float64`, unless the point is an enumeration or a double point, which read a code (D-134);
  - a counter (`counter`, or `kind: counter`) never holds a floating raw type, even with an enumeration (C5), with the message `<tag>: a counter cannot hold <raw type>`, which has no row in the contract's table (on #136);
  - without a scaling, `int64` does not hold `uint64`, nor `uint64` a signed raw type.

  Not checked, because CS does not decide them (on #136): `epoch`, `datetime` and `mod10k` with each value type, `float64` on a 64-bit integer, an enumeration or a double point on `uint64`, and an enumeration or a double point with `value_type` `float64`, `bool` or `uint64`.
- **CF-516.** With an integer scaling (no `exponent_from`, an integer `gain` and `offset`, `divide_by` absent or 1), each end of the raw range, times `gain`, plus `offset`, must fit the value type. It is computed exactly in 64-bit sign and magnitude, with no wider type (no compiler extensions). The raw range is that of the raw type, of its bit `field` (sign-extended for a signed raw type), or for `mod10k` 0 to 10^(4 x count) - 1. Stage 3 bounds `count` to 2 to 4 (A.2); the loop is bounded to 4 here too, so its top is below 10^16 whatever reaches it.
- **CF-517** at stage 5: an integer value type, given or derived from an enumeration or a double point, with `exponent_from`, a `gain` or `offset` that is not an integer, or a `divide_by` other than 1, after expansion; so a `divide_by` written as an expression counts with its value. Where one point object holds both keys, stage 3 refused it already, and stage 5 does not run.
- **Computed values (the lead's ruling on #146: the range of a computed value).** Every value that a configuration expression computed at expansion (`register: "r * 65536"`, `bit: "b"`, `divide_by: "d"`, a `register` of `identify`, the ends of `read.ranges`, a `health` rule's `good_when.field`) is checked as stage 3 checks the value written at its key, by `SchemaSet::check_value`. The device's own keys (`identify`, `read`, `health`) are evaluated in stage 4 with the parameters and no variable, like every other expression key (M7): names, CF-410 and canonical text.
  - The key's schema is found by its path below `<protocol>.schema.json#/$defs/device`, then `#/$defs/template` (`points`, `[]`, `address`, `register`), following every `$ref` and `allOf`, and `unevaluatedProperties` where no schema names the key (`overrides`). Where several joined schemas give the key a rule, the value meets each.
  - A `oneOf` whose branches give the key different rules is refused with INTERNAL (M8): the branch a value meets is not known from the key alone, and joining the branches would refuse what stage 3 accepts. No built-in schema has such a key (`SchemaSet::keys_split_by_one_of`, tested on every built-in schema).
  - The value is checked by stage 3's own code, so every rule of the key applies: the type and its integer form (CF-203), `minimum` and `maximum` (CF-204, `address.register must be between 0 and 65536`), `x-scada-min` and `x-scada-max`, `enum` and `const`. A sequence of which an item was computed is then checked as a whole, as stage 3 checks it (M3): a bit `field` `[lo, hi]` needs lo <= hi (CF-204, the same rule as stage 3's, `bit_field_order_error` in `rules.h`).
  - The schema is the only source of the bounds. The key is named from its point (`address.bit`), or from the device for its own keys (`identify[0].address.register`).
  - A computed value whose key has no rule in the schema, or rules of another set (`KeyRules` is built only by `key_rules`), is an internal error (INTERNAL from `validate_and_expand`), never a value taken as valid. With the built-in schemas no key reaches it, so the unit tests of `check_merged_points` and `SchemaSet` cover that path.
  - Point names substituted by a `repeat` are not checked again: stage 4 checks them (S3). The computed values are the list `ExpandedTree::computed`.
  - The result of a value kept for its key is the protocol's: the key of the cache is the protocol, the key's path, the text and the label (item 2 of the delta review).
  - A name that a device's own key or a read range's end uses and no parameter gives is CF-410 `"<name>" is not a parameter of this device`: these keys have the parameters and no variable. In a point it stays `... is not a parameter and no variable bound to this point` (D-109).
  - **Its cost.** A computed value is a node of the expanded tree, so there are at most 4,000,000 of them; the work of their evaluation is the expression budget's (D-114), and the checks are bounded by the node limit. The rules of each key are found once per protocol and path (`SchemaSet::key_rules`), and the result of a value at a key is kept for the next instance that computes the same (at most 65,536 kept; past that, values are checked again, with the same result). Measured (release build, x86-64, all values distinct): six instances of a 65,536-point `repeat` with a computed `register` (393,216 values) take 2.9 s in all, 0.55 s more than the same tree with the register written; four instances with three computed keys each (786,432 values), 2.9 s. The reviewers measured 1.8 s for 983,040 values and 2.3 s for 1,160,000 on their machine: about 2 µs a value, linear.
- **No error twice.** Stage 5 runs only when stages 3 and 4 found none, so the cause that C5 names (`counter: false`, an `address.type` under `protocol: io`) is already refused, and CF-504 is never given beside it.
Reason: each check reads the point as the driver will, after every merge, so a template that a base, a default or an override completes is valid, and one that none completes is refused at the instance that uses it.
Consequences: the corpus case `s5-integer-on-floating-raw` is the one saved in the slice that moved CF-504 to stage 5, with instances added, since only expanded points are checked. The cases `s5-computed-values-by-parameter` and `s5-computed-values-by-repeat` give `register` 70000, `bit` 16 and `divide_by` 0 through a parameter and through a `repeat` variable, beside each key's edge, which passes. `s5-integer-scaling-range` (CF-516), `s5-integer-scaling-merged` (CF-517) and `s5-incomplete-merged-maps` (CF-202) are conformance test 2 at stage 5. The references that need point tags come with slice 7 of #100.


## D-116: The tag names of the expanded tree and the references to them (stage 5)
Date: 2026-11-27
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03), slice 7a; review of PR 153
Status: accepted (the lead, review of PR 153 and its delta check), with the stated choices below on #136

Context: contract 2.6 resolves a tag reference against "every tag of the expanded tree, generated tags included (section 12.5)", and 12.4 and 12.5 list those tags. The tag set itself, with owners, queues and the hash, is #101; the owner has since approved the owner-name rule (#151), which #101 builds on and slice 7a does not use. Slice 7a needs only names and types.
Options: build the tag set of #101 first; build names and types only for stage 5.
Decision: stage 5 builds the tags by name with their value types (`src/tag_names.cpp`, `TagNames`), with no owner, queue or flag, so #101 may reuse it without taking a naming rule from it.
- **The tags.**
  - A point's tag `<device>.<point>`, unless `tag: false`; its type is the given `value_type`, else derived as in 4.7 (shared with the merged-point checks, `src/point_types.cpp`): `string` for the raw types `string` and `bytes`. An `io` point without `value_type` is `bool` on a `gpio` channel and `float64` on another backend, the simulator's included (B.2: a line is digital, an IIO channel analog; stated choice, on #136).
  - A bit's tag `<device>.<bit>`, BOOL (5.4).
  - The tags of tag files, with their `value_type`; a virtual tag's type is given by slice 7b (D-117).
  - An interlock's name, BOOL.
  - The generated tags of 12.5:
    - of an output: `.mode`, `.status`; `.req_manual` and `.req_auto` of its target's type, for a target that is a tag; `.refresh` with `refresh`; `.failsafe` when a fail-safe key, given or by its default (7.2, 7.4), is `safe` or `release`, so a local output has one by default; a `step`, `pulse` or `trigger` has none, as it has no safe action (7.2, CF-719). Driver interface 8.13.3 gives every output a fail-safe state; the tag follows CS 12.5, the contract of the names (stated choice, on #136). `.device_selected` never in phase 2 (no protocol declares a native select);
    - `sys.area.<area>.authority` for each area;
    - `<tag>.alarm.<id>` and its `.shelved_until` for each alarm, wherever it stands; `<tag>` is the alarm's own `tag`, else the tag it is listed at, and the `name` of an expression alarm; `id` defaults to the `type`. An alarm on a tag that no rule makes has no state tags (its CF-310 is reported). An alarm listed at a `tag: false` point that names no `tag` has none either: it is CF-310 for the point's tag (stated choice, on #136). An expression alarm is on no tag: its state tags against any other of their name are CF-302 (item 3 of the delta check), not CF-812;
    - `sys.proc.<process>.config_id` and `.config_state` for the processes of 12.2 always in the tag set, and every driver signal of the driver interface contract (11.1) for `io` and `drv-<protocol>` when the tree has a channel of it. These are process names of 12.2, not owner names;
    - `sys.drv.<channel>.<signal>`, with `period.<group>` (`float64`) for each poll group of the channel, and `sys.dev.<device>.<signal>`, with `identity` and `identity_ok` only for a device with `identify`, and `refresh_failures` only for a device a `refresh` output writes (11.1: "Phase 5, devices with a refreshed output (8.12)"; stated choice, on #136).
  - Not built: `ctl.<program>.<block>.<param>`. The block library declares no block type in phase 2, so no tunable parameter exists.
- **How they are held** (M7 of the review). The tags of a device and of a channel are not stored: `TagNames::find` makes them from the device's and channel's names and the signal tables of 11.1. A stored tag is one map entry: its name and a `TagInfo` of views into the Documents of the tree (value type, origin, defining node, point, device, protocol), so a `TagNames` lives no longer than the call that made it. A device keeps the sorted names of its points, a channel its poll groups. Measured (release build, x86-64, peak of the whole validation against main): 55,000 one-point devices (990,000 tags) +4 MB (349 MB before the review); 199,000 devices stop at CF-1001 with a peak of 229 MB (1,259 MB before); 300,000 alarms (600,000 state tags) +93 MB; 300,000 point tags +9 MB. The peak of 858 MB of D-114 is unchanged.
- **CF-1001.** `limits.max_tags` counts the tags of the tag set, generated tags included (4.1). The count is `TagNames::size()`, the number of tags #101's tag set has. Stage 4 counts the point and bit tags as it expands; stage 5 counts all of them, the process tags first, and gives `{n} tags, the limit is {limit}` at what made the tag that passes it; a process tag is made from nothing a user wrote, so its CF-1001 is at `limits.max_tags` in system.yaml. The tags after it are not collected, and the references are not checked. Stage 5 gives CF-1001 and CF-403 for generated tags, while the stage table of 1.4 lists both at stage 4 only (on #136). A `max_tags` below 21, the process tags of a tree with one protocol, can never pass.
- **CF-301, CF-302 and CF-403.**
  - CF-301 for a name a user writes in `sys`: a tag, a device (once, at the device's name; its points are then not refused one by one), an output, an expression alarm's name. A refused output or alarm makes no tags.
  - The tags are collected in phases, so the tags an output or an alarm needs are known when it is met: the process tags; areas, channels, devices with their points and bits, the tags of tag files and interlocks; the alarms; the outputs. An alarm whose tag comes later (an output's tag, another alarm's state tag) waits for it.
  - CF-302 for a name made twice: two user tags at the second; a user tag and a generated tag at the user's tag, the name to change, with "first at" naming what made the other; two generated tags from different causes at the second (stated choice, on #136). A stored name that is a device's or a channel's tag is CF-302 too, although those tags are not stored (item 4 of the delta check). An alarm whose state tag is not made makes no `shelved_until`, so it is one error. A user tag against two alarms of one id on its name is CF-302 twice at one place, with two "first at".
  - Not CF-302: the state tags of two alarms of one id on one tag (CF-812, slice 7c), and the tags of a device or channel given twice, which are collected once (CF-303). CF-303 does not name outputs: an output given twice is CF-302 once, at its `.mode`, and its other tags are made once (on #136).
  - CF-403 `generated tag {name} is {n} bytes, the limit is 127` for a generated name over 127 bytes: an alarm's state tags and an output's. A device's name is at most 100 bytes and a channel's and a poll group's at most 31 (2.1), so their tags never pass 127 bytes.
- **Placement and grouping.** An error on the expanded tree is placed by `expanded_diagnostic`: at the node when the instance's own file wrote it, else at the instance with the template note and the point. Every such error of stage 5 goes through `GroupedErrors` (D-114), and only those: an error of another file goes to the Diagnostics at once, which keep the first of the sorted order of 1.4 however many the expanded tree has (item 2 of the delta check). one cause, by its code, its template line and its message without the instance's name (`instance_free_key`, and "first at" the instance's own place read as `<instance>`), met at many instances is one error naming the others; an error the instance's own file contributes to is never grouped. A template line that names one instance's tag gives an error at each other instance and none at that one: two errors where the cause is one. A generated name's CF-403 is keyed without its byte count, which differs between instances.
- **References** (CF-310 `tag {name} does not exist`):
  - an output's `target` and `feedback` (a tag, `on` and `off`, or `tag`);
  - an area's `authority.local_panel.input`;
  - an alarm's `tag`, wherever the alarm stands (an alarm file, a tag file, a point or a bit), its `limit` when written as a tag, and its `setpoint`;
  - a program block's source (`in`): `{ tag }`, or a scalar that is neither a literal (a boolean, a number, a duration) nor a pin `<block id>.<pin>` of the program (9.2); and the tags its `out` writes. A literal is read from the text, so a quoted `'5'` is a literal too.
  - The kind a reference needs is checked with the rule that names it: the output's target and feedback (CF-702, CF-715) and the local panel's input (CF-718) in slice 7e.
- **CF-312** for a `limit` or `setpoint` tag that is not a number: a limit and a setpoint are compared with a value (8), and the contract names no other type for them. A virtual tag is checked with the type slice 7b gives it.
- **Within a device** (2.6: "The points of the same template after expansion"): `exponent_from.point`, `quality_from[].point` other than `self`, `source_time_from`, a health rule's `point` and `applies_to` names, a restart indication's and an identity check's `point` name a point of the device, or CF-310 `point {name} does not exist`. A bit is not a point: a companion that names a bit is CF-310 (stated choice, on #136). An `applies_to` pattern (2.5) that matches no point is CF-311 `pattern {pattern} matches no tag`, the contract's text; the patterns of one validation are compared with at most 20,000,000 point names in all, and those past it are not checked. A device given twice is checked once.
- **Poll groups.** A channel's `poll_groups` replace the defaults `fast`, `normal`, `slow` (4.6: the defaults are the key's default; CS 4.6 and 4.7 support it, both reviews of the delta check follow it). A point's poll group, given or the default `normal`, is one of its channel's, or CF-604 at the `poll_group`, or at the point's name for the default. A `wo` point is never read (4.7) and has no default group; a group given to it is checked as any other and refused by CF-614 (slice 7d). An `io` channel has no `poll_groups` key (its schema): its groups are the defaults, and a group given to an `io` point is checked against them. The `period` tags of a channel are those of its groups.
- **For slice 7b and #101.** Both take the names from the same `TagNames`: `find(name)` (a copy of the `TagInfo`), `size()`, `complete()`, `points_of` and `poll_groups_of`. Slice 7b adds what only it needs, with its caller and its tests (item 5 of the delta check): `set_value_type` for a virtual tag of a tag file, taking a constant or a view into the tag file, and `each_with_prefix`, which visits the stored tags and those of the devices and channels whose names can start with the prefix (a range of each map, and each dotted beginning of the prefix), in the order of names (`TagNamesPrefix.*`). `validate.cpp` keeps the `TagNames` as a local of stage 5 until #101 needs it after stage 5. #101 adds owners and queues to the names it gets this way; nothing here depends on how owners are named.
Reason: a reference error now stays an error once #101 builds the tag set, since both read the same names; finding a device's and a channel's tags from their names keeps memory to the tags a user wrote.
Consequences: `References.AReferenceToATagGivesNoErrorYet` is replaced, on purpose, by `References.AReferenceToATagNamesATag`, and three tests of other rules now list the CF-310 of their dangling tags. The `exponent_from` row of `MergedPoints.EveryKeyAMergedMapNeeds` (#149) now names a point that exists, as it tests CF-202 only. Corpus cases `s5-tag-references` and `s5-generated-tags-limit` (2,800 one-point devices, refused at the 2,777th by the default limit); corpus case `s5-program-of-section-9-2` now lists the CF-310 of its interlock source, a bare tag source. The README holds the table of every code of section 10 with where it is given or which issue holds it.

## D-118: The pipeline's apt reads only the distribution's sources
Date: 2026-11-27
Phase / issue: 2, #160
Status: proposed

Context: the runner images `ubuntu-24.04` and `ubuntu-24.04-arm` carry apt sources of their own besides the Ubuntu archive, among them `packages.microsoft.com`. On 2026-11-27 that host answered `403 Forbidden` for its `InRelease`, which apt reports as "is no longer signed". `apt-get update` then fails, and with it the LLVM step of `setup-build-machine`, in every job, on the same image version (20261122.23.1) that passed the day before. The pipeline installs nothing from those sources: LLVM 18 (`clang-18`, `clang-format-18`, `clang-tidy-18`, `clang-tools-18`, `libclang-rt-18-dev`, `llvm-18`, version `1:18.1.3-1ubuntu1`) and the OCaml packages that build herd7 (`ocaml-nox`, `ocaml-dune`, `menhir`, `libmenhir-ocaml-dev`, `libzarith-ocaml-dev`) are packages of the Ubuntu archive.
Options: move every source list but the distribution's aside before `apt-get update`; give `apt-get update` a list of sources to read (`-o Dir::Etc::sourceparts`); let `update` fail and install from the lists it fetched; pin LLVM elsewhere.
Decision: the first step of `setup-build-machine` moves every file of `/etc/apt/sources.list.d` except `ubuntu.sources` aside, for the job only, and prints the sources apt then reads. Without `ubuntu.sources` the step fails. Nothing else changes: the same packages at the same versions, the same retries, checks and flags.
Reason: apt then reads only what the pipeline installs from, so a source the project does not use cannot stop it. Moving the files is visible in the log and is the same on both architectures. Ignoring a failed `update` would also hide a failure of the Ubuntu archive.
Consequences: the herd7 cache key hashes `action.yml`, so the model check builds herd7 again once (version 7.58, checked against its commit). Tool versions still come with the runner image: LLVM 18 is preinstalled there and apt finds it the newest version; CMake (3.31.6), GCC (13.3.0) and Python (3.12.3) are the image's own. Pinning them is #141, not this decision.

## D-117: Run-time expressions checked against the tags (stage 5)
Date: 2026-11-27
Phase / issue: 2, #100 (P2-03), slice 7b
Status: proposed

Context: contract 5.6 has virtual tags, alarm expressions, `suppress_when` and interlock conditions compiled and type-checked at validation, over the tags of the expanded tree (2.6), with patterns resolved then (2.5), a cycle of virtual tags refused (CF-525) and a virtual tag's `value_type` derived from its expression. Slice 7a (D-116) built the tags by name and type; a virtual tag's type was left empty. Some points are left open, below.
Options: for each open point, the simplest reading of 5.6, or a question on #136 where the contract does not decide.
Decision: `check_run_time_expressions` (`src/runtime_expressions.cpp`) runs after the tags are collected and before the references to them are checked (D-116), so every later check reads a virtual tag's derived type. Past `limits.max_tags` the tags are not whole (CF-1001), and it checks nothing, as the references do not (M4 of the review of #163).
- **The sites:**
  - a virtual tag's `expr` in `tags/*.yaml`, run by its `task` (`normal` by default, 5.1);
  - an interlock's `condition` in `outputs/*.yaml`, run by its `task` (`normal` by default, 7.3);
  - without `task`, `normal` is the task; a `control/tasks.yaml` without `normal` leaves the tag or interlock with none: CF-310 `task "normal" does not exist` at its name (S4 of the review of #163; an explicit `task` is checked with the other names, D-113);
  - an alarm's `expr` (of `type: expression`) and `suppress_when`, wherever the alarm stands: `alarms/*.yaml`, a tag of a tag file, a point or a bit of the expanded tree. An alarm runs in no control task (`scada-alarm`).
  
  An expression of the expanded tree is placed at its instance with the template note and the point, and one cause at many instances is one error (D-114). Structured text is not in phase 2.
- **Names and types** (`run_time_type`, `src/expression_types.cpp`):
  - a reference is a tag, read as its type: `bool` BOOL, `float64` REAL, the integer types (and an enumeration, a double point) INT; a `uint64` tag reads as INT (5.6);
  - `x.quality` is INT and `x.age` TIME when `x` is a tag; a name that is both a tag and such an attribute is CF-521;
  - a STRING tag is CF-522 `string tag {name} cannot be used in an expression`, also through `.quality`, `.age`, `tag("...")` or a pattern, whose error names the first such tag it matches (S1 of the review of #163);
  - a name that is no tag is CF-310 `tag {name} does not exist`, in the form of D-116;
  - `quality`, `age` and `is_good` take one tag reference (a tag by name or `tag("...")`, not `x.quality`);
  - `tag("...")` names one tag; a pattern there is CF-523;
  - the count of `shl` and `shr` written as a number, a literal or `-` of one (`-1` parses as the negation of 1), is 0 to 63 (5.6), or CF-523 `expression: shl: the count must be from 0 to 63`; any other count, a tag's or an expression's, is the run time's. A configuration expression evaluates its shifts, and gives CF-410 there.
- **Aggregates:** one pattern, resolved now against every tag (2.5), CF-311 `pattern {pattern} matches no tag` when it matches none; or two or more tag references. BOOL inputs are CF-523 for `sum`, `avg`, `min_of` and `max_of`, naming the first BOOL tag a pattern matches; `count_good` takes any input but STRING. A pattern without a wildcard names one tag and is read as a pattern: `avg("n.b1.p")` over a BOOL tag reads `tag "n.b1.p" of pattern "n.b1.p"`.
- **Time functions** (`moving_avg`, `rate`, `totalise`): a tag reference that is a number and a TIME literal. The window must be 1 to 10,000 cycles of the task's period, the window at least one period and at most 10,000 (CF-524). A window that is not a whole number of cycles passes (`T#150ms` in `normal`): 5.6 asks for none (on #136). The periods are those of `control/tasks.yaml`, else `fast` 10 ms, `normal` 100 ms, `slow` 1 s (9.1). An alarm's windows are not checked: 5.6 names no task for them (on #136). A task that does not exist is CF-310 elsewhere (D-113), and its windows are not checked.
- **Results:** an alarm's `expr`, its `suppress_when` and an interlock's `condition` are BOOL, or CF-523 `expression: the result must be BOOL, not {type}`.
- **Virtual tags and interlocks:**
  - An interlock is evaluated "like a virtual tag" (7.3) and is a command permissive: it is a vertex of the same graph (R1 of the review of #163). A cycle through interlocks, or through interlocks and virtual tags, is CF-525, in the contract's text `virtual tags form a cycle: {names}`, which says "virtual tags" also for a cycle of interlocks only. Confirmed by the owner on 2026-11-27 (#136). An interlock's condition is then checked with the other expressions, so an interlock in a cycle may also give its own CF-523.
  - What one reads: the virtual tags and interlocks it names, with `.quality` or `.age`, through `tag("...")`, or through a pattern that matches them (5.6: a cycle through a pattern that matches the tag itself counts). Each distinct pattern is one vertex of its own, whose inputs are the virtual tags and interlocks whose names it matches. They are walked from the sorted names that start with its prefix when the walk reaches it, once, and are never stored (M1 of the review of #163).
  - A name given twice is CF-302 (D-116): the vertex is the tag that defines the name, and the other is not read as it (S3 of the review of #163).
  - The components of that graph are found with Tarjan's walk, iteratively, and each is complete before any that reads it. A component of more than one vertex, or one that reads itself, is CF-525, once, at the name of its first virtual tag or interlock in the order written, their names in that order; a pattern is not named. The message is cut at 1,024 bytes with `...`, and no name past the cut is built. Its virtual tags stay of unknown type.
  - A cycle is CF-525 whatever the tasks of its members, also when they run in different tasks: 5.6 refuses a cycle and leaves cycles across tasks open, where one task would read the other's value of the cycle before (on #136).
  - A missing `normal` gives one CF-310 per virtual tag or interlock without `task`, at its name.
  - Every other virtual tag is typed after its inputs: BOOL gives `bool`, INT `int64`, REAL `float64`, TIME `int64` (milliseconds, 5.6). A given `value_type` that is not the derived one is CF-527 `{name}: the expression gives {value type}`, at the given `value_type`, named as `value_type` is written. The given type stays the tag's, as its readers expect it.
  - An expression that reads a tag of unknown type (in a cycle, or whose own expression is refused) is not checked further and gives no error of its own: the cause is reported where it stands.
- **Cost** (M1 to M3 of the review of #163):
  - A pattern is resolved once, from its prefix before the first wildcard (`TagNames::each_with_prefix`, D-116). What it matches is kept as a summary of fixed size (`PatternReads`): the count, the first STRING tag or tag of unknown type, the first BOOL tag, whether any is REAL. Every virtual tag a pattern matches is an input of what reads it, so it is typed before the pattern is first resolved, and the summary does not change. One use of a pattern is then O(1), whatever it matches.
  - At most 65,536 summaries and 65,536 results of an expression text (with its task) are kept; past them a pattern is resolved, and a text checked, at each use (`ExpandLimits::kept_run_time`). One template line at every instance is checked once. A kept result is keyed by views into the documents, its text and its task's, and copies no text but its error's message; a kept summary copies its pattern and the two names it holds. Those bytes are at most 16 MiB in all (S-1 of the delta check of #163); past it, as past the count.
  - The work counted (`Validation::run_time`) is the comparisons of a pattern with a name: in the graph, each distinct pattern against the names of virtual tags and interlocks under its prefix, once; when resolved, against the tags under its prefix, once. It is the number of distinct patterns times the names under their prefixes, times the steps of the matcher, which retries a `*` over names of up to 127 bytes; no limit of 1.3 bounds that product.
  - **The work budget** (ruling of the delta check of #163, item 1, as D-114 did for stage 4): 32 units per name visited, in the graph or when a pattern is resolved, and 1 per step of the matcher, at most 1e10 units (`ExpandLimits::run_time_pattern_work`). The ruling's intent is the time of the stage-4 budget, about 13 s; the first figure, 2e10, was an estimate of it, and was lowered to 1e10 once measured. A pattern resolved again past the kept summaries counts again. Past the budget the tree is refused with CF-113 `the pattern matching of run-time expressions exceeds its limit of 10000000000 units`, at the expression being checked, or at the virtual tag or interlock whose inputs were being walked. Nothing more is checked, and the expression being checked reports nothing, so no CF-311 comes from a pattern resolved in part. The budget is a limit the contract does not state (on #136, a row for 1.3). `scada-conf-runtime-limits-test` bounds the work of each case.
  - The worst accepted time under the budget, measured on x86-64 in release, is that of patterns that each match every name under their prefix, which read each name they match, at about 1.45 ns per unit: 45,000 tags and 2,350 such patterns (`count_good("r.i*")`, `count_good("r.i**")`, ...) use 9.93e9 units and are accepted in 13.7 to 14.4 s. With 2,380 patterns (1.009e10 units) the tree is refused. Patterns of the shape `t.*a{110}bNNNNN`, which make the matcher retry the most, take 0.96 ns per unit; short names that do not match, 0.8 ns. The tree of the review of #163 with 45,000 tags and 4,000 such patterns (1.88e10 units) is now refused with CF-113, after 13.8 s; that is intended. So is the one with 25,000 tags and 5,000 patterns (1.38e10 units). The tags of devices and channels are made and sorted once, at the first pattern, and each pattern takes its range of them (item 1 of the budget check of #163): before, each pattern under `sys.dev.` made and sorted about 20 names per device, uncounted, and a valid tree of 2,400 devices and 2,400 distinct `count_good("sys.dev.*NNNNNN.stat?")` took 46.2 s at 8.8 ns per unit. Now it takes 4.5 s; with 4,500 distinct such patterns (9.86e9 units) it is accepted in 8.1 s, and 4,700 are refused. After CF-113, the pattern being resolved stops at once.
  - Measured (release build, x86-64, the whole of `validate_and_expand`):
    - 2 instances of a 65,536-point `repeat` whose points each have an alarm with a `suppress_when` over a pattern: 2.12 s, against 2.21 s on main, without the check;
    - 10,000 virtual tags in one chain, written in reverse order: 0.09 s;
    - 2,000 virtual tags that each sum one pattern over 60,000 tags: 1.35 s, against 0.51 s on main;
    - 2,000 virtual tags of 2,000 different patterns with no literal prefix over 60,000 tags: 2.93 s, against 0.35 s on main;
    - the trees of the review of #163: in the pull request;
    - the shape of `slow2000` of the delta check, rebuilt here (2,000 patterns `t.*a{110}bNNNNN` over 47,000 tags of 117 bytes, max_tags 50,000; the patterns match no tag, so each is CF-311): 546 s at 7c9544b; now CF-113 after 9.3 s, peak 40 MB.
    - `dev4500` of the budget check, rebuilt here (2,400 devices, 4,500 distinct `count_good("sys.dev.*NNNNNN.stat?")` and `stat*`): 8.1 s, 9.86e9 units, accepted, peak 22 MB.
Reason: this is 5.6 as written. Where it does not decide (the windows of an alarm, a given `value_type` that differs from the derived one only in width), the narrower reading is taken and asked on #136, so that nothing is accepted that a ruling might refuse later, except the alarm's window, which is not checked rather than guessed.
Consequences: tests whose run-time expressions named tags that do not exist now list their CF-310, or define the tags where the test is about something else; the corpus case `s3-valid-expressions` defines the tags it reads. Corpus case `s5-run-time-expressions`. The README's code table names the source of each code.

## D-130: Device local times are converted with a table built from the unit's compiled zone files
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07); review of PR 128 (C++ B3, SCADA decisions; delta check N2, N3, N6)
Status: accepted

Context: driver interface contract 5.8 and configuration schemas contract 4.6 have the driver convert a device's local time (`device_time: { zone: local, tz }`) to UTC, with the earlier offset in a repeated hour and a move forward in a skipped hour, both marked TIME_UNCERTAIN. Neither says where the zone rules come from, and the conversion runs on a worker for every sample with a device time. A first version used the C++ library's time zone database (`std::chrono::get_info`); with GCC 13.3 and tzdata 2026e it gives wrong offsets for zones whose standard offset changed while a daylight rule was in force (America/Vancouver in 2026: -8 h where the zone file says -7 h), with no flag.
Options: the C++ library's database; the C library (`localtime_r` with TZ, which is process-wide and not thread-safe for several zones); our own reader of the compiled zone files (TZif, RFC 9636) that the C library reads too; shipping zone data in the repository.
Decision: `ZoneTable::build` reads the zone's compiled file from the zone directory (the environment variable TZDIR, else the CMake option SCADA_ZONEINFO_DIR, default `/usr/share/zoneinfo`; no path in the sources), with a bounded reader (version 2 or later, no leap second records, counts and offsets checked, file at most 256 KiB, the footer's TZ string parsed with the extensions of RFC 9636), and keeps every interval of constant offset from 1970 to 2100: the file's transitions, then the footer's rule. The zone name is checked against a strict grammar before a path is built, and names the system chooses rather than the database are refused: `localtime`, `posixrules`, and anything under `posix/` or `right/` (copies on other time scales). The directory must be an absolute path (TZDIR as well as the CMake option). The file is opened beneath the directory with `openat2` and `RESOLVE_BENEATH | RESOLVE_NO_MAGICLINKS` (Linux 5.6; the minimum kernel is 5.16, so this is the path in use), with `O_NONBLOCK`, and must be a regular file: a link whose target stays beneath the directory, as zone aliases are installed, is followed; one that leads out of it, an absolute link, and `..` are refused (PERMISSION_DENIED). Where `openat2` is not available (ENOSYS), the path is resolved in user space, checked to lie beneath the resolved directory, and opened with `O_NOFOLLOW`; the directory belongs to root, so a change between the check and the open needs root. That fallback accepts an absolute link whose target lies beneath the directory, which `openat2` refuses; no zone database installs one. EPERM from a seccomp filter is a refusal, not a missing call, and is not a reason to fall back: a service sandbox must allow `openat2` (a deployment requirement, in the library's README). A file without a footer rule gives no offset after its last transition: from then on every conversion is marked uncertain (all of the table, for a file with no transition either). Changes of a footer rule at one instant are ordered by instant, then by the year of the rule, then the start before the end, so that the result is the same every time: an end that meets the next year's start gives daylight time all year (RFC 9636 3.3.1, `EST5EDT,0/0,J365/25`), and a start and an end of one year at one instant give none, as the C library reads it. The rule's changes are made one year beyond each end of the table, so that a change on the table's edge meets its neighbour. `ZoneTable::to_utc` is a binary search with no allocation, lock or system call (`SCADA_RT`), saturating at the limits of int64; a time outside 1970 to 2100 takes the nearest end's offset and is marked uncertain. One table per zone is owned by the compiled configuration; rules borrow it (`DeviceTimeRule::zone`).
Reason: the zone files are what the C library and `zdump` read, so the unit agrees with its own tools; the reader is small, bounded and fuzzed, and does not depend on a standard library's release.
Consequences: a test compares every zone of the build machine's database with the C library's reading of the same files, by a full search, at about a million local times around every change from 1970 to 2100 (`scada-driver-zones-test`), and five footer rules no zone uses today (one instant, one day, times outside 0 to 24 h) with a 30-minute scan. Daylight time all year is tested against RFC 9636 rather than the C library: glibc 2.39 gives standard time from 00:00 UTC on 1 January to the rule's start. The zone files are read when a configuration is compiled, so an update of the unit's zone files takes effect at the next reload or generation change, not before; nothing reports its version yet (to be added with the start log, slice 5). Deployment requirement: the target image carries the compiled zone files in SCADA_ZONEINFO_DIR (or sets TZDIR); without them a configuration with a local-time device is refused when compiled. `scada-conf` validating offline on another machine may read other zone files than the unit; the check mode on the unit reads the unit's.

## D-131: Details of the processing after decode that the driver interface contract leaves open
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07); review of PR 128
Status: accepted

Context: contract 5.1 to 5.8 and 7.5 fix the order and the rules of the processing after decode, but leave some marks open. None touches a command or an output.
Options: for each, the reading of the contract and of tag database contract 6 that never shows a value as better than it is (below), or leaving it to each protocol.
Decision:
- NOT_INIT stays on every write of a point until a value read from the device has been written since the attach (tag database contract 6: the initial state; driver interface contract 5.2: "NOT_INIT if never read"). A `substitute` counts as written; its quality is UNCERTAIN.
- A sample that is not VALUE is written with the receive time and LOCAL_TIME: it records when the failure was seen, stamped by the driver.
- A read **confirms** a point (contract 7.5, X-11) when its outcome is VALUE, no invalid marker matched and the value is finite. The staleness timer starts at the attach, so a point that never answers gets STALE after `max_age` too. A STALE already given stays on writes that do not confirm.
- A matched invalid marker counts in `sentinels`, like a NO_VALUE sample: both mean the device has no value (contract 5.2, row 2; 11.1).
- An exponent outside -20 to 20, or not an integer, gives BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE. A scaled result that is not finite gives BAD with OVERFLOW.
- The health rules and the `quality_from` rules (step 6) apply to every outcome and every marker action; the worst result is the tag's quality (CS 4.7, 4.9). A rule on the point's own word applies only to an answer with a value.
- Flags that come from a device or a map (a protocol's mapping of NO_VALUE or DEVICE_INVALID, a marker, a `quality_from` entry) are masked to OUT_OF_RANGE, OVERFLOW, BLOCKED and TEST (`DEVICE_FLAGS_MASK`); SUBSTITUTED joins them with tag database 1.1.
- DEVICE_INVALID is never GOOD (a mapping that gives GOOD is UNCERTAIN), and never writes a value nobody read: before the first valid value it is BAD. After one, the last valid value is held UNCERTAIN with STALE and the mapping's flags, and BAD with STALE once `max_age` has passed since it was read, as `keep_last` is held (CS 4.7), for the reason contract 5.2 gives for `keep_last`. A mapping that gives BAD is BAD, with its flags.
- A companion carries the quality its own processing gave it (`CompanionInput::mark`). A companion that is BAD, or whose outcome is not VALUE, makes the points that depend on it BAD with its flags (COMM_FAIL and the device flags); an UNCERTAIN one makes them at most UNCERTAIN, with its flags, whether it is read as an exponent, a time, a `quality_from` word or a health point. A health point that is BAD counts as not good. A time companion that is not a time is BAD.
- Bits named by `bits` take the quality and flags of the word they come from, and keep their last values when the word has none.
- A STRING value that is not well-formed UTF-8 is BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE; a string cut to 250 bytes keeps its quality and gets OVERFLOW (contract 5.2, last table).
- A floating raw value read as a code (an enumeration, a double point) is taken only within 0.001 of an integer (CS 5.5), otherwise BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE. Any other INT64 or UINT64 point on a floating raw type is refused (D-134).
- An enumeration is an INT64 tag of the device's code, without a scale: it is read and written as the code (CS 7.2), so the two are inverse. The compile step refuses an enumeration with a scale or on another type.
- A `quality_from` map without `default` gives BAD for a value it does not list. A counter on a negative or floating raw value is BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE. A raw value whose width does not fit its kind (a protocol fault; a width of 0 would make every marker miss) is BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE. A device time on a tag that is not INT64 is BAD.
- `validate_point_rules` states what the compile step refuses (companion slots, bit and field numbers above 63, unsorted tables, a `divide_by` of 0, CF-517, a local time without a zone); the processing writes BAD for any rule that reaches it anyway, never undefined behaviour.
- Amended by #169 (the lead's ruling of 2026-11-27: the library holds to its own documented rules whatever the compile step catches first):
  - `process_sample` calls `validate_point_rules` (real-time: no allocation, one pass over the rules' lists) before any step. Refused rules give BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE for every outcome, the value kept, and the driver's time (LOCAL_TIME). No marker, hold, `quality_from`, `health` or time companion is applied. Before, some paths, such as a substitute marker or a young hold, still gave UNCERTAIN.
  - The rules do not change between samples, so the check could be done once; caching it is for later, when a measurement asks for it.
  - `validate_point_rules` also refuses the CF-504 pairs of the compile step (`check_value_type` in libscada-conf, D-115): a STRING tag if and only if the raw is a string, a BOOL tag only on a bool raw, and no counter on a floating raw. The two lists must change together.
  - Two differences from `check_value_type`:
    - `check_value_type` runs for Modbus points only, the one protocol whose address names a raw type. `validate_point_rules` applies to every protocol's point.
    - `PointRules::raw_kind` defaults to UNSIGNED, and a BOOL or STRING point left with it is refused, so every one of its samples is BAD. `raw_kind` is therefore required: the compile step (slice 8 of #104) must set it from the configuration for every point of every protocol.
  - A marker never applies to a STRING tag: a sample of another kind on a STRING tag (a protocol fault) goes to the string step, which gives BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE, and is never substituted.
  - Whether the framework must refuse a sample of any other kind than the configured raw type is a contract question, on #136.
Reason: each is the reading that never shows a value as better than it is; the rest is left as tag database contract 6 defines its flags.
Consequences: conformance tests 4, 7, 8, 17 and 40 test these readings with the test protocol; a protocol cannot change them. A time-valued point's TIME_UNCERTAIN follows the lead's ruling on CS 4.6, not this decision.

## D-132: A double-point raw code that the `states` map does not name is a bad state
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07)
Status: accepted

Context: configuration schemas contract 5.3 has `states` map the device's raw codes onto the four codes of driver interface contract 5.4, and requires a map for the field and integer shapes (CF-613). It does not say what a raw code missing from the map gives.
Options: code 3 (bad state) with BAD; keep the last value with UNCERTAIN; code 0 (intermediate).
Decision: code 3 with BAD and OUT_OF_RANGE. The compile step fills the default map of the bit shapes.
Reason: an unknown position must not be shown as a position; 3 with BAD is what the contract already uses for "faulty", and `scada-cmd` compares feedback by these codes (phase 5).
Consequences: a template with an incomplete map shows the device faulty, not travelling.

## D-133: An integer value type needs an integer scaling (CF-517); the framework never computes another
Date: 2026-11-23
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07)
Status: accepted: ruled by the lead on 2026-11-23 (CF-517, configuration schemas contract 0.6)

Context: configuration schemas contract 0.5, 4.7, derives `float64` for `scale: { divide_by }` but says that with an integer value type "the computation is in INT64 or UINT64, checked", without saying what an INT64 tag gives when `raw ÷ divide_by` is not whole. Found while writing the processing after decode and reported to the lead (finding F4).
Options: truncate; round half away from zero; BAD at run time when not exact; refuse the configuration.
Decision: the lead ruled that the configuration contract refuses it: a `value_type` of `int64` or `uint64` needs an integer scaling, and `divide_by` above 1, a decimal `gain` or `offset`, and `exponent_from` are CF-517 (configuration schemas 0.6, #100). `divide_by: 1` with an integer `offset` is an integer scaling (a gain of 1) and reads and writes exactly. The integer paths cover no scale and an integer scaling (`is_integer_scaling`); anything else reaching them is written BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE on read and refused on write; neither side computes a value.
Reason: every other choice makes the read and the write of the same point disagree, or turns a configuration mistake into a run-time BAD value.
Consequences: the ruling is in the contracts on main (8498993, PR #134): configuration schemas 0.6, 4.7 and the CF-517 row of section 10 ("`divide_by` above 1, a `gain` or `offset` written as a decimal, or `exponent_from`"), and driver interface 0.6, 8.4 ("an absent gain and `divide_by: 1` count as a gain of 1 ... an integer target takes no other scaling (CS 4.7, CF-517)"). This code follows that text. The compile step of the next slice refuses the same cases (`validate_point_rules`).

## D-134: An integer value type on a floating raw type is refused (CF-504), except for a code
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07); delta check of PR 128 (N1)
Status: accepted (the lead, 2026-11-24)

Context: an INT64 tag with an integer scaling and a floating raw type was read as the raw value's nearest integer without the scaling, and written GOOD: with `gain: 10`, raw float32 4.0 read as 4 where an int16 raw 4 reads 40, and a write of 40 sent raw 4.0. Configuration schemas 0.6, 4.7 says read and write of an integer value type are exact inverses, derives `float64` for any floating raw type, and gives CF-504 for "a `value_type` that cannot hold the raw value"; it does not say whether an explicit `int64` on a `float32` raw type is that.
Options: route the nearest integer through the integer scaling, on read and write; refuse the combination.
Decision: refused. INT64 or UINT64 cannot hold a floating raw value (its fractions, NaN, a float32 up to 3.4e38), so it is CF-504: `validate_point_rules` refuses it, the processing writes BAD with OUT_OF_RANGE for one that reaches it anyway, and `to_raw_value` refuses the write. An enumeration and a double point keep reading a floating raw value as a code within 0.001 (CS 5.3, 5.5); an enumeration writes its code exactly. A counter on a floating raw type is refused too. `PointRules::raw_kind` carries the raw type for the check.
Reason: a rounding of a measured value hidden inside an integer tag is what CS 4.7's derived type avoids; the refusal keeps read and write inverse without a rule the contract does not state.
Consequences: a template that wants an integer from a floating register writes `value_type: float64`, or the protocol's integer type. The fuzz target checks every accepted write by reading its raw value back through the processing.

## D-135: The framework's block planner: segments, clusters, split forms and keep-alive
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07), slice 2
Status: accepted; the side-effect rule is the lead's ruling R4 and the chain rule D3, both accepted (the lead, 2026-11-24)

Context: driver interface contract 6.3 gives the rules of a read plan (largest request, largest gap, the four kinds of range, never split a value, never separate a point from its companions, a split form per request) and 5.5 says a point and its companions are read "in one poll of one poll group, back to back with no other request to that device between them (one request when they fit in one)". CS A.4 gives the limits per table (`max_registers`, `max_bits`) and says a side-effect range is never split. The contract leaves open the grain of the split form, what is read of a range a point touches, and which request is the keep-alive. The committee review of PR 135 found that overlapping ranges of one kind were read in parts, that `max_values` was not kept by every request, and that a far companion made a chain where one request fits.
Options: for the split form, one request per point, per run of points without a bridged gap, or per cluster (a point with its companions); for a range, read only the units of the points in it, or the whole range; for the keep-alive, a dedicated one-unit read or a planned request.
Decision: `plan_blocks` (scada/driver/plan.h), which a protocol whose points are spans of numbered units can use as its whole `plan`:
- Ranges of one kind and place that overlap are one range, their union (CS allows them).
- An atomic range is read whole: a point in it is planned with the whole range.
- A side-effect or exclusive range is not read whole (ruling R4): all points in it are read by one request, over the span from the first to the last unit they decode, and the plan is refused when that span does not fit one request. No gap is bridged into or out of it. An exclusive range's request holds nothing else, and a point across its edge is refused.
- Limits are per space (`ReadRules::spaces`: `max_units` and `max_gap`; the defaults for spaces not listed). `max_values` holds for every request, those of an atomic or side-effect range included; points that cannot keep it are refused.
- A cluster whose points fit one request is one request, with the points between them. Requests then grow in address order where the next fits: within the limits, over a gap of at most `max_gap` units that touches no range, never with an exclusive request, never across spaces, unit ids or poll groups. A cluster whose points do not fit one request is read by consecutive requests marked `chained`, sent back to back; each of those requests still takes its neighbours, so a chain does not cut a run. D3 (the lead's ruling): a chain's side-effect request is sent last, a chain that would hold two side-effect requests is refused, and a side-effect point of another cluster never joins a request of a chain. So a chain an order drops (D-136) never repeats a side-effect read, and a chain whose last request was sent is complete.
- The split form of a request of several parts is one request per part, where a part keeps every cluster whole: after a refusal, the points of a part that is refused again are ABSENT and the others are read. A request of one part, of a chain, or one that reads a side-effect range (CS A.4: never split), has none; refused, its points are ABSENT.
- The keep-alive and the probe are the shortest planned request outside every side-effect and exclusive range and outside every chain, so that a keep-alive never reads a value without its companion. When every planned request is in a chain or reads a side effect, a request of its own (not in `by_group`) reads the shortest part of a chain whose clusters are whole and that reads no side effect (E1); with none, the plan has no keep-alive, which the compile step reports when one is configured.
- Rules the compile step should refuse give INVALID_ARGUMENT, never a plan (the list is in plan.h).
Reason: the cluster is the smallest unit the contract lets a plan split, so its split form is the most precise; R4 reads only what is decoded of a range whose read has an effect, which is what CS A.4 means; a planned request is a real read the device serves, which a dedicated keep-alive may not be.
Consequences: a device with many single-point clusters behind one refused merged read gets many split requests until the next reconnect (6.3: "the rest keep the split plan until reconnect"). Protocols may plan otherwise; Modbus (#105, #106) is expected to use this planner, with `spaces` for its tables. The points this decision raises for the next contract revision are in #136 (D-6, D-8). A seeded property test checks the rules above over 10,000 random plans: each point read once, the limits, no-read and atomic ranges, one request per side-effect range, exclusive requests, the bridged gaps, the split forms (each part named once, their points those of the request), a cluster that fits one request read by one, chains and the D3 rule, and the keep-alive. It fails on each of the planner mutations the delta check tried. Cluster fusion stops as soon as a run cannot fit one request, never walks a segment it has absorbed again, and the split form is found in one sweep, so planning is linear in the points but for sorting and per-cluster maps: 50,000 points whose clusters do not fit, and 50,000 points that one request of 75,000 units holds, each plan in under 0.5 s. A failed fusion still walks up to one request's worth of segments, so the bound in the worst case is the number of segments times the segments one request holds.

## D-136: The scheduler's cycles, ties, orders and published values
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07), slice 2
Status: accepted; R1, R2, R3, R5, R6, R7 and the list rule D1 are the lead's rulings, accepted (the lead, 2026-11-24)

Context: driver interface contract 6.2 orders the worker's choices (orders in progress, new orders, due poll groups by priority then due time, keep-alive, probes) and 6.4 says a paced device does not delay its neighbours. 11.1 publishes `sys.drv.<channel>.period.<group>` "over its last 10 periods" and `sys.dev.<device>.pacing_limited`, "a group misses its period because of pacing". The contract does not say when a late cycle's next one is due, how ties are broken, what the achieved period of a channel's group is when its devices differ, how an order is paced between its steps, or whether a new order waits for a chain of companions. The committee review of PR 135 asked for those, and for orders that can be ended.
Options: one cycle per channel and poll group, or one per device and poll group; a late cycle catching up in a burst, or starting again from its completion.
Decision: `Scheduler` (scada/driver/scheduler.h):
- Each device has its own cycle of each poll group, first due when the scheduler starts, so a paced device never holds up the others. A cycle sends the group's planned requests in plan order, each once. The next cycle is due a period after the last one was due, or at once when that time has already passed: a late group sends at most one cycle at once, never the missed ones in a burst. A chain whose last requests are refused ends with its cycle.
- Ties: a chain in progress first, then priority, then due time, then device order. Strict priority can starve a lower group of a busy device; its achieved period shows it.
- R3: devices of a channel that share a physical unit (`CompiledDevice::unit`, the device's default unit id, 3.4, X-4) share its pacing (the largest intervals of the devices) and its keep-alive silence, and an exclusive order or a chain on one holds all of them. A device without a key (`OWN_UNIT`, the default) is a unit of its own (E3).
- The next step of an exclusive order comes first, and nothing else is sent to its unit between its steps; other units are served meanwhile. R1, R6: between the select and the operate of a select-and-operate order only the request intervals apply, the unit's and the channel's (8.6: "at once"), never a write interval, the gateway's included; the write interval applies before the select and after the operate. D1: a list of writes (8.5) is an exclusive sequence whatever its ticket says, and keeps the write interval between its writes. R5: a cancel is held by the request interval only. A READ_NOW is a read: it neither waits for nor takes the write interval (F2). A select and operate has two steps; a write, a cancel and a READ_NOW one; an order to a disabled device is refused (E8).
- R2, D2: no order step goes between the requests of a chain: any order step chosen while a chain is open on its unit drops the chain (`Choice::chain_dropped`, and `chain_device` names whose, which may be a sibling device's); the worker publishes nothing of it, and it is read again from its first request after the order. Other orders run in arrival order; a device's later orders wait behind its earlier ones. R7: orders may starve a chain on a busy unit (a unit paced at 10 s, a chain of 3 and a write every 25 s complete no chain); there is no cap, and the achieved period shows it.
- Every request keeps its unit's and the channel's request intervals, and every write also the write intervals, counted from the send.
- An order can be ended (`abort_order`), a queued one removed (`remove_order`), and a choice not sent undone (`not_sent`, which restores the pacing, ends an order and returns its id). A reconnect ends the orders in progress (a selection does not survive it, 8.6 rule 3) and writes their ids for the worker to answer (E10); queued orders stay.
- K1: a keep-alive is sent in its current form (a split part not refused; none when every part is), and its refusal never rewinds a cycle.
- The achieved period of a group is, per device, the average of its last 10 periods (start to start), or the age of its running or overdue cycle when that is longer, so a group that never completes is seen; the channel's is the longest of its devices'. `pacing_limited` is true when a cycle of the device waited for a pacing interval and its last request was handed out after its next due time, or it is still running past that time (E4: the time of the last request, not of the `take` that finds the cycle complete). Ages saturate: a clock before a cycle's start gives 0 (E9).
- The order queue is the channel's `max_pending_writes`, reserved at construction. Threads (F9, 2.4): only the worker calls the scheduler; it drains the order queue into `add_order` at each transaction boundary, and since the command thread admits at most `max_pending_writes` unanswered orders, `add_order` refuses only a malformed order or a broken admission rule, which the worker answers REJECTED, BUSY (8.8).
- The scheduler checks its channel once when it is built (`check`, `status`): a plan without one list per poll group, a request index out of range, a period not above 0 or a negative interval give INVALID_ARGUMENT and a scheduler that chooses nothing; `take` checks nothing.
Reason: per-device cycles are what 6.4's second rule needs; restarting a late cycle at its completion keeps a slow device's load at what its pacing allows; the longest period is the one the runtime comparison with the configured period must see (reference 7.5); R1 to R3 are the lead's rulings on command handling.
Consequences: probes of demoted devices (6.2 step 5), retries and devices that are not polled came with failure handling (D-138); the orders' content comes with the command slices (6, 7). Scheduler state is tied to one CompiledChannel; a reload (slice 8) must carry it over. A list of n writes holds its unit and the unit's siblings for (n − 1) write intervals: 5 writes at a 10 s write interval hold them for 40 s with no poll and no keep-alive. The points this decision raises for the next contract revision are in #136 (D-2 to D-5, D-7, D-11, and C-10 for the list warning).

## D-137: Shapes of the protocol interface that the contract leaves to the implementation
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07), slice 2
Status: accepted

Context: driver interface contract 2.2 gives the frame protocol's calls and their result types, and names DriverConfig, ConfigErrors, SampleSink, TxBuffer, Attempt, ChannelState, DeviceState and ProtocolData without defining them.
Options: for each, the smallest shape that the calls of 2.2 and 2.3 need (no allocation, no lock and no system call in encode, frame_state and decode).
Decision:
- `DriverConfig` is the configuration tree of libscada-conf (`scada::conf::Document` and the root node of the driver's part; ruling (i) of the lead, D-111), and `ConfigErrors` is `scada::conf::Diagnostics`.
- A write reaches `encode` as a `Request` of kind WRITE, built by the framework for each step of an order (8.3): the point, its space, unit id and span, and the raw value in `payload` (converted exactly from the command's value, 8.4), which lives in the order's slot and is valid during the call. A planned read has kind READ and no payload.
- `Attempt` holds the transaction id, the channel's `framing` (`tcp`, serial, `rtu_over_tcp`), set by the worker, and a fixed area of 16 bytes in which `encode` records what the answer must echo (a write's function, address and quantity, annex A) for `frame_state` and `decode`. The area has an alignment of 1; a protocol copies values in and out as bytes (`std::bit_cast` to a byte array, `std::copy`), never through a cast pointer. Nothing in it allocates.
- `SampleSink` is a concrete class over a buffer the framework sizes for the request with the most points: `add` stores a sample or, when full, counts it; nothing allocates. `TxBuffer` is a span sized from the device's `max_frame_bytes`.
- `ProtocolData` is a base class with a virtual destructor, read back with `protocol_data<T>`, a static downcast to the type the protocol's own `configure` stored.
- `plan` receives `out.by_group` already sized for the channel's poll groups, since the device does not know them.
- The test protocol of the conformance tests is compiled in code; its `configure` returns UNSUPPORTED until the framework's compile step arrives (slice 8).
Reason: each is the smallest shape the calls need; a virtual sink would put a virtual call per sample on the worker for no gain.
Consequences: none beyond the protocol libraries' (#105, #106) use of these types.

## D-138: The probe budget, retries and devices that are not polled in the scheduler
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07), slice 3
Status: accepted; the probe bound, the retry's turn and the probe of a device with no probe request follow the review of PR 137 (Q1, Q4, Q5, R8); the order of live and failing devices and the probe's window follow its delta check (G5, G7); the windowed probe's time-out, the overdue probe and the early verdict its final pass (J1 to J4), the chain's retries its last C++ pass (K2); the hold of failing polls (G5, K3, L1) and J3's turn taking are taken out by the lead's decision of 2026-11-25 into #147, whose starting point is commit 78c68aa; a probe past its floor goes under the lead's spacing rule N1 instead (its final check of 31d46e4)

Context: driver interface contract 6.2 puts a probe of a demoted device last, "within the probe budget", and 7.4 says probes are "sent only in time no due request needs, at most one probe in flight per channel". 7.1 says a read is retried within its poll group's turn, 3.4 that a time-out of one device delays the others by one request at most, and 6.6 that a side-effect read is never retried. Neither says whether a due request that waits for its spacing makes the time "needed", how long a probe may hold the line, or where a retry goes among the choices of D-136.
Options: a probe whenever nothing can be sent at this instant; or a probe only when no request is waiting and none falls due during the probe.
Decision: `Scheduler` (scada/driver/scheduler.h):
- A probe is chosen only when no order is queued or in progress, no polled device has a retry, a chain or a request of a started cycle left (even one that waits for its spacing), and its unit is not held. Its time-out is its device's, at most 1 s (7.4). When a poll falls due within that time-out, the probe goes only if the window before that poll, less the quiet time a serial line keeps after a time-out, is at least `PROBE_MIN_WINDOW_NS` (100 ms, or the time-out if shorter); its time-out then ends at the window's end (`Choice::timeout_ns`, G7). A probe that waited `PROBE_FLOOR` (10) time-outs past its due time goes in the next idle instant with its whole time-out, so that probes still happen on a line with no window. One windowed probe is sent per due time, and its time-out is no failed probe (K2: the same holds for any probe cut short to a window; its late answer is discarded with the quiet time before the next poll, which then goes one quiet time late) (J1, DI 7.4: a probe has its device's time-out): the back-off does not double and the due time stands, so a device slower than every window is still found by the floor probe.
