A probe past its floor goes by the lead's ruling N1 (replacing "as a due poll of its group", whose floor probes of units demoted together in a faster group held the slower live units 21 s in every 311 s): per channel, at most one probe past its floor is sent in any `PROBE_FLOOR` + 1 probe time-outs (`choose_floor_probe`); when one may go and several are overdue, the oldest overdue goes, ahead of due polls whatever its device's group, since on a saturated line it is the only way a returned unit is found (J3). Floor probes therefore cost the live devices at most one probe time-out in every `PROBE_FLOOR` + 1 (11), on any line and with any number of dead devices; and each dead device is probed at the latest (number of overdue devices + 1) x (`PROBE_FLOOR` + 1) probe time-outs after its floor, beyond its back-off, the one more for a spacing that a device since returned still runs (review Q3; 20 dead units with 1 s probe time-outs: within 231 s). No floor probe goes while any device of the channel has a chain of companions in progress: the chain goes first (DI 6.2 step 3), and a probe between its parts would read a value and its companion a probe time-out apart (review Q1). Measured (`FloorProbesOfAFasterGroupLeaveTheLiveUnitsTheirPeriod`, the reviewer's run: tcp, 20 dead units in the 200 ms group, 5 live in the 1 s group, default keys): after the demotion a live unit's longest gap over 2,000 s is at most its period and one probe time-out (2.05 s bound; 21.0 s before). `EngineRandom` bounds the probes of demoted units by the same rule; its channels of at most 6 units cannot show the old 21 s, which the targeted test does. A cycle probe (below) has no window: its first request is the probe and waits its whole probe time-out, at most 1 s; the cycle's later requests are polls with their device's time-out. At most one probe is in flight per channel, until `probe_done`, a reconnect or `not_sent`. One probe serves every demoted device of a unit (3.4; the engine sets it on the first).
- A retry is its device's next poll, after the orders, and takes its turn among the other devices' polls by its group's priority, due at the time of the failure (Q1). An order step that drops the device's chain drops the retry too (D-136). `retry` ignores a side-effect request.
- The order of service is the contract's (DI 6.2 step 3, conformance test 9): orders first, then a chain of companions in progress, then priority, then due time, in device order. A retry takes its turn in that order (Q1). Dead devices are not held back behind live ones: the hold of failing polls (reviews G5, K3, L1: devices that answer first, the held-back failing polls and retries, the 4-time-out guard, the gap rules for failing polls and probes) is taken out of this pull request into #147, and the last version of it is commit 78c68aa. The cost is a known limit: until a dead device is demoted (`demote_after` requests, each with its retries, every attempt up to its time-out), its requests take their turn in the order like any due request and delay the live devices of its priority and below; a dead device of a faster group, whose attempts take longer than its period, keeps that group always due, and the slower groups wait until it is demoted. A chain of companions with a dead part never rests (K1) and is never demoted while its other parts answer, so when its cycle takes longer than its period the slower groups wait while the part is dead (D-140). A site bounds these with `max_age` on every point that matters, a `timeout` near the real latency, and fewer units per line.
- The probe request is the device's verdict (review M4): the planner takes it among the device's requests whatever their history, and a demoted device returns only when the probe answers. With `demote_after` 1 and a dead block that is also the probe request, the device stays DEMOTED, its points BAD with COMM_FAIL, although its other blocks would answer; a dead block that is not the probe request lets it return and be demoted again (it flaps, G6). Both are pinned by `TheProbeRequestDecidesWhetherADemotedDeviceReturns`. With the default keys (`demote_after` 3) the same holds for one dead block of a faster group (review N2): its attempts, with their retries, come before the device's slower blocks are asked, so the count reaches 3 and the whole device is demoted; when that block is the probe request the device stays DEMOTED, and its answering blocks are BAD with COMM_FAIL. One dead block takes the whole device down when it is the probe request; a point on #136.
- On a line that a faster group saturates from the start, the slower groups' units are never asked (the priority order of slice 2, DI 6.2 step 3): a dead one is then never COMM_FAIL or DEMOTED, since it is never asked, and only `max_age` marks its points. A unit demoted before the line fills is still probed (N1, `ADemotedUnitOfASlowerGroupIsFoundOnASaturatedLine`), but its polls then wait behind the faster group as well (the SCADA reviewer's final check). Choosing the probe among requests that answered before is left to the compile step (slice 8), with the other choices of the plan.
- A demoted device with no probe request (every read has side effects or is chained, plan.h) is probed by one whole poll cycle at each back-off instant (the review, R8): its groups are due at once, the cycle's first request is the probe, and its samples are published as a poll's, so a side-effect read is made once per back-off as a poll makes it. The cycle's answers recover the unit as a probe's do. The compile step (slice 8) reports such a device.
- A device that is not polled (not INITIALISING or RUNNING, 3.5) gets no poll, keep-alive or retry, and its chain is dropped. When it is polled again, every group of it is due at once, and the outage is not published as an achieved period; the next cycle of each group is due one period after that cycle started, so a returned device is read once, side-effect reads included (6.6; review P1: before, it was read twice within 2 ms, `AReturnedDeviceIsPolledOncePerPeriod`).
Reason: a probe that starts while a live device's request waits, or just before one falls due, holds the line for up to 1 s and delays that request by as much: the window keeps the live devices' periods, and the floor bounds the worst case on a line with no window to one probe time-out of delay in every 11. A dead device's request costs its whole time-out (1 s) against milliseconds for a live one, so in the contract's order the dead take most of a busy line until they are demoted: the known limit above, the subject of #147.
Values go bad on time (the lead's ruling J2, D-140): a dead device's points are COMM_FAIL at the latest one period of its poll group, or its `retries` times its time-out if that is longer, after its first time-out, plus the end of the exchange on the line then, however long its retries wait for their turn.
Before its first request after a death a point shows nothing: DI 5.2 promises nothing before the request. The time until a unit is asked at all (about 51 s at the start and after a common death on the 250-unit line below) is bounded for a site by `max_age` on every point that matters, a `timeout` near the real latency of the line, and fewer units per line (L3).
The device state tag lags the points: on the 250-unit line the dead are DEMOTED up to 7 minutes after their points are COMM_FAIL, and they hold nearly all of the line meanwhile (below). Slices 6 and 7 (commands) must therefore not rely on DEMOTED to refuse a write to a dead unit: they refuse on the points' COMM_FAIL or on the unit's failure count (L3; a note for those slices).
Consequences: measured by the simulated clock (review P2) in the contract's order of service, the known limits of this pull request (serving answering devices first is #147), with the default keys (time-out 1 s, retries 2, `demote_after` 3, back-off 10 s to 300 s), one point per unit in a 1 s group, on the simulated serial line (`scada-driver-line-perf-test`, `ctest --preset perf`; the bounds of the test sit just above these figures, so a regression fails it):
- 250 units at 38,400 bit/s, every fifth dead from the start, one dead unit returning at 30 min. The dead hold all of the line for about 8 minutes, and a live unit waits up to 102.8 s. Every dead unit is COMM_FAIL by 55 s after the start (J2), and all are DEMOTED at 467 s. Once they are, a live unit's worst gap is 2.03 s: the period and one floor probe (N3). The returned unit is RUNNING 111 s after its return; the bound is `backoff_max` plus the floor and the spacing of floor probes.
- The same line with all 250 answering until 2 min, when every fifth dies (J4): every dead unit is COMM_FAIL by 55 s after the death, all are DEMOTED at 587 s, and a live unit waits up to 102.8 s after the death (1.0 s before it, 2.03 s once all are DEMOTED). The returned unit is RUNNING 231 s after its return.
- The first line with the dead units in the 200 ms group, of a higher priority than the live ones' 1 s group: the dead go first by priority until they are demoted, but the 1 s polls still go between them; after the demotion their floor probes go one in 11 probe time-outs (N1; as due polls of the faster group they took 52.1 s of the live units' line at every round). COMM_FAIL by 54 s, all DEMOTED at 463 s, a worst live gap of 2.03 s, the returned unit RUNNING 107 s after its return.
- 25 units at 19,200 bit/s, every fifth dead: all dead COMM_FAIL 8 s after the start and DEMOTED at 47 s; the worst live gap is 10.3 s until then and 1.90 s after (a dead unit's windowed probes are no failures, J1, so each of its back-offs ends with a floor probe of the whole 1 s, which holds one poll). The returned unit is RUNNING 180 s after its return.
- With the hold of #147 at commit 78c68aa the first case measured a worst live gap of 2.00 s, COMM_FAIL by 77 s and all DEMOTED at 1,674 s: the trade-off #147 has to settle.
- Conformance test 12 (`TheProbeBudgetKeepsTheLivePeriod`): one live unit in a 1 s group and 20 dead ones, time-out 100 ms, back-off 1 s to 4 s: the live period at most 1.003 s.
Tested by `EngineRandom.TheInvariantsHoldForEverySeed` (the lead's request on the final pass, widened by review M2, M3 and M5): 100 random channels of 2 to 6 units on every transport, drawn with its own SplitMix64 arithmetic so that each seed builds the same channel on every standard library. Each unit is in the 200 ms, 1 s or 10 s group, some with a chain of two or three parts (a device whose only reads are a chain has no probe request and is probed by a cycle, R8), some with a second block in another group, some with a keep-alive or a pacing interval, and now and then a second device on unit 1, sharing its failure count or keeping its own (OWN_UNIT). Over 120 s each come random deaths, busy answers, silent companions, silent blocks and returns, and peer closes of a stream or noise on a serial line. Checked every 100 ms: no point of a silent unit, block or chain part GOOD past its verdict bound; every unit asked within its back-off, period and reconnect bounds plus the requests ahead of it; a unit that answers again RUNNING within its probe back-off and reconnect bounds plus the requests ahead of it; no value GOOD past the bound while its companion is silent; no spin (at most 2,000 engine steps in any 100 ms); no wake before the step that returned it; and a unit that answers has its value asked within the bound of the contract's order. The requests ahead of a unit, in the order of DI 6.2 step 3, are each unanswered request sent meanwhile, at most its time-out, and each answered one of a higher priority, or of the same priority once per unit and register, at most its exchange, each with the quiet time. These mutants each make it fail: a wake 1 ns later, a wake 100 ms before now, no verdict, no recovery of a probed unit, a device never polled. Two are killed by their targeted tests instead, since the random channels cannot show them within their 100 ms sampling: a keep-alive's time-out that arms no verdict (`AKeepAliveTimeOutArmsTheVerdictToo`), and a failed middle part written only at its chain's end (`AFailedMiddlePartIsWrittenBeforeItsChainEnds`; without the hold a chain's later parts follow at once). Its first run found three defects the targeted tests had not: a chain in progress held back by the K3 gate (a spin), the retry of a chain's last part held back while the chain's other samples waited, and a chain's failed part written only at the chain's end; the first two were in the hold, now #147.
The reading is point D-9 of #136, with the bound as proposed wording.

## D-139: The time-out that loses a connection is not counted toward demotion
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07), slice 3
Status: accepted (the lead, 2026-11-24)

Context: driver interface contract 7.3 (R-15): "After each addition, and before the timed-out device's own demotion count is applied, the connection is lost when the run holds at least `lost_after_timeouts` requests and its unit ids include every eligible unit id." It does not say whether the time-out that makes the connection lost is still counted toward its device's demotion (7.4), nor what holds when no unit is eligible.
Options: (a) that time-out is not counted; (b) it is counted after the check.
Decision: (a), the lead's ruling. `FailureTracker::read_timed_out` adds the time-out to the run first; when that loses the connection, the time-out is not counted toward demotion; otherwise it is counted as a failed request. With one unit, `lost_after_timeouts` 3 and `demote_after` 3, the third time-out loses the connection and the device keeps 2 failures; after the reconnect the run is empty, so the next time-out makes 3 and demotes it, with the run at 1. One dead unit among live ones is demoted without the connection being lost. With no eligible unit, the condition "its unit ids include every eligible unit id" holds as written, and the run's length alone decides.
Reason: "before" has a meaning only under (a); the point of R-15 is that a lost connection or gateway is handled as a lost connection, not as dead devices: a gateway outage demotes nobody.
Addition (the lead, 2026-11-24, on the review of PR 137, P1): values go bad through the lost connection, so its path is never skipped. When the run of time-outs finds the connection lost, the rules of a lost connection apply at once: every device CONNECTING, its points BAD with COMM_FAIL; then the reconnect follows. Any other close the driver makes itself (a broken frame on `tcp`, a lost side-effect read, a partial send) keeps tags and states only when the reconnect succeeds within `connect_timeout`; when that time passes first, the rules of a lost connection apply then, whatever the connect does after. A reconnect never clears failure counts or demotion (7.3: counts and states are kept), so a dead unit behind a peer that accepts connections is demoted in the end, and a demoted device stays demoted across a lost connection.
Case (the review, R9): with `lost_after_timeouts` 1 and one eligible unit, every time-out loses the connection, so the unit is never demoted; its values go bad through the lost connection (COMM_FAIL) and it is reconnected by the `reconnect` back-off. This is the intended reading.
Consequences: a dead single unit is demoted one request after the reconnect. The clarifying sentence and the case with no eligible unit are points D-1 and D-10 of #136; the loss applied at once for the run of time-outs, where 7.3 lists it among the closes that keep tags, is a further point there, as is a CS warning when `lost_after_timeouts` is below `demote_after` on a channel with one unit (R9).

## D-140: The channel engine's choices that the contract leaves open
Date: 2026-11-24
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07), slice 3
Status: accepted; the retry rule is the lead's ruling on D-12 of #136 (2026-11-24), the gateway answer its revised ruling (2026-11-24); the probe, chain, encode, carry, key and connect rules follow the review of PR 137 (R10, P8, P9, Q11 to Q14), the chain, gap, discard, wait and dead block rules its delta check (G1 to G4, G8, H1 to H7)

Context: driver interface contract 7.1 to 7.5 and CS 4.6 give the failure rules of a channel worker; `ChannelEngine` (scada/driver/channel_engine.h) applies them. DI 7.1 says "A read that times out is retried up to `retries` times"; CS 4.6 says `retries` are "Retries of a failed read". Not stated: how an exception answer counts, what the points of a request that failed show, when samples of a chain of companions are published, when an idle worker wakes for staleness, and what a probe's answer does to the run of time-outs.
Options: for retries, time-outs only, or every failed read; for the rest, the simplest reading that keeps "no resend of a write" and the side-effect rules.
Decision:
- Retried, up to `retries` times and within the poll group's turn (D-138): a time-out, and a broken or truncated frame on a serial line and on `rtu_over_tcp` (review H3). Never an exception answer (busy, either gateway code: a gateway has waited already, and a retry holds every unit behind it), never a side-effect read (6.6), a probe, or over a closed connection. On `tcp` a broken frame closes the connection (7.3), so there is nothing to retry. The request then counts once toward demotion (7.4).
- A frame of another request (an earlier transaction id, another unit id) is stale: dropped, and the request waits on to its own deadline; only bytes that may start its own answer start the second stage of 7.1. It ends as a time-out if nothing else comes.
- Busy never counts and is never retried (7.4, annex A, conformance test 19): the points keep their values and go STALE by `max_age`; CS's `busy_retries` has no effect until the next revision.
- A gateway's "did not respond" answer counts toward its unit's demotion, without a retry (the ruling). It does not add to the run of time-outs: it is a valid frame from the gateway, so it proves the connection, empties the run and resets the reconnect back-off like any valid answer (the lead's revised ruling, 2026-11-24, on the delta check of PR 137; DI 7.3). Its points go bad like those of any failed request.
- An encode that refuses a request (`EncodeResult::REFUSED`) ends it as failed without I/O (Q11): its points are written BAD with COMM_FAIL, it is not counted toward demotion or the run, and the engine takes the next choice; a refused probe counts as a failed probe.
- Devices with no shared unit (`CompiledDevice::OWN_UNIT`) each have a failure count of their own (H5; before, they shared one).
- The verdict does not wait for retries that wait for their turn (the lead's ruling J2): when the first attempt of a poll or of a keep-alive times out (a keep-alive too, review M1: a device that falls idle is next asked by a keep-alive), its points are written BAD with COMM_FAIL at the latest one period of its poll group, or `retries` times its time-out if that is longer, after that time-out, if its retries have not all been made by then (`early_verdicts`). A probe arms no verdict: a demoted device's points are COMM_FAIL already. The retries then go on as attempts to recover, and the first answer makes the points GOOD again. Demotion still counts the request once, when its attempts end. A dead unit's blocks are asked one after the other, each with its retries (its retry is its next poll, Q1), so each block's bound runs from its own first time-out; the unit's demotion, after `demote_after` failed requests, writes every point COMM_FAIL, so the last block of a dead unit goes bad at most one period plus `demote_after` x (1 + `retries`) x (time-out + quiet time) after the death (review M6, P2: five blocks, default keys, 9.0 s after a death at the start of a period by the simulated clock, and 9.87 s on tcp, 10.09 s on serial at 19,200 bit/s with the death in mid-period; `TheLastBlockOfADeadUnitGoesBadByItsDemotion`). Figures here and in D-138 are measured by the simulated clock, which a step leaves at the end of the exchange it ran, not by the test loop's time, which lags it by up to one time-out (review P2). DI 5.2 says "after its retries" and gives no bound in time; a point on #136.
- A request that fails after its retries writes its points BAD with COMM_FAIL (5.2, annex A); its device is demoted by its count as before. A device that becomes DEMOTED or CONNECTING has all its polled points written BAD with COMM_FAIL once.
- The samples of a chain of companions are held until its last request is in, and then processed together, companions first, in place and without allocation (P8). A part that fails after its retries is part of the chain: its points are BAD with COMM_FAIL, and the points that depend on them are processed with their companion's outcome, so a value whose companion failed goes BAD (DI 5.5, conformance test 8; review G1). A busy part publishes nothing of its chain, also when it is the first request of a cycle probe (H4); the parts that follow are read as planned, and the points keep their values until STALE; but a part that failed after its retries still has its points written BAD with COMM_FAIL (K4). A failed part's own points are written at once, not at the chain's end; its dependents at the chain's end. A chain cut by a close, a lost connection, a reconnect, a dropped chain (D-136) or a demotion publishes nothing of the parts it held (P9). A chain whose samples do not fit its buffer is counted (`chain_overflows`) and not published, never cut silently.
- A probe's answer, busy or not, recovers its unit. It neither empties the run of time-outs nor resets the reconnect back-off (the review, R10; 7.3: probes "neither add to the run nor reset it"), so only a poll's answer is the "valid answer" of 7.3's back-off; the two 7.3 sentences are a point on #136. The probe back-off is reset only by a poll that answered (D-139's tracker, Q3). A probe's time-out is at most 1 s (7.4), in the scheduler's bound as in the wait.
- A serial line keeps 3.5 characters, and at least 1.75 ms above 19,200 bit/s (the serial line rule of the Modbus protocol, G2), plus CS `turnaround`, of silence after every answer, from its last byte (`inter_frame_gap_ns`, no 20 ms floor), and the quiet time after a time-out or a broken answer: the larger of the margin (at least 20 ms) and that silence, `turnaround` included (`quiet_time_ns`, H2). Input is discarded at the start of a quiet time and again before the next send; when that discard finds bytes, the line was not quiet, and another quiet time follows (G3). When quiet times have kept finding bytes for `JAM_NS` (5 s, at any bit rate and `turnaround`; longer than a 255-byte answer at 1,200 bit/s), the line counts as jammed (`jams`, once) and requests are sent without waiting until a discard finds it quiet; with no request for at most 5 s plus one quiet time (J6; before, 256 rounds were 5.1 s at 9,600 bit/s and 257 s with a `turnaround` of 1 s). A frame not taken whole is a time-out on a serial line and a close on a stream (7.2, 7.3).
- A planned request that fails after its retries `demote_after` times in a row while its device answers other requests in between (a dead block of a live device, which demotion never ends) rests for its own back-off: `backoff`, doubling to `backoff_max`, and is polled once at the end of each (G8). Its points stay BAD with COMM_FAIL; an answer ends the rest, and so does a reconnect. No request of a chain of companions rests, first, middle or last (the lead's ruling K1, replacing J5): a chain is read whole every period. The cost, accepted: a dead part of a chain is attempted every period, with its retries; with the verdict rule its points are COMM_FAIL on time. In the contract's order of service (D-138) a chain whose dead part makes its cycle longer than its period keeps its group always due, and the channel's slower groups wait while the part is dead, by priority: a known limit with the order, #147. A resting request is not sent as a keep-alive either (K5). The contract has no rule for a dead block of a live device; the point is on #136.
- The worker's idle wait polls the transport's `poll_fd()` for POLLRDHUP only, on a stream, and nothing on a serial port; never POLLIN, which would wake it at once while bytes no exchange reads lie there. `closed_by_peer` learns of a close without reading (G4).
- On a stream, bytes after a complete frame are kept for the next answer (Q12); on a serial line they are discarded with the quiet time. A read that returns OK with 0 bytes is a close (Q14).
- The engine refuses, with its `status`, compiled keys out of range (Q13, H7): a `reconnect` min or `connect_timeout` not above 0, a max below the min, `bits_per_char` 0, a bit rate other than 0 (a stream) outside CS `baud`'s 300 to 4,000,000, a `turnaround` outside its 0 to 1 s, a device time-out or back-off not above 0, `demote_after` 0. Deadlines saturate in both directions.
- `step` blocks in the transport's connect up to `connect_timeout` (Q14); until the command slices (6, 7), nothing else waits on the worker. Those slices make the connect started and polled, so an order never waits on it.
- The engine asks the transport whether the peer closed before every send and at every boundary (7.3 "Idle close"), wakes when a point's `max_age` runs out, also while idle (7.5), and a close it makes itself reconnects at once when a valid answer came since the connect, else after the `reconnect` back-off (7.3).
- The exchange and the connection are not real-time (DI 2.5): `step`, the transport's calls and the functions that reach them carry no `SCADA_RT`; the processing they call does.
Reason: the ruling narrows CS's "failed reads" to what a retry can fix without holding a gateway's other units; the other choices are the smallest that meet the contract's tests and the review's runs.
Consequences: orders (writes, select and operate, READ_NOW) are not sent by the engine before the command slices (6, 7); identity checks and device restarts (7.6) come with slice 5; the real transports, and the worker's wait on the connection and the order eventfd, with slices 4 and 5 (transport.h states the rules they keep). Points for the next contract revision are on #136: D-12 (retries), the busy and `busy_retries` rule, the probe and the run (7.3), and the 20 ms rest of an answer on `tcp` and `rtu_over_tcp`.

## D-141: The real TCP and serial transports
Date: 2026-11-25
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07), slice 4
Status: accepted

Context: driver interface contract 7.2 and 7.3 state what a serial port and a TCP connection must do (exclusive open, no flow control, a rate the port refuses is a connect error of its own, `TCP_USER_TIMEOUT`, `SO_LINGER` 0, literal addresses), and `transport.h` (D-140) states what the engine needs of them: a connect up to its deadline that leaves the transport closed when it fails, `closed_by_peer` without reading, a send that is whole or PARTIAL, and a receive that reads once after its wait. Neither says which rates a port is set to, how a failed connect is told apart for the diagnostics, how the reads of a terminal are set up, or what the integration tests run against before the Modbus simulators (#105, #106) exist.

Decision:
- `connections.h` holds `TcpTransport`, `SerialTransport` and `SystemClock`. A transport owns its descriptor (`scada::Fd`) and reads the time from the `Clock` it is given. `last_error()` tells why the last connect failed (`ConnectError`: an address that is not a literal, a path not under `/dev/`, no such port, busy, no permission, not a terminal, a rate or a format the port refuses, refused, timed out, other with `last_errno()`), for the channel's diagnostics of slice 5; IoStatus is unchanged, so a refused rate is FAILED with ConnectError::RATE, as the engine expects.
- TCP: a socket opened non-blocking and close-on-exec, with TCP_NODELAY (a request is one frame) and TCP_USER_TIMEOUT. `tcp_user_timeout_ns` computes it: `lost_after_timeouts` times the largest `timeout` or `write_timeout` of the channel's devices, at least 10 s, in milliseconds rounded up. The connect is non-blocking and waits for POLLOUT in `ppoll` up to the deadline, then reads `SO_ERROR`: ECONNREFUSED is REFUSED, the deadline TIMEOUT, anything else FAILED; every failure closes the socket. The connect stays blocking for the engine (D-140, Q14): a connect polled by the worker's loop needs the loop of slice 5 and the order eventfd of slices 6 and 7, so it is not simpler now. A host is read by `inet_pton`, IPv4 first, then IPv6: no name, no brackets, no zone index; anything else is ConnectError::ADDRESS before any system call. `send` is one `send` with MSG_NOSIGNAL and MSG_DONTWAIT (again only when interrupted): all of it OK, some PARTIAL, none FAILED (EAGAIN), a reset or a closed peer CLOSED. An abortive close sets `SO_LINGER` 0 first.
- Serial: `open` with O_RDWR, O_NOCTTY, O_NONBLOCK and O_CLOEXEC by the configured path, so that a replugged adapter is found again by a stable name. Exclusive by `flock` (binds every process, root included) and TIOCEXCL (binds every later open without CAP_SYS_ADMIN). The exclusive mode belongs to the terminal, not to the descriptor, and outlives a close while another descriptor holds the terminal (the master of a pseudo-terminal): `close` clears it (TIOCNXCL), or the transport's own reopen would be refused. Raw 8-bit characters (CS A.1: `data_bits` 8): no canonical mode, echo, signals or translation, no IXON, IXOFF or CRTSCTS, CLOCAL and CREAD on, INPCK with a parity (a character with a parity error reads as 0 and the frame check fails). VMIN 1 and VTIME 0: with O_NONBLOCK a read of an empty port is EAGAIN; with VMIN 0 a terminal read returns 0 when empty, which is also the end of file of a port that went away. The settings are read back after `tcsetattr`, which succeeds when any part was taken: another rate is ConnectError::RATE, another parity or stop bits ConnectError::FORMAT. Low latency (ASYNC_LOW_LATENCY) is asked where the driver has it, best effort (`low_latency()`). Both queues are dropped (`tcflush`) after the open and before every close, abortive or not, so that a close never waits for output a held-off line does not take (7.2). The connect makes no wait, so its deadline is not used.
- Rates: the standard rates of the terminal interface from 300 to 4,000,000 bit/s that the C library defines (`is_standard_baud`). CS A.1 allows any `baud` from 300 to 4,000,000; another rate (250,000, say) would need a Linux-only interface (`termios2`, BOTHER) and is refused at connect with ConnectError::RATE, never set to the nearest rate (7.2). A point on #136.
- `receive` waits for POLLIN in `ppoll` up to the deadline and then makes one read that does not block, whatever the wait saw, so that a worker stalled past its deadline still reads what arrived. OK with bytes, TIMEOUT when nothing is readable, CLOSED at an end of file or an error (a reset, the user time-out, EIO of a port that went away). `closed_by_peer` is a `poll` with time-out 0 for POLLRDHUP (a stream), POLLHUP or POLLERR, and consumes nothing. `discard_input` reads and drops what is readable now, at most 64 KiB a call, so that a line that never falls quiet cannot hold the worker in it.
- `SystemClock` reads CLOCK_MONOTONIC and CLOCK_REALTIME through `libscada-common`.
- Tests: `scada-driver-transport-test` (integration) runs a channel engine against `scada-driver-test-sim`, a process that serves the test protocol's simulated bus (`tests/loopback.h`) on 127.0.0.1 or on a pseudo-terminal pair and reports every request and answer with its monotonic time; the other cases run against a socket or a pty master the test holds. The simulator takes its few faults on the command line (a silent unit, a close after n answers); the control socket and the faults of contract 14 are the Modbus simulators' (#105, #106). A pseudo-terminal keeps no parity, so the serial cases run with parity `none`, and a parity on a pty is the test of ConnectError::FORMAT.

Reason: the smallest transports that meet 7.2, 7.3 and `transport.h`, in POSIX calls where POSIX has them; the Linux-only parts (POLLRDHUP, TCP_USER_TIMEOUT, the rates above 38,400 bit/s, low latency) are the contract's own or best effort.

Revised after the review of PR 148 (Linux systems and C++, 2026-11-26):
- **Self-connection (M1, command safety).** A connect to one of the host's own addresses on a free port of the local port range can meet itself with nobody listening: every frame then comes back as its own answer, and a write would be confirmed with nothing executed. After `SO_ERROR` the connect compares the socket's local and peer address and port (`getsockname`, `getpeername`) and fails a self-connection with ConnectError::SELF. The check is tested twice: on a socket made to connect to itself (bound to a port, connected to the same port), and through the transport (`AConnectThatMeetsItselfIsRefused`, delta check 2): Linux gives `connect` the even ports of its local port range, so connects to a free even port of that range with nobody listening meet themselves now and then. The test reads the range from the kernel, connects until it sees SELF, at most 200,000 times, and fails if any try is OK or SELF never comes; the even-port choice is the kernel's, not ours. Here it took 235 to 42,474 tries (the most under asan-ubsan, under 1 s), and the reviewer's runs on the old head 325 to 21,198; the test prints its count in the pipeline's log. With the call of the check deleted, a try returns OK (local and peer port the same) and the test fails.
- **Waits (M2, M3, S4).** The time left to a deadline is 0 for any deadline at or before now, never a subtraction that can overflow (`receive` with a deadline of `INT64_MIN` is a TIMEOUT). A wait that saw input whose one read then finds nothing waits again while the deadline has not passed: only the deadline makes a TIMEOUT. The loop is `receive_loop`, apart from its system calls so that a test drives it. An interrupted non-blocking connect goes on like one in progress; the second `connect` that gave EALREADY is gone. No test forces EINTR from `connect` (a non-blocking connect returns before a signal can interrupt it on Linux), so treating it as an error would pass every test (delta check 5).
- **Serial (M4, M5).** The terminal check (`tcgetattr`) comes before the `flock`, so a wrong `device` is NOT_A_TTY and is never locked. Mark or space parity (CMSPAR) is cleared with the rest of the format and checked in the read-back. `close` clears TIOCEXCL (as before).
- **Small (S1, S5).** `last_errno` is the errno of the last failed system call since the last connect, 0 when none failed (a time-out or a refusal of the transport's own is no errno). Port 0 is refused before any system call (ConnectError::ADDRESS). An empty `into` is FAILED, a caller's fault (transport.h). The constructors refuse a temporary clock.
- **Tests that hold beside other tests (delta checks 1, 3, 6).** The test of a locked non-terminal takes a shared lock on /dev/null: shared locks of other test processes coexist with it, and the transport's exclusive one still fails against it. The serial gap test gives its device a time-out of 10 s, so that a stalled simulator gives no time-out whose late answer would pair with the next request. The full accept queue waits until its first connection is in the queue before the transport connects.
- **Tests that hold on any host (S6).** The partial send sets small socket buffers on both sides; the serial gap test pairs each request with the last answer stamped before it. The flush at close has no test: a pseudo-terminal passes output to its master asynchronously, so a test cannot tell a dropped frame from one in flight; the flush after the open has one.
- **Recorded, no code now.**
  - A send that takes nothing (FAILED, EAGAIN) is a closed connection to the engine (D-140), while PARTIAL on a serial port is a time-out. On a stream both close the connection, as 7.3 asks. On a serial port, FAILED should be the time-out PARTIAL is (a full output queue is no lost port), and a port that is gone is CLOSED anyway. The engine's handling of FAILED on a serial line is changed with the command slices (6, 7), where a write that takes nothing is REJECTED with nothing sent (7.3 "Idle close").
  - `close` may wait on some USB serial adapters despite the flush (their drivers wait for the UART to drain); HUPCL and low latency are left as the port had them, apart from the low latency asked at open; IoStatus returns carry no `[[nodiscard]]` (the interface of D-140 is unchanged).

Consequences: the worker of slice 5 builds a transport from the channel's keys; `host`, `port`, `device`, `baud`, `parity` and `stop_bits` are protocol keys (CS A.1) that the compile step (slice 8) puts into the compiled channel. A write deadline grows with `write_timeout_per_value`, which is not compiled yet; the user timeout covers it when it is. Points on #136: rates outside the standard set, the zone index of an IPv6 literal, the user timeout of a channel whose largest deadline is a list's write, and that the RTU simulator over a pseudo-terminal runs with parity `none`.

## D-142: The driver process: threads, attach, the writes after attach, the channel watchdog and the stop
Date: 2026-11-26
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07), slice 5a
Status: accepted; revised the same day by the review of PR 150 (M1 to M12) and its delta check. The cut of slice 5 into 5a (process) and 5b (diagnostics) is the lead's (2026-11-25). Review by a person: restart and stop behaviour, the watchdog's exit, the writes before the first request.

Context: driver interface contract 2.3 and 2.4 give the threads, their calls and their tag owners, 2.6 the channel watchdog, 9.1 the writes after every attach and 10 the stop. It leaves open how a worker waits, how the stall limit counts line time, when the service manager hears "ready", how long a stop may take, what the process does with a signal it has no use for yet, and what happens when the whole process is paused. The coding standard (7) allows `std::_Exit` only in `main`.

Decision:
- **The process.** `Driver` (`driver.h`) is the process in `libscada-driver`; an executable builds the compiled configuration and the transports and calls `run`, once (a second call fails). The settings are checked at construction: a process name, a heartbeat period above 0 and at most 10 s; a run with other settings fails at once. `DriverSettings` carries the retain directory, which attach passes on (TD 12). `exit_status(RunEnd)` gives 0 stopped, 1 failed, 4 stall; 2 is a usage error of the executable. The test executable `scada-drv-test` is the first such `main`; its logic is in `drv_test_support`.
- **Signals.** `main` first calls `block_stop_signals`, before any thread starts, the logger's included: SIGTERM, SIGINT and SIGHUP are blocked on every thread and taken by the main thread from a signalfd. SIGPIPE is blocked too and never taken, so a write to a standard error or a socket nobody reads fails with EPIPE instead of ending the process (delta 2; scada-sim does the same, scada-tagd leaves SIGPIPE at its default). Every other signal keeps its default, so SIGABRT (a service manager's watchdog abort) and the fault signals end the process at once with a core (review M8). A child process the driver may one day start inherits this mask: it must reset its signal mask (and SIGPIPE) before it runs anything.
- **The service manager.** As in scada-tagd, a notify environment that names a service manager wrongly (a bad NOTIFY_SOCKET, an empty WATCHDOG_PID) fails the start with "invalid environment", and a READY=1 that cannot be sent fails it with "cannot report readiness to the service manager" (review M10). A failed watchdog notification is logged once and the process runs on; STOPPING=1 is best effort.
- **Threads.** The main thread and one worker per channel, a disabled channel included: every tag has one writing thread (TD 13.2), and a disabled channel's worker writes 9.1 for its tags and then only waits for the stop (review M2). It is never connected and has no transport (CS 4.6); slice 8's live enable can hand it an engine. A worker is a POSIX thread with a stack of `SCADA_DRIVER_WORKER_STACK_BYTES` (2.4: 256 KiB; 8 MiB in the sanitizer builds). The command thread comes with slices 6 and 7.
- **Owners** (review M3). The main thread learns each channel's owner from the channel's first tag, checks that it is a thread of this process, and refuses two channels on one owner before any worker starts ("channels a and b both write owner o"). After the workers start it resolves every point tag `<device>.<point>` (CS 12.4), disabled devices included, and checks its type and that its owner is the channel's ("tag t of channel c is owned by o"). Either refusal fails the start, exit status 1. So does an owner of the process that no channel writes ("owner o of this process is written by no channel"), whose tags would otherwise keep the previous life's values, GOOD (the lead's ruling, delta 3). The one exception is the main thread's own owner, named like the process, for the `sys.proc` tags (D-146).
- **9.1, first** (review M6). After attach, each worker writes every tag of its owner, by owner slot (`scada_owner_info`, `scada_tag_handle`), before anything else: its value as stored, BAD with NOT_INIT and source time 0 (the library stores its own update time with LOCAL_TIME, TD 13); a STRING keeps its text; a tag marked RESTORED is left as it is. Then one `scada_tag_notify`. It covers tags no point names. A read or write the library refuses fails the start (review M9). The window that remains, in which a tag of the previous life reads GOOD after the attach, is the time from the attach to the end of its owner's walk: one resolve and one tag information per channel, the start of the worker thread, and the slots before the tag in its owner's walk. A window of zero needs the tag library (attach marking the owner's tags); that is a point for #136.
- **Seeding** (review M4). After 9.1 and the resolve, the worker reads each point's tag and gives its engine that value as the point's last value (`ChannelEngine::seed_point`), so that a COMM_FAIL before the first read keeps the value as stored (5.2 "keeps its last value") and never writes 0.
- **Calls on the main thread** (review M12). `device_caps` and `point_caps` run on the main thread (DI 2.3). The worker builds its engine with the caps the main thread asked (`ChannelEngine` with a span of `DeviceCaps`). The test executable checks the thread of every call of the protocol.
- **The loop.** `step`, the progress counter, one `scada_tag_notify` if anything was written (one per delivery, reference 5.3; an unchanged value is not written), then a wait in `ppoll` until the time the step returned, at most a third of the stall limit (2.6), on the worker's stop eventfd and, on a stream only, the connection for POLLRDHUP (transport.h), then the counter again. Refused tag writes and notifies are counted; the main thread logs a change of a channel's count at its heartbeat step, at most once a second per channel ("tag writes refused"; the lead, 2026-11-26), until the queues of 5b-3. 11.1 has no system tag for them; the tag is a point for #136.
- **The stall limit** (`stall_limit_ns`): three times the longest single wait, the largest request deadline of the channel's enabled devices or `connect_timeout`, at least 1 s. A request deadline is 7.1's two stages at their longest: a request of the device's `max_frame_bytes` on the line, the larger of `timeout` and `write_timeout`, an answer of `max_frame_bytes`, and the margin.
- **The watchdog.** The main thread writes the heartbeat every `heartbeat_period_ns` (100 ms by default), the service manager's watchdog notification at half its interval, and checks every worker's counter at each heartbeat. A check that comes more than two heartbeat periods after the one before means the main thread did not run for that time (SIGSTOP, a debugger, the freezer, a paused machine, or a thread not scheduled): only the time it lost, the gap less one period, is added to every worker's last move, and the history is kept (review M7, delta 1). So a pause of the whole process is no stall, and repeated short pauses never hide a worker that is stuck: it is found once its own time, less the pauses, reaches the limit. "process paused" is logged at most once every 10 s, with the number of pauses since. The watchdog's own clock starts with the workers' (when they are made), so a pause before its first check is credited too. Heartbeat results other than OK and RETIRING are logged once and fail the run.
- **The start.** READY=1 is sent once every worker runs (its engine built, or idling for a disabled channel). A worker that is not running within 10 s fails the start. The watchdog notification is sent only after READY=1. Recorded: a start that fails at READY (the notification not sent) has already polled the devices for that time; and the transport factory runs for every channel before any worker starts, so the last channel's 9.1 writes wait for the others' factories (26 ms for the last of 100 channels, measured by the review).
- **A stall**: the record `driver-stall` with the channel's name, the logger flushed (at most 1 s); no heartbeat and no watchdog notification from then on; the other workers asked to stop and given their request deadline (a third of their stall limit) plus 1 s, then "workers stopped after a stall" with their count; then `run` returns STALLED and `main` ends the process with `std::_Exit(4)` without destroying the Driver, which would wait for the stalled thread.
- **The stop** (DI 10, R-14; review M5). The stop eventfd is also the transport's wake descriptor (`Transport::set_wake`): it ends a receive and a connect in progress with ABANDONED. A read in flight is given up at once, nothing is counted and nothing written, and its points keep their values; the worker then stops at that boundary and closes its connection in order (a FIN). After an abandoned read whose answer left unread bytes, the kernel closes a TCP connection with a reset, not a FIN. The worker does not wait the quiet time of a serial line after an abandoned read: it closes the port. A send is a single call that does not block (connections.h); a write request is another matter: slice 6 takes the wake away (`set_wake(-1)`) while it waits for a write's answer, so that a write in flight is never abandoned (R-14), and makes the stop budget at least the longest write deadline. Nothing enforces that yet: there are no writes. The stop budget is 2 s (`STOP_BUDGET_NS`), below the service manager's stop time the installed unit sets (#105); it does not allow for a pause of the process during the stop or for a slow close of a serial port.
- **One bounded stop for every end** (review M1). A stop signal, a failed start, a retiring generation (until slice 8) and a refused heartbeat all take the same stop: the workers are asked to stop, the heartbeat and the stall check go on, and the workers are joined as they return. A worker still not returned when its stall limit or the budget passes is logged (`driver-stop-overrun` with its channel) and left: `run` returns STALLED for a stop and FAILED for a failure, `joined()` is false, and `main` ends the process with `std::_Exit(exit_status(end))`. No thread is joined that has not returned; a Worker that has not returned is never destroyed, and the context is not detached under it.
- **SIGHUP**, the reload of 12, is taken from the signalfd, logged as ignored, and does not stop the process until slice 8.
- **Logging**: only the main thread logs, through the logger of `libscada-common`; the workers log nothing until the per-thread queues of slice 5b-3 (11.3).
- **scada-tagd going away.** TD 10.1 keeps existing mappings working when scada-tagd stops (`scada_tag_status` then reports SCADA_E_TAGD_DOWN), and DI 9.2 and 8.13 keep the old generation running when no RETIRING comes. The driver needs nothing more: it runs on, and a new generation reaches it as RETIRING. Reporting the daemon's state comes with the system tags (5b) or the generation change (slice 8).
- Owner names are not the driver's: it resolves tags, and checks their owners only against each other. The names in the tag set must agree with TD 1 (one owner per process is named like the process, several are `<process>.<writer>`), which CS 12.3 does not: a point for the lead (2026-11-26), on #136.

Reason: the smallest process that meets 2.3, 2.4, 2.6, 9.1 and 10 with the engine of slice 3 and the transports of slice 4, with every end bounded.

Consequences: `TagSink::device_state` writes nothing: the system tags of 11.1 are written by the worker after each step (slice 5b-1, D-146); identity checks and restarts (7.6) come with 5b-2; the command thread and the orders with slices 6 and 7; the compile step, bit tags, reload and the generation change with slice 8.

Tests:
- `scada-driver-process-integration-test` runs scada-tagd, `scada-driver-test-sim` and `scada-drv-test` as processes, with a listener standing in for the service manager, and observes the tags, the exit and the log:
  - 9.1 and the start: the writes of 9.1 before READY and before a request is answered; a disabled device and a disabled channel written NOT_INIT; a restored tag left alone and kept through a COMM_FAIL; a kill (OWNER_DOWN asserted) and a restart that keeps the value; two channels on one owner and a tag of another channel's owner refused; the exit statuses of `--help`, a usage error and a missing tag.
  - Notifications: one cycle of `scada_tag_notify` per change; READY, WATCHDOG and STOPPING; a wrong or dead notify socket.
  - The stop and signals: a read in flight abandoned within 1 s and the connection closed with a FIN; an idle worker stopped within 500 ms; a peer's close seen within 600 ms; a stop with a hung worker ending with status 4 within the budget, the heartbeat going on; the stall check during a stop; a retiring generation with a hung worker ending with status 1; SIGINT stops, SIGHUP does not, SIGABRT ends the process by the signal.
  - The watchdog (conformance test 2): a hung worker ends the process with status 4 after 3 to 5 s with no heartbeat after the stall; a stall stops the other worker; a spinning worker stalls; a pause of the whole process past the stall limit does not; an idle worker and a connecting one do not, over 20 s.
  - Conformance tests 16 and 17 at tag level: a lost and a refused connection with COMM_FAIL on every device; STALE after `max_age`, cleared by the next read.
- Unit tests: the engine with an abandoned read and connect, a seeded point, too few caps; the receive loop's wake; a TCP receive and connect abandoned by an eventfd; the settings, a second run, the exit statuses and the signal mask; the value conversions; the command line of `scada-drv-test`.
- Added by the delta check:
  - short repeated pauses with a hung worker, still found within 4 s;
  - an owner no channel writes;
  - 9.1 written before the resolve (a start that fails at the resolve has written its tags NOT_INIT);
  - a peer's close during the idle wait;
  - SIGPIPE (a write to a pipe with no reader gives EPIPE);
  - the TagWriter in process against scada-tagd: STRING writes wait for the notify, a point without a tag is skipped, a refused write is counted, 9.1 by owner slot keeps the values and refuses another process's owner.
  - The hung-worker tests wait for the hung channel's request before the stop (a stop before its first step ended the run with 0). The cycle counter is waited for, the pause test waits for READY, and the dead notify socket has a short absolute path.
- Added by the final list:
  - the watchdog's arithmetic in a class of its own (`StallWatch`, src/stall_watch.h), unit-tested on a fake clock: a stuck worker found at its limit; a pause credited exactly the gap less one period (not the whole gap, not twice); a gap of exactly two periods not a pause; a pause before the first check credited; repeated short pauses never hiding a stuck worker; a moving or returned worker never stalled;
  - a pause of a process whose only channel is disabled; the watchdog not fed before READY=1 (a resolve of 256 tags beats once before it); a STRING write past the room the TagWriter reserved, refused and counted;
  - the simulator outlives a connection reset while it writes answers. It ended on SIGPIPE before, and a pause of the driver makes exactly that reset (a read given up across the pause, closed with its answer unread): the driver then ran on with nothing to connect to, its tags COMM_FAIL and never OWNER_DOWN. That was the failure of the pause test under asan-ubsan with load (the lead's 3 of 3); the start-time hole gave its other failure there, a pause without a record (3 of 15 runs on the old code). With both fixed: 30 clean runs under asan-ubsan with twice the cores in busy loops.
- Not tested, and why:
  - The log of a changed refused count ("tag writes refused", at most once a second per channel): a refused write cannot be provoked in the driver process through the real library once the owner checks have passed; the count itself is tested on the TagWriter.
  - Whether a stop of the process lands before the watchdog's first check cannot be chosen from outside the process (the window is microseconds after "driver running"); the unit test covers that case.
  - The two progress moves of the loop, after the step and after the wait, are equivalent mutants for the watchdog: one move a loop bounds the time between moves by a step plus a wait, at most two thirds of the stall limit. Both are kept for margin.
  - A refused heartbeat and a refused 9.1 write after the owner check cannot be provoked through the real library.
- What is proven: conformance test 1 for every protocol call the process makes (configure, plan, the caps, encode, frame_state, decode); test 2 with a hung, a spinning, an idle and a connecting worker, and a pause, with the limit bounded on both sides for one channel; tests 16 and 17 at tag level only.

## D-143: Tag database contract 1.1: owner names, the meaning of SUBSTITUTED, the retain age bound
Date: 2026-11-26
Phase / issue: 2, #151 (P2-14); with #89 and #136
Status: the three changes were approved by the product owner on 2026-11-26 (#151; #136, the committee's recommendation on owner names; #89, both comments). The contract text was reviewed by the committee of the lead session (SCADA, Linux systems, C++) on PR #152; the lead ruled on the interface of the bound, the difference from reference 5.6 and the sentences of other contracts. For review by a person (contract change; restart behaviour).

Context: contract 1.0 is frozen (D-66); a change is a layout version, a minor version with a migration note, or an editorial revision (D-66 f). Three points were open against it. Section 2 named the only owner of a process like the process and several owners `<process>.<writer>`, so configuration schemas 12.3, which gives every process a plain owner beside its thread owners, could not be built, and an owner's name, ring and retain file would change when its process gained or lost a writing thread (#136). Section 6 defined SUBSTITUTED as "entered by a person", narrower than driver interface ruling R-6 and configuration schemas 4.7 (#89, second comment). Section 12 left a bound on the age of a restored value to phase 2 (D-59, point 2; D-74; #89, first comment).

Decision: tag database contract 1.1, one revision, layout version 1 unchanged.
- Section 2: an owner is named `<process>` or `<process>.<writer>` with a `<writer>` that is not empty; the number of owners of a process has no effect on their names; by convention the plain name belongs to the thread that writes the tags of the process itself. `check_owner_names` (`libs/scada-tag/src/tag_set.cpp`) loses its counting pass; a foreign prefix (`px.b` or `q.b` for `p`), a name that only starts like the process (`px`), another process's name that does not begin with `p.` and an empty writer (`p.`) stay refused (with processes `p` and `p.a`, an owner `p.a` of `p` is valid, as under 1.0), with one message: "an owner of process 'p' must be named 'p' or <process>.<writer> with a writer that is not empty". Configuration schemas 0.7 cites 1.1 in its header, 12.3 and 12.6, and states that an owner keeps its name when its process gains or loses other owners.
- Section 6: SUBSTITUTED marks a value not from the source: entered by a person, substituted by configuration, or reported by the device as forced. Same bit. A consumer tells an entry by a person by its journal record; an HMI must not show the flag as "entered by an operator". The driver interface contract and `libscada-driver` set it for the two new cases with #154, not in this revision.
- Section 12: the owner's process passes the bounds to its library at attach; the library restores a value whose age is greater than its bound, or unknown, as BAD with RESTORED (carried flags, value and source time on file) before the region is `ready`; at most the bound, or without a bound, UNCERTAIN as in 1.0. The configuration refuses a bound on mode, authority, counter and shelving tags (CF-508); the library does not check. `scada-tagd` takes a record that is exactly what such a restore wrote as no change, as it does the UNCERTAIN one (D-59, B1), so the file keeps the value until the owner writes the tag. It judges by the record's shape, so this holds for every owner, with or without bounds: a BAD + RESTORED record with the value and source time on file, which 1.0 removed (D-45), is kept and restores UNCERTAIN at the next restart unless a bound applies. A BAD record with RESTORED and another value is looked up in the files as a restore from another file; when the look is complete and no file holds it, it leaves the file (D-45); when the look is incomplete, the file keeps it and the look repeats. The age the bound is judged on is that of the file's stamp, not of the value (D-59, B2); its consequences are stated in section 12, and whether a quiet owner's file is stamped again is for the owner to decide in #89. The bounds are not in the tag set, in `config_hash` or in shared memory (D-74).
- Section 13, ruled by the lead on PR #152: `scada_retain_bound { const char *tag_name; int64_t max_age_ns; }` and `scada_tag_attach_retain(process_name, opts, bounds, n, out)`, which is `scada_tag_attach_opts` with the bounds (`opts` NULL: the defaults). A new function, not a field of `scada_attach_opts`, which has no size member (D-66 f names a function as a minor change). `SCADA_E_INVALID` before any attach step for `bounds` NULL with `n` above 0, `n` above 1,000,000, a NULL or malformed name (read up to 128 bytes), a bound not above 0 and two bounds for one name. A bound whose name is no RETAIN tag of the caller is not used and is no error, and is counted in `scada_retain_status_t.bounds_unused` (the former `reserved` word), so that it is not silent. A bound is judged once, at the restore; the library keeps neither the bounds nor the names; every attach passes its bounds again.
- Implemented in this pull request: the owner-name rule and its tests. The retain part of section 12 and 13 (the bound in the library, `scada_tag_attach_retain`, `bounds_unused`, the kept record in `scada-tagd`) and its restore tests ("older than the bound", "unknown age", "within the bound") come with the pull request that closes #89. Until it is merged no owner passes bounds, `scada-tagd` and every owner come from one build of the install tree, and `retain_max_age` keeps warning CW-510; the contract says so in its header, in sections 12 and 13 and in the migration note.

Reason: the count-free rule is the committee's recommendation (#136): with a count, a second writing thread added by an upgrade renames the owner and moves its retained modes, authority and alarm states through the moved-tag path; `<process>.main` would need a reserved word and change the driver interface contract 2.4 and D-75. The owner name carries no authority: the writer check, file ownership, attach and heartbeat go by process index and uid. The wider SUBSTITUTED and the age bound are the proposals of #89 as approved.

Consequences:
- Every tag set valid under 1.0 is valid under 1.1 with the same `config_hash`; `TagSetHash.ATagSetValidUnderContract10KeepsItsHash` pins three hashes computed by the 1.0 library (commit 0bc8052).
- A 1.0 library attaches to a generation built by a 1.1 `scada-tagd` from a tag set with `p` beside `p.w`: no attach path applies the naming rule (only `resolve` in `tag_set.cpp`, reached from `validate`, `config_hash`, `build_header` and `GenerationHost::start`, does), and the run in the pull request of #151 shows it. A 1.0 `scada-tagd` refuses such a tag set, so `scada-tagd` and `scada-conf` are built to 1.1 before one is used.
- Slice 5b of #104 (the `sys.proc` tags of a driver's main thread) and the tag-set generator of #101 can build on the 1.1 rule.
- Reference 5.6 says a restored value reads UNCERTAIN with RESTORED; under a bound it can read BAD with RESTORED. Recorded as a difference in contract section 17, for the next revision of the reference (accepted by the lead).
- Configuration schemas 0.7 also ties CW-510 and 5.2 to the library part of the bound (#89), not to the version, and cites the wider SUBSTITUTED in 4.7 and difference 16. The driver interface contract and `libscada-driver` set SUBSTITUTED for the two new cases with #154.

## D-144: The zone table searches every interval that may hold a local time
Date: 2026-11-27
Phase / issue: 2, #158; follows D-130
Status: accepted for review

Context: `tzif_fuzz` aborted on `main` (the run for b67f666) at the round-trip check: a local time converted to an instant other than the one it was written from, without being marked uncertain. The input is a valid version 2 zone file. It has three changes in mid-1970: to +15:04:16, 38,656 s later to +18:08:23, 128 s later back to +15:04:16, and then the footer `CET-3` (+3 h). `ZoneTable::convert` looked at two intervals on each side of the one that holds the local time read as UTC (`SEARCH_WINDOW`). That is enough only while every interval of constant offset is longer than the span of the offsets (under 51 h, RFC 9636). Here the interval that holds the answer was three places away. The only match found was the footer's interval, a later instant, given as certain.

The same check then aborted in the fuzz job of PR 159 on a second input: changes 2 min and then 1 s apart (+2 h, +1 h, +2 h) and a footer without daylight time at -3 h. Two causes act there: (a) `from_tzif` added the footer's change at the last transition's own instant, which left an interval of length zero; (b) the window of two, as above.

Decision:
- `from_tzif` computes the window each table needs: the largest number of interval begins that lie within less than the span of the table's offsets (the largest minus the smallest, with 0) of one another, at least 2. `convert` searches that many intervals on each side.
- A table that would need more than 16 is refused, so that a conversion, which runs on a worker for each sample with a device time, stays bounded. `to_utc` does one binary search over the intervals and then looks at no more than 33 intervals (16 on each side and the one found) twice: once for the matches, once for a skipped hour. The header of `ZoneTable` states the bound.
- The refusal is DATA_CORRUPT ("the zone file's changes of offset are too close together for its offsets"), as for any other file a table cannot be built from. INVALID_ARGUMENT is documented by `build` for a name that is not a zone name; the configuration compile reports both file cases as an unusable zone file. `from_tzif` now documents its failures.
- The span bounds the distance in both directions at once, so it can be up to twice what one direction needs. It refuses more hand-made files than it must, never a real one, and keeps the rule simple. It is left as it is.
- A change at the instant the last interval begins replaces that interval (`ZoneTable::add_change`), so no interval is empty; if the offset before it is the same, the two merge. A change at the begin of the table's only interval takes its place; no file reaches that today, since every change lies after the table's start.
- The fuzz target's check is right and is not changed. Both inputs are in the corpus (`changes-closer-than-offsets`, `footer-at-the-last-transition`).
- Tests (`zone_table_test.cpp`):
  - each input as a unit test, and its mirror west of UTC, where the answer lies after the interval found;
  - 16 close changes taken and 17 refused;
  - a footer change back to the offset before;
  - a seeded property test: on 4,000 generated tables, `to_utc` agrees with `ZoneTable::to_utc_searching_all`, a search of every interval written apart from `convert`.
  These fail for each mutant the review of PR 162 found: either side of the window fixed at 2, the span without 0 or as `highest`, a cap other than 16, `>=` for `>`, the replacement without the merge.

Reason: the window follows from the table itself, so a correct answer does not depend on how real zones happen to be laid out. The bound keeps the real-time cost of a conversion fixed.

Consequences: real zone files are unaffected. On the build machine no zone file has two changes closer together than the span of its own offsets, so every table keeps the window of 2. `scada-driver-zones-test`, which compares every zone with the C library, passes unchanged. A hand-made or damaged file of that kind is either converted correctly (the repeated local time gives its earliest instant, marked uncertain) or refused, never converted wrongly.

## D-145: Drivers set SUBSTITUTED (tag database contract 1.1)
Date: 2026-11-26, revised 2026-11-27 after the review of PR 157
Phase / issue: 2, #154 (P2-15); follows #151 (D-143)
Status: for review by a person (contract change: driver interface contract 0.7).

Context: tag database contract 1.1, section 6, gives SUBSTITUTED the meaning that ruling R-6 of the driver interface contract asked for: a value substituted by configuration, and a value the device reports as forced, besides one entered by a person. The driver interface contract 0.6 and `libscada-driver` held the flag back "until tag database 1.1" (decision D-131: device flags masked to OUT_OF_RANGE, OVERFLOW, BLOCKED and TEST).

Decision:
- One source gives SUBSTITUTED: the `substitute` action of `on_invalid` or of an invalid marker (CS 4.7) writes the configured value UNCERTAIN with SUBSTITUTED.
- The flag stays with the value (review R1). `PointState::substituted` records that the value held is a configured substitute. Every write that keeps it carries the flag, with that write's own quality: after COMM_FAIL, ABSENT and NO_VALUE, after a stale check, in the writes of driver interface contract 9.1 (`kept_flags` in `tag_writer.cpp`), and after a restart, where `TagWriter::seed` reads the flag from the stored value. The next value read from the device ends it. A companion does not pass it to a dependent point (`companion_flags`): the flag describes the companion's value.
- Two masks (review R2). `CONFIG_FLAGS_MASK` is the flags the configuration may give in a marker or a `quality_from` map: OUT_OF_RANGE, OVERFLOW, BLOCKED and TEST, as CS 4.7 allows. `DEVICE_FLAGS_MASK` is the flags a protocol's mapping may give: the same four. Neither holds SUBSTITUTED; the last point of D-131 on the mask stands. `keep_last` stays without SUBSTITUTED (contract 5.2).
- A value the device reports as forced is room only (review R3), until a protocol carries a value with a mark (#136). A NO_VALUE or DEVICE_INVALID answer carries no value of the device's, so the flag would land on a kept value the device did not give. Row 4 of contract 5.2 no longer lists SUBSTITUTED.
- Bits of a word (`bits`, CS 4.7): on a substitute they keep the values last read, and `ProcessedSample` gives them the word's quality and flags, SUBSTITUTED included (`sample_processing.h`). Nothing writes bit tags yet (`TagWriter` writes the point's own tag only). The slice that writes them decides whether a bit of a substituted word carries the flag.
- The driver interface contract goes to 0.7: it cites tag database 1.1, 5.2 states the rule without "until 1.1", R-6 says what is in effect, and 2.5 no longer names TD 1.1 for the wake-only form of #116, which 1.1 does not have.
- The golden vector of `substitute` (`tests/golden/driver-vectors.txt`, shared with #105 and #106) expects SUBSTITUTED,LOCAL_TIME.

Reason: the code and the contracts follow tag database 1.1; nothing read the flag from a driver before. A flag that a kept value lost at the next failed poll would tell an operator the substitute was a measurement.

Consequences: the writer of the driver process (`tag_writer.*`, PR 150) is on `main`, so the flag reaches the tag database; `tests/integration/tag_writer_integration_test.cpp` shows that the writes of 9.1 keep it. `DriverProcess.ASubstituteIsSubstitutedAndAValueReadEndsIt` (`tests/integration/driver_integration_test.cpp`, #154) tests `on_invalid: substitute` through a running driver process and a real tag, with `scada-drv-test --substitute`: the substitute is UNCERTAIN with SUBSTITUTED, the stored value keeps the flag after the stop, and a value read by the next driver ends it. The fuzz target `sample_processing_fuzz` checks both ways that a write has the flag exactly when its value is a configured substitute.

## D-146: The system tags of the process, of a channel and of its devices
Date: 2026-11-26; rebased on the reviewed slice 5a (D-142) on 2026-11-27
Phase / issue: 2, #104 (P2-07), slice 5b-1
Status: accepted; the cut of slice 5b into 5b-1 (system tags), 5b-2 (identity and restart) and 5b-3 (log queues) is the lead's (2026-11-26). Review by a person: it adds writes after every attach (9.1) and at the stop.

Context: driver interface contract 11.1 lists the system tags and their types, and says states are written when they change and counters, times and averages at most once a second. CS 12.5 names them and gives them to the channel's owner. Neither says what counts as an error answer, when `response_ms` starts and ends, how `requests_per_s` is windowed, when the channel's `state` is "connecting", or what a worker does with a system tag the tag set lacks.

Decision:
- This slice writes, per channel: `state`, `connects`, `refused_connects`, `secured` (always false in phase 2, reference 13.3), `period.<group>` for every poll group of the channel, and `requests_per_s`. Per device, disabled devices included: `state`, `last_ok_time`, `requests`, `errors`, `timeouts`, `response_ms`, `requests_per_scan`, `pacing_limited`, `last_status`, `exceptions`, `refusals`, `sentinels`, `invalid` and `points_absent`.
  - `identity`, `identity_ok` and `restarts` come with 5b-2.
  - `selections_lost` and `last_command_stage` come with the command slices.
  - `refresh_failures` comes with phase 5.
  - The validator generates `restarts`, `selections_lost` and `last_command_stage` for every device, `refresh_failures` for a device that a refreshed output writes, and `identity` and `identity_ok` for a device with `identify`. The driver does not resolve them, and they read BAD with NOT_INIT, from the walk of 9.1, for the driver's whole life until their slice (PR 159 review S2).
- Per process, written by the main thread as the owner named like the process (`drv-<protocol>`, CS 12.5; the count-free names of tag database contract 1.1, D-143): every signal of 11.1. `realtime` is false: a driver has no real-time thread (DI 2.5). `config_id` is 0 until the compile step gives the driver one (slice 8); `config_state` is 0 (loading) from the start, 1 (applied) once READY=1 is sent, and 0 again at the stop, once every worker returned (review M3), so a state read with `config_id` 0 matches no configuration `scada-conf` waits for. What `config_state` says across a reload is slice 8's. `stale_outcomes`, `answers_dropped`, `safe_unjournalled` and `log_dropped` are 0 until the slices that count them (the command slices, the fail-safe slice, 5b-3).
- The process owner: right after the attach, before any worker starts, the main thread finds the owner named like the process, writes 9.1 for it by owner slot (so it is that owner's first writer, TD 13.2), resolves its `sys.proc` tags with the type and owner checks, and writes them. A tag set without that owner or one of its tags fails the start ("the tag set has no owner p for the sys.proc tags"). A channel whose tags have that owner is refused ("channel c writes owner p, the main thread's"): it would be a second writer. The unused-owner rule of D-142 does not apply to it.
- The system tags are written through `DriverSettings::system_write`, the library's `scada_tag_write` unless a test gives another. `scada-drv-test --refuse-write TAG VALUE SKIP` gives one that refuses the writes of VALUE to TAG once SKIP of them were written, so that every refused-write path runs in a process test.
- Refused writes of the `sys.proc` tags follow D-142's rules for the point tags (review M4). A refused write at the start fails the start ("the writes of the sys.proc tags of p were refused"): the tags would keep the previous life's values. One while running is counted and logged at the heartbeat, with the workers' counts ("tag writes refused", field OWNER). The refused writes of the stop are logged once, as soon as every worker has returned. A disabled channel's worker stores its refused count as a running one's does.
- The main thread resolves every tag of this list after the point tags of the channel. The checks are those of D-142 for a point tag, but in code of their own (`resolve_one`, review S3). A tag the tag set lacks, one of another type, or one of another owner than the channel's fails the start (`RunEnd::FAILED`, exit status 1) with "the tag set has no tag t", "tag t is not of its type (11.1)" or "tag t of channel c is owned by o, not by the owner of channel c". Such a tag set was not built for this configuration (CS 12.5). The handles reach the worker with its point handles (`Worker::begin`).
- A channel's owner is that of its first point tag, as in D-142, or, for a channel without one, that of its `sys.drv.<channel>.state` tag.
- Values are written GOOD, with no flags and source time 0 (the library stores its own update time with LOCAL_TIME, TD 13). The walk of 9.1 by owner slot (D-142) writes them NOT_INIT with the other tags of the owner; once the worker has its engine, every one is written with its current value, then one notify, all before READY=1 (9.1: "Diagnostic tags are written with their current values").
- A disabled channel has no engine (CS 4.6): its worker writes its system tags once, at its start, as a channel never connected: `state` 0, every device DISABLED (0), `secured` false, every count, time and average 0, and `requests_per_scan` that of the device's plan.
- States are checked after every step and written when they change: the channel's and every device's `state`, `secured` and `pacing_limited`. Everything else is written at most once a second, and only when it changed. A worker's wait ends at the next such second, so an idle channel's counters move too. At the stop, once the connection is closed and nothing more is counted, the counters that changed since their last write are written, whatever the period, so a reader after the stop sees every request the channel sent (review M1). The states follow (review M3): the channel's `state` 0; each device's `state` as 3.5 gives it for a channel not connected, CONNECTING, or DISABLED for a disabled device; and `pacing_limited` false. Then `config_state` is 0 (above). The stop does not write states through the engine, which still says connected.
- What remains of the window of D-142 (review M3). After a clean stop the tags say "not connected" and "loading" until the next life's walk of 9.1. After a kill or a stall nothing is written: the tags keep the previous life's values with OWNER_DOWN. Once the next life attaches, OWNER_DOWN is gone and those values read GOOD until its walk by owner slot, the first thing each thread does after the attach. So, from the attach to the walk, a reader can see the previous life's "connected", "running" and "applied". D-142 keeps that time as short as the threads' start allows.
- The channel's `state`: 1 (connecting) is written and the readers woken just before a connect begins, since the connect blocks the worker up to `connect_timeout`. It is 2 once connected, and 0 otherwise: after a refused or failed connect, during the back-off, after a loss or a close of the driver's own, and at the stop once the connection is closed.
- Device counts, kept by the engine for every device:
  - `requests` counts every frame sent to it, retries and probes included.
  - `timeouts` counts the attempts that got no byte of an answer by the first deadline (7.1); a partial send on a serial line counts as one (7.2).
  - `errors` counts the answers that are errors: `exceptions` plus `refusals` plus broken answers.
  - `exceptions` counts the answers SPLIT, ABSENT, DEVICE_INVALID and REFUSED: error answers with a protocol code.
  - `refusals` counts BUSY and UNREACHABLE answers (busy and gateway).
  - `last_status` is the `raw` code of the last answer that `exceptions` or `refusals` counted. The contract fills `raw` only for a REFUSED write, so for reads it stays 0 until the protocols report a code there too (a point for #136).
  - `last_ok_time` is the CLOCK_REALTIME of the last SAMPLES or WRITE_OK answer, 0 before the first.
  - `response_ms` is the average, over the device's last 100 complete answers, of the time from the send to the first byte of the answer. Bytes left from before the send count as 0 ms, and bytes of frames dropped as stale do not start the time. It is rounded to 0.1 ms before it is compared with the value last written (`rounded_ms`, review S1): finer than the ms of 11.1, so that a local answer of a fraction of a ms does not read 0, and coarse enough that noise below a tenth writes nothing. The rounding is `round(x * 10) / 10`, which rounds once and gives the double nearest the tenth: 0.7, where `round(x / 0.1) * 0.1` gives 0.7000000000000001.
  - `sentinels` and `invalid` come from the sample processing of contract 5 (`SampleCounts`).
  - `points_absent` is the number of its points that are ABSENT now.
  - `requests_per_scan` is that of the plan (6.3).
- `requests_per_s` is the number of frames the channel sent in the 10 complete seconds of the monotonic clock before the running one, divided by 10 (review S1). The running second is not counted until it is complete, so a steady rate reads the same at every point of a second and is not rewritten each second. Its resolution is 0.1 per second, and it needs no rounding. Over the first 10 s after the start it ramps up from 0.
- `period.<group>` is the scheduler's achieved period in ms (6.3), rounded as `response_ms` is; `pacing_limited` is the scheduler's verdict (6.4).
- Notes from the review:
  - `period.<group>` reads 0 when every device of the channel is demoted, since no group cycle ends.
  - On a channel that never connects, `state` moves between 1 and 0 at each attempt: two writes per attempt, at most one attempt per `reconnect_min`.
  - After a stop overrun or a stall, nothing writes the stop states: `config_state` stays 1 (applied), read with OWNER_DOWN.
  - A start that fails on a refused write logs the refusal twice: once as the start failure, once in the refused-write record of the stop.
  - The 0.1 ms step does little where the answer time jitters by milliseconds. The SCADA reviewer measured 0.24, 0.59 and 0.87 writes of `response_ms` per device-second at 1, 3 and 10 ms of jitter; the figures are on #136 with the write-volume point.
  - `last_status` is 0 for reads (above).
